The global context has shi ed drama cally since 2019, with increased geopoli cal instability, soaring food
prices, and the COVID-19 pandemic exacerba ng exis ng vulnerabili es and crea ng new challenges. Yet
food systems remain squarely centred at the nexus of food security, human health, environmental
sustainability, social jus ce and the resilience of na ons. Ac ons on food strongly impact the lives and
well-being of all and are necessary to progress toward cri cal goals as ar culated in the Sustainable
Development Goals, the Paris Climate Agreement, and the Kunming-Montreal Global Biodiversity
Framework, amongst others. While current food systems have largely kept pace with popula on growth,
ensuring suﬃcient caloric intake for many, they are the single most inﬂuen al driver of planetary
boundary transgression. More than half the world's popula on struggles to access healthy diets, leading
to devasta ng consequences for public health, social equity, and the environment. Although hunger has
declined in certain regions, recent increases linked to expanding conﬂicts and emergent climate change

impacts have reversed this posi ve trend. Obesity rates con nue to rise globally, and the pressure exerted
by food systems on planetary boundaries shows no signs of aba ng. In this moment of increasing
instability, food systems s ll oﬀer an unprecedented opportunity build the resilience of environmental,
health, economic and social systems and are uniquely placed enhance human well-being while
contribu ng massively to Earth system stability.
This updated analysis builds upon the 2019 EAT-Lancet Commission, expanding its scope and
strengthening its evidence base. The ﬁrst Commission deﬁned food group ranges for a healthy diet and
iden ﬁed the food system shares of planetary boundaries. In this Commission, we add an analysis of the
social founda ons for a just food system. It incorporates new data and perspec ves on distribu ve,
representa onal, and recogni onal jus ce, providing a global overview on equity in food systems.
Substan al improvements in modelling capacity and data analysis allow for the use of a mul -model
ensemble to project poten al outcomes of a transi on to healthy and sustainable food systems.
The Planetary Health Diet (PHD) remains a cornerstone of our recommenda ons and can be seen as a
framework within which diverse and culturally appropriate diets can exist. Robust updated evidence
reinforces a strong associa on with improved health outcomes, large reduc ons in all-cause mortality,
and a substan al decrease in the incidence of major diet-related chronic diseases. The reference PHD
emphasises a balanced dietary pa ern, predominantly plant-based, with moderate inclusion of animalsource foods and limited consump on of added sugars, saturated fats, and salt. The successful
implementa on of the PHD requires careful considera on of cultural contexts and the promo on of
culturally appropriate and sustainable dietary tradi ons. This diversity of contexts, bounded by the PHD’s
reference values, represents signiﬁcant ﬂexibility and choice across cultures, geographies and individual
preferences. However, confronted with today’s mul ple crises, transforma on will require urgent and
meaningful changes in our individual and collec ve behaviours and culture of unhealthy, unjust, and
unsustainable food produc on and consump on.
For the ﬁrst me we quan fy the global food system share of all nine planetary boundaries. These food
system boundaries (FSB) conﬁrm that food is the single largest cause for transgression of the planetary
boundaries driving the transgression of ﬁve of the six breached boundaries. In addi on, food systems
exert a signiﬁcant impact on the transgressed climate boundary and on the Ocean acidiﬁca on boundary.
Food becomes the most signiﬁcant driver of climate when its transporta on, and energy use contribu ons
are included. Unsustainable land conversion, par cularly deforesta on, remains a major driver of
biodiversity loss and climate change calling for zero conversion of remaining intact ecosystems. Food
systems account for the near totality of nitrogen and phosphorus boundary transgression, highligh ng a
cri cal need for improvements in nutrient management, eﬃcient nutrient redistribu on, and the
development of circular nutrient systems. The massive use of novel en es in food produc on,
processing, and packaging (ranging from plas cs to pes cides) remains of signiﬁcant concern, yet
alarmingly understudied.
Our assessment of jus ce integrates three dimensions—distribu ve, representa onal, and
recogni onal—within a human rights framing that includes the rights to food, a healthy environment, and

decent work. Analyses reveal important inequi es in access to healthy diets, decent work condi ons, and
healthy environments, dispropor onately aﬀec ng marginalised groups in low-income regions. We
therefore propose nine social founda ons that enable these rights to be met and assess their status.
Enabling access to, aﬀordability of, and demand for healthy diets is paramount. Equally crucial is the right
to live and work within a healthy environment and a stable climate system, recognising the profound
impact of environmental degrada on on human health and well-being. Meanwhile, a living wage and
meaningful representa on would enable individuals to be recognized, heard, and par cipate in building
healthy, sustainable, and just food systems. However, nearly half of the world's popula on falls below
these founda ons, undermining their ability to meet basic human rights. At the same me, the dietary
pa erns of most (6.9 billion people) of the world exert pressures that threaten to further transgress
planetary boundaries. The destabilising eﬀect of unhealthy overconsump on on the Earth's systems
highlights the cri cal importance of viewing healthy diets not just as a human right, but also as a shared
responsibility.
A model intercomparison including ten integrated assessment models (IAMs) across mul ple scenarios
reveals the substan al poten al for reducing environmental and health impacts through dietary shi s,
improved agricultural produc vity, and reduc ons in food loss and waste. Crea ng demand for and
increasing adop on of diets that adhere to the PHD, coupled with ambi ous climate mi ga on policies,
will result in signiﬁcant reduc ons in greenhouse gas emissions and land use. However, the results are
sobering, showing that even with the ambi ous transforma ons (sustainable intensiﬁca on, reduced
food loss and waste, and transi on to ea ng within the PHD), the world is barely able to come back into
the safe space for freshwater use and climate change, while con nuing to transgress the biogeochemical
boundary for nitrogen and phosphorus loading - albeit with signiﬁcant reduced pressure.
Deep-dive analyses focusing on sustainable and ecological intensiﬁca on food produc on prac ces, along
with more circular nutrient systems, suggest that widespread adop on of these prac ces could reduce
further greenhouse gas emissions , increasing carbon sequestra on , reducing the land area dedicated to
food produc on, decreasing water footprints, and make substan al progress on addressing nitrogen and
phosphorus boundaries, even in the face of a growing global popula on and food consump on.
To advance towards the goals of healthy (the PHD), sustainable (within the food system boundaries), and
just (above the social founda ons) food systems by 2050, eight priority solu ons are proposed, each
accompanied by speciﬁc ac ons and policy measures: (1) create food environment to increase demand
for healthy diets ensuring they are more accessible and aﬀordable; (2) protect and promote healthy
tradi onal diets; (3) implement sustainable and ecological intensiﬁca on prac ces; (4) apply strong
regula ons to prevent loss of remaining intact ecosystems; (5) improve infrastructure, management, and
consumer behaviour change to reduce food loss and waste; (6) secure decent working condi ons; (7)
ensure meaningful voice and representa on; and (8) recognise and protect marginalised groups. These
proposed solu ons and ac ons should be organized into coherent ‘bundles’ of interven ons to enhance
poli cal feasibility and policy eﬀec veness. The most suitable and eﬀec ve bundles will vary by context
and tailored to the speciﬁc challenges and opportuni es of each region and sector.

This Commission reinforces the urgent need for a “great food transforma on”. The targets of the EATLancet Commission for healthy people (PHD) on a healthy planet (FSB) with just food systems (social
founda ons) can only be met through concerted global ac on and unprecedented levels of transforma ve
change. The Commission calls for cross-sectoral coali ons, that develop context-speciﬁc roadmaps,
aligning with exis ng and emerging global frameworks, such as the Paris Agreement, the Conven on on
Biological Diversity, and the post-2030 SDG agenda. These roadmaps include the se ng of science-based
targets with monitoring and accountability mechanisms in place. Mechanisms must be established to
insulate policymaking from undue corporate inﬂuence, while civil society and social movements play a
vital role in promo ng transparency and oversight.

Signiﬁcant ﬁnancial resources, es mated between US$200-500 billion per year, are needed to support the
transforma on to healthy, sustainable, and just food systems. However, there is good evidence that the
price of ac on is much lower than the cost of inac on, and that the investments rapidly shi to economic
beneﬁts, es mated at US$5 trillion per year. There is an opportunity to repurpose exis ng ﬁnance by
realigning incen ves with goals, such as shi ing exis ng support to producer communi es adop ng
sustainable ecological intensiﬁca on prac ces or transi oning to the produc on of currently underconsumed food groups, while elimina ng support to pollu ng prac ces or to foods whose overproduc on
drives poor health.
The Commission posi ons jus ce as both a goal and a driving force for food systems transforma on. Food
systems cannot be just without ensuring healthy diets that meet the PHD are aﬀordable and accessible,
and without signiﬁcantly reducing planetary boundaries transgressions. At the same me, jus ce
is necessary to overcome the deeply entrenched structural barriers that currently impede transforma ve
change. In this sense, jus ce is not only an outcome of food systems transforma on, but a prerequisite

The food system has an outsized impact on human wellbeing and planetary health. What we eat, and
where and how this food is produced and distributed strongly inﬂuences the length and quality of people's
lives, as well as our capacity to stay within planetary boundaries. How food systems are governed and
managed determine the extent to which people can par cipate and beneﬁt. Since the 2019 publica on of
Food in the Anthropocene: the EAT-Lancet Commission on healthy diets from sustainable food systems, 1
evidence from a growing number of sources show an alarming rise in stress to all natural systems on Earth,
escala ng diet-related ill-health across the world, worrying inequali es in food consump on pa erns
within and between countries, and disrupted food supplies. Given this context and the expected increase
in the global popula on to approximately 9.6 billion people in 2050, the need and urgency for a food
system transforma on to remain within 1.5 degrees C of global warming (Figure 1) and to mee ng a broad
range of global goals related to human health, environmental sustainability and social jus ce is clear.

Unsustainable food systems threaten the func oning of the biosphere and the stability of the en re
climate system, and underpin unhealthy diets, which accounts for approximately 15 million avoidable
deaths each year (see Panel 2). Access to food is inequitable, with an insuﬃcient access to healthy diets.3–
5
The food system produces enough calories to feed the world’s popula on, yet average diets trend to
low composi onal quality with global underconsump on of whole grains, fruits, nuts, and vegetables.3,6
Unhealthy overconsump on, notably of red meat, is a leading driver of climate and land-use impact. 1 Our
food supply is inherently sensi ve to water scarcity and clima c variability, and is increasingly sensi ve to
rapidly rising human climate change induced extreme events, like droughts, ﬂoods, heatwaves, or pest
and disease outbreaks. Prevailing food produc on prac ces are driving biodiversity loss, water scarcity,
overuse of pes cides and overloading of fer lisers, climate change, and excessive use of an microbials.
Food prices do not reﬂect the true cost of unsustainable produc on, while rising global poverty makes
healthy diets too expensive for billions of people.7,8 This is a major challenge, as it shows that the planet
is subsidizing cheap food as no environmental externali es (e.g., biodiversity loss, nutrient overloading,
freshwater use, and GHG emissions) are paid for, while almost one billion people suﬀer from
undernutri on. The economic costs of these challenges are staggering, while global food system
generates US$10 trillion in value each year, the nega ve externali es are es mated at US$15 trillion, with
the health sector contribu ng the most.9,10

These trends drive cri cal and unacceptable injus ces. The current socio-economic situa on for billions
of people in the world undermines fundamental human rights. Inadequate and inequitable access to safe,
suﬃcient, and nutri ous food for nearly 50% of the global popula on10 violates the right to food,
contribu ng to malnutri on, ill-health, and maternal and child mortality.6 The food system drives
transgression of mul ple planetary boundaries viola ng the right to a healthy environment and a stable
planet, resul ng in illness, and the loss of work, food, and life. Poor labour condi ons for many food
system workers, o en underpaid, and lacking social beneﬁts, violate the right to decent work, resul ng in
exploita on and low purchasing power.11–13 In addi on, the lack of representa on and freedom of
expression of many food system workers, alongside market concentra on, with a few large food system
ﬁrms inﬂuencing decision-making, policies, and prices, undermines the achievement of all of these
rights.14–17

The global food agenda has made signiﬁcant progress over the past ﬁve years. New evidence provides
stronger support for the importance of food for human health and planetary stability, and there is a rising
recogni on that food jus ce forms an integral part of successful food transforma ons. Scien ﬁc a en on
to food systems has surged with new evidence emerging daily, including more than 10,500 cita ons of
the 2019 EAT-Lancet Commission alone. This evidence spans the development of planetary health diet
indices,18,19 na onal downscaling of dietary impacts on human health and environmental condi ons,20,21
economic analyses of food systems transforma on assessments on novel technologies,22–24 and behaviour
change. Most of this evidence supports the 2019 ﬁndings, adding new insights and stronger support for
food policy, ac on on public health, and raising public awareness, but it also signals need for addi onal
a en on to socioeconomic dimensions feasibility, and environmental impacts across all planetary
boundaries.25

Cri cal scien ﬁc assessment

Complemen ng the growing a en on by the research community, a diversity of interna onal, na onal,
and local actors is driving a variety of commitments and ini a ves. A key moment came in 2021 with the
United Na ons Food Systems Summit (UNFSS). Since then, two thirds of countries have adopted na onal
food systems transforma on pathways and have commitments to integrate foods into their Na onally
Determined Contribu ons (NDCs) to the Paris Climate Agreement. More than 290 ci es, with a combined
popula on of 490 million, have signed onto the Milan Urban Food Policy Pact enac ng more than 620
speciﬁc food system ac ons. Both the IPCC and IPBES have assessed food systems, 26,27 no ng food’s
dependency, and impacts on biodiversity, climate, water, and health. The Kunming-Montreal Global
Biodiversity Framework signed in 2023 set speciﬁc food system targets. In these eﬀorts, and other, food
is recognised as uniquely central in the nexus of human health, social jus ce, and environmental
sustainability.28 We are alarmed by recent eﬀorts to undermine, hide, or obfuscate climate science,
environmental protec ons, and a en on to jus ce. Ignoring these trends aggravates, rather than resolves
the challenges they represent and their growing impact on human society.

The growing a en on in science and prac ce is encouraging. Yet with few excep ons, emerging plans
have targets that are poorly ar culated, non-speciﬁc with no accountability, insuﬃciently ambi ous,29
include too few and siloed interven ons,30–32 and lack appropriate ﬁnancial support. The slow rate of
ac on is unjust and dangerous to both current popula ons and future genera ons.33,34 Considering this
surge in evidence, and interest in food system ac on, an updated assessment is cri cally needed. Our
primary objec ve of this Commission is to provide that update.
First, we review new evidence published since 2019 on diet and health to provide the latest scien ﬁc
update on the PHD paying speciﬁc a en on to research on dietary pa erns and incorpora ng evidence
from a broader range of popula ons and world regions. We highlight long-established or tradi onal
dietary pa erns that are in line with the PHD and emphasise the poten al of achieving culturally diverse
healthy diets. However, we also recognise that there is similarly ample evidence that ‘modern’ diets,
appropriately conﬁgured, can provide similar health beneﬁts. The diversity of fruits, nuts, vegetables, and
whole grains available in many modern socie es, and the interest in ethnic cuisines from around the world
is unprecedented, as is the number of choices available to those desiring healthy op ons where they are
available. In addi on, we provide updated health impacts and nutri onal assessments of adop ng the
PHD and related dietary pa erns by country and socio-demographic group.
Second, we review the evidence on the impact of food produc on systems on the Earth system and assess
the current state of food systems across all nine planetary boundaries, upda ng the ﬁve quan ﬁed in the
2019 EAT-Lancet Commission. For the ﬁrst me, we propose speciﬁc food system boundaries, as a food
system share of the safe opera ng space provided by the planetary boundaries, for use in target se ng
for food. We provide a novel assessment of how sustainable and ecological intensiﬁca on food produc on
prac ces can support a transforma on of food produc on systems within safe food system boundaries.
Third, we establish social founda ons of a just food system and assess where current food systems are
placed in rela on to the founda ons. By adding a focus on jus ce, we place people at the centre of food
systems recognising that a just food system is one where human rights can be met. We assess the global
distribu on of injus ces focusing both on where unhealthy food consump on exerts undue pressure on
planetary boundaries, and regions where social jus ce in rela on to the food system are not met.
Fourth we include a mul -model analysis to quan fy the consequences of a food systems transforma on
to healthy diets, sustainably produced by 2050. We include ten well-established agro-economic and
environmental models with socio-economic rebound eﬀects to expand our understanding of the poten al
outcomes of the proposed food systems transforma on, including direct eﬀects on food produc on
quan es and environmental impacts, and a greater considera on of poten al socio-economic impacts.
Finally, based on the reviewed evidence, we propose solu ons and ac ons to ensure access to, and
demand for, healthy, sustainable, and just food systems for all. While these solu ons are impac ul in
making healthy and sustainable diets available, accessible, and aﬀordable, we emphasize the opportunity
and need to ensure that foods oﬀered must also be delicious and aspira onal to ensure that demand

As with the 2019 Commission, we recognize that few decisions on dietary health, environmental
sustainability, and social jus ce can be made based on absolute certainty. In many cases, exis ng evidence
is incomplete, imperfect, and evolving. This Commission takes stock of how knowledge has evolved over
the past ﬁve years as one means of reducing this uncertainty. We have based our es mates on the best
science acknowledging uncertain es where they exist. Conﬁdence levels are provided and improved
through wider systemic reviews and broader community eﬀorts to test the validity of ﬁndings. For
example, a new contribu on of this Commission’s work is a mul -model intercomparison, involving 10
integrated assessment modelling teams, tes ng the contribu on of adop ng the PHD and transi on to
sustainable food produc on prac ces, in feeding humanity within planetary boundaries (featured in
sec on 4). This work highlights the degree of certainty, and variance, in outcome results. We have adopted
treatment of uncertainty in the same manner addressed by IPCC and IPBES. All of the models in the model
intercomparison exercise have calibrated their results ensuring that historical trajectories can be
reproduced. Considering the 2019 Commission, and the new research summarized here, we have a high
level of scien ﬁc certainty about the overall direc on and magnitude of change required, although
considerable uncertainty can exist around some of the more detailed quan ﬁca ons. Our modelling work
considers major trends and opportuni es; however, it is important to recognize the myriads of pathways
that food systems actors might choose to navigate this transi on, and which we encourage them to
explore.

While the challenges are daun ng, we ﬁnd that achieving access to healthy diets for all, produced,
processed, distributed, and consumed fairly within planetary boundaries largely remains biophysically
possible. Important synergies between economic, social, and environmental outcomes exist, but are
currently missed in today’s socio-economic and socio-poli cal systems. These synergies should be be er
leveraged. Healthy diets are not only a fundamental right, but a shared responsibility. Major eﬀorts are
needed to manage an increasingly urgent food systems transforma on that secures human and planetary
health, addressing the cri cal lock-ins and poli cal economies inhibi ng progress on this necessary
transi on. The urgency to accelerate the “great food transforma on” is undeniable. This Commission
provides clear quan ta ve guidance on health, sustainability, and jus ce with the aim that this guidance
will assist food system actors to navigate this progress with greater conﬁdence and impact. The “great
food transforma on” needed to achieve healthy, sustainable, and just food systems is huge; it will require
the eﬀorts of individuals, organisa ons and governments at levels from local to global and across
essen ally all sectors in society.

This Commission provides scien ﬁc measures for healthy, sustainable, and just food systems based on
our current understanding. The model results are our current best es mate of plausible change (or lack
thereof), and their implica ons for human health and planetary stability. However, the actual feasibility
and impact will vary based on decisions and pathways adopted by individual socie es. We do not assess

how we got to our current state but evaluate a broad range of solu ons with demonstrated impacts in

solu ons to their contexts. Here it is worth reminding that the PHD and the FSBs merely provide healthy
and sustainable boundary condi ons, within which a myriad of food dietary composi ons and prac ces
ﬁt. It is beyond the scope of this Commission to prescribe which ac ons should be adopted locally, and
we recognize the need for much greater interac ons between science, policy, and prac oners in ﬁnding
context speciﬁc solu ons.

Key Messages
1. Food systems sit at the nexus of health, environment, climate, and jus ce. A food systems
transforma on is fundamental for solving the climate, biodiversity, crises, and the food related
health and jus ce health crises. The nexus posi on of food systems emphasizes the
interac ons between goals, rather than each goal separately. This calls for adop ng food
system ac ons as a global integrator across economic, governance and policy domains.
2. The updated Planetary Health Diet (PHD) has an appropriate energy intake, a diversity of whole
or minimally processed foods that are mostly plant-sourced, fats that are primarily
unsaturated with no par ally hydrogenated oils, and limited amounts of added sugars and salt.
It allows ﬂexibility and is compa ble with a wide variety of foods, cultures, dietary pa erns,
tradi ons, and individual preferences. The PHD provides both nutri onal adequacy and
diminishes the risks of non-communicable diseases. At present, all na onal diets deviate
substan ally from the PHD, but a shi to this pa ern could avert approximately 15 million
deaths per year (27% of total). Such transi on would reduce the rates of many speciﬁc noncommunicable diseases and promote healthy longevity.
3. Food drives ﬁve out of six planetary boundary transgressions, including land system change,
biosphere integrity, blue water change, biogeochemical ﬂows, and novel en es, while
accoun ng for approximately 30% of greenhouse gas emissions driving climate change. How
and where food is produced, which foods are produced and consumed, and how much is lost
and wasted, all contribute to planetary boundary transgressions. There is no safe solu on to
the climate and biodiversity crises without a global food system transforma on. Even if the
world succeeds with the global energy transi on away from fossil-fuels, the food system alone
will make the world breach the Paris Climate agreement range of limi ng global mean surface
temperature to 1.5°C.
4. Human rights related to food systems (the rights to food, a healthy environment, and decent
work) are not being met, with nearly half the world’s popula on below social founda ons.
Meanwhile, across countries and people, responsibility for the planetary boundary
transgressions from food systems is not equal: the diets of the richest 30% of the global
popula on contribute over 70% of the environmental pressures from food systems. This
highlights the large inequali es in the distribu on of both beneﬁts and burdens of current food
systems. Na onal policies that address inequi es in the distribu on of both beneﬁts and
burdens of current food systems would go some way to mee ng human rights as they relate
to food systems.
5. The Planetary Health Diet needs to be available, aﬀordable, convenient, aspira onal, and
delicious. To increase demand for healthy sustainable diets and enable necessary dietary

10

shi s, food environment interven ons, increased purchasing power, and protec on and
promo on of healthy tradi onal diets are important ac ons.
6. An EAT-Lancet aligned food system transforma on that includes a shi to healthy diets,
improved agricultural produc vity, and reduced food loss and waste would substan ally
reduce environmental pressures on emissions and land-, water-, nitrogen-, and phosphoroususe. However, no single ac on is suﬃcient to ensure a healthy, just, and sustainable food
system. Comparing 2050 values to current state, a shi to healthy diets in isola on could lead
to a 15% reduc on in agricultural emissions, compared to 20% when all three ac ons are
implemented. Individually all three ac ons modestly reduce future nitrogen- and
phosphorous-use (27-34% vs 41% increase compared to 2020 under business as usual),
however in combina on they substan ally reduce future growth in nitrogen- and
phosphorous-use (15% increase compared to 2020)
7. Addi onal nature-posi ve beneﬁts are accrued through sustainable and ecological
intensiﬁca on prac ces. Unprecidented investments and eﬀort in these prac ces poten ally
making the food system net carbon nega ve. A diversity of context-speciﬁc prac ces can
sequester addi onal carbon biomass, create and connect habitat, reduce nutrient applica ons
and increase intercep on and capture of excessive crop fer liser before it pollutes ground and
surface water systems. These prac ces can be enabled by securing equitable access to land
and water resources, strengthening public advisory services, addressing structural imbalances
between producers and dominant agribusinesses, and through public and private sector
investments that support farmers shi ing towards sustainable prac ces.
8. An EAT-Lancet aligned food system transforma on could lead to a less resource- and labourintensive food system capable of supplying a healthy diet for 9.6 billion people with modest
impacts on food costs. This would however have profound implica ons regarding what, how,
and where food is produced and those involved in these processes. For example, as a part of
this restructuring some sectors would need to contract (e.g., ruminant meat: -33%) and others
would need to expand (e.g. fruits, vegetables, and nuts: +63%) compared to 2020 produc on
levels.
9. Jus ce is needed to unlock and accelerate ac on for transforma on. A fair distribu on of
opportuni es and resources, such that the rights to food, a healthy environment, and decent
work are met, and a fair distribu on of the responsibility to produce, distribute, and consume
healthy diets within planetary boundaries are the basis of a successful food systems
transforma on. Power asymmetries, discriminatory social and poli cal structures prevent
these rights from being met, which results in harms to health of people, precarious livelihoods
of food systems workers, and lack of voice undermining freedom, agency, and dignity. Ensuring
liveable wages, and collec ve bargaining while regula ng and limi ng market concentra on
and improving transparency, accountability, representa on, and access to informa on are all

impac ul ac ons. We emphasize the protec on of basic human rights in conﬂict areas a as
fundamental founda on of jus ce.

Glossary
Planetary Health Diet
Based on latest evidence on diet and health, the Planetary Health Diet represents a dietary pa ern
that supports op mal health outcomes, and which can be applied globally for diﬀerent popula ons
and diﬀerent contexts while suppor ng cultural and regional varia on. It emphasizes a plant-rich diet
where whole grains, fruits, vegetables, nuts, and legumes comprise a greater propor on of foods
consumed, with moderate or small amounts of ﬁsh, dairy, and meat. The Planetary Health Diet is
en rely based on direct eﬀects of diets on human health, not on environmental criteria. We refer to
it as the Planetary Health Diet since there is good evidence that its adop on would lower the
environmental impacts and nutri onal deﬁciencies of most current diets.
Food System Boundaries
Food system boundaries are science-based targets represen ng the share of the safe opera ng space
for food systems. They are based on the available evidence represen ng the required or possible
mi ga on of food systems to return or remain within planetary boundaries, including the present-day
contribu on of food systems to planetary boundary transgressions in rela on to other sectors,
es mates of minimum environmental impacts from food systems that are hard-to-abate (i.e. through
op miza on modelling across sectors), and es mates of limited Earth system impact while remaining
produc ve agricultural systems.
Sustainable and Ecological Intensiﬁca on (SEI)
Sustainable Intensiﬁca on (SI) entails achieving important reduc ons in the environmental impacts
though increased eﬃciency, reduced losses, and reduced pollu on. Ecological Intensiﬁca on (EI) is a
subset of SI that enhances the environmental performance by promo ng ecological processes within
agricultural ﬁelds, farms and landscapes, such as above and below ground carbon sequestra on,
nutrient cycling and storage, pollina on, and biological pest regula on.
Social Founda ons
Social founda ons require basic human rights, such as food, water, health, and energy, to be met for
everyone. We build on previous work deﬁning the minimum resources required to meet people’s
human rights, to avoid resource depriva ons and harms, and focus on the minimum condi ons that
enable people’s human rights to be met.
Great Food Transforma on
An unprecedented commitment globally and na onally to an interlinked range of ac ons to be taken
by all sectors to make healthy food accessible to all—produced, processed, distributed, and consumed
fairly within planetary boundaries

Section 1: What is a Healthy Diet?

328
329
330
331
332
333
334
335

We review the evidence on diet and health and present reference values for food group intakes in the
reference Planetary Health Diet (PHD) (Table 1). Since the 2019 Commission,1 we included additional
health outcomes, considered effects of food processing, and examined implications for young children
and women of reproductive age. The relation between the PHD with mortality and other health outcomes
has now been examined in numerous epidemiological studies that we summarise. This section also
describes gaps between current dietary intakes and the PHD in different regions, and how different
dietary cultures may align with the PHD.

336

Approach to Defining Healthy Diets

337
338
339
340
341
342
343
344
345
346
347
348
349
350
351
352
353
354
355
356
357

359

Healthy diets should be adequate, diverse, balanced, and moderate.35 Defining healthy diets for a global
population is complicated because nutritional requirements vary by, for example, age, sex, body size,
physical activity level, pregnancy and lactation, health status, and genetic factors. However, the effects of
diet are similar across ancestry or ethnic groups due to common underlying human biology. 36,37 Here, we
focus on diets of generally healthy populations two years and older, although the PHD is also key to
resolving health risks in populations facing a high prevalence of undernutrition.

360

Review of Evidence on Diet and Health

361
362
363
364
365
366
367

The goal of this Commission in defining the PHD is to provide the evidence and a quantitative description
of a healthy dietary pattern that can be applied globally for different populations and different contexts
while supporting cultural and regional variation. In summarising evidence and evaluating causality, we
considered prospective epidemiologic studies, randomised trials with intermediate risk factors as
endpoints, and the few available randomised trials with health endpoints (Panel 1). Because changes in
dietary components by individuals or populations are usually made within a constrained total energy
intake, isocaloric substitution analyses comparing specific dietary components, such as red meat versus

We have described healthy diets as combinations of foods as this most directly connects health with food
production and consumption. Nutrient adequacy is important and included in our analyses, but our
primary considerations are specific health outcomes related to insufficient or excessive food intakes,
rather than numerical targets for essential nutrients (e.g., average requirements (ARs) or estimated
average requirements (EARs)). Nutrient requirements are largely based on small, short-term studies, and
are often not supported by sufficient evidence for long-term overall health. In addition to food groups,
we include added fats, sugar, and salt as they are substantial components of most diets. Our conclusions
assume that most foods are consumed whole, unprocessed, or minimally processed.
Although the PHD is based on direct effect of diets on health, we also discuss potential indirect effects of
diets on health, including antimicrobial resistance, pandemic risk, and those mediated by environmental
changes (Panel 3). Control of microbial and other contaminants of food and water is critical but not
comprehensively reviewed here. While the cost and affordability of diets, and the environmental impacts
of their production and consumption, are critical issues and influence people’s access to healthy diets,
these factors do not define what is healthy in a diet. We address those separately in other sections.

14

368
369
370
371

legumes, nuts, or fish, are particularly informative because these represent realistic dietary choices. A key
part of this Commission is the evaluation of the PHD as an overall dietary pattern in relation to mortality
and other health outcomes.
Panel 1: The Planetary Health Diet and Health: Evidence for Causality
Ideally, evidence for effects of specific foods, nutrients, and overall diets on major health outcomes
would be based on large, randomised trials with disease and functional endpoints in representative
populations across the world. However, this type of evidence is limited because such trials require
many thousands of participants, many years or even decades of follow-up, and sustained high
adherence to assigned diets. This is neither feasible nor ethical, and randomised trials not meeting
these criteria can easily yield misleading, null or negative results. Thus, for studies of diet, and other
behavioural and environmental exposures, frameworks to assess causality, such as the Bradford-Hill
criteria,P1 have been developed. For dietary factors, these typically include reproducible evidence from
prospective epidemiologic studies (e.g., following a cohort over time) in combination with randomised
trials that include intermediate risk factors as outcomes.P2 Rigorous control of confounding factors by
study design and statistical methods is essential.
An example illustrates the value of combined evidence from observational and experimental studies
in informing the links between the PHD and health. A central feature of the PHD is the importance of
nuts and legumes, including beans, pulses, and soy as major protein sources, with limited amounts of
red meat and dairy foods. The far higher ratio of polyunsaturated to saturated fatty acids in these plant
foods compared with red meat and dairy products predicts lower LDL-cholesterol levels; this was
confirmed in a meta-analysis of randomised trials of red meat intake. P3 In long-term cohort studies
with repeated assessments of diet, mortality was lowest when plant protein was compared to red
meat, dairy foods, and poultry;P4 associations with type 2 diabetes and coronary heart disease have
been similar.P5 Other key features of the PHD include relatively high amounts of fruits and vegetables,
shown to lower blood pressure in the Dietary Approaches to Stop Hypertension (DASH) randomised
trial;P6 whole grains, shown to improve blood lipids in randomised trials;P7 and largely unsaturated
plant oils, shown to reduce LDL-cholesterol.P8 Components of the PHD may operate by additional
pathways, including modulation of the microbiome, but at a minimum, beneficial effects on firmly
established risk factors are clear. As described in the main text, in multiple large cohort studies with
careful adjustment for smoking and other potentially confounding variables, participants with diets
most consistent with the PHD have had lower risks of many major health outcomes, including type 2
diabetes, cardiovascular disease, and total mortality.
Important causal evidence for benefits of the PHD also comes from decades of research on the
traditional Mediterranean dietary pattern, a specific example of the PHD. P9 In addition to the types of
evidence from observational studies and trials of intermediate endpoints described above, P10 in a
large, randomised trial in people with raised cardiovascular disease risk, a Mediterranean diet with
added nuts or olive oil reduced the risk of cardiovascular disease,P11 type 2 diabetes,P12 cognitive
decline,P13,P14 and other adverse health outcomes.P15 In another randomised trial, adherence to a
Mediterranean diet reversed the prevalence of a pre-diabetic state by half over one year, P16 and in a
two-year randomised trial, this dietary pattern reduced body weight compared to low fat or high fat
diets;P17 weight loss was sustained at six years.P18
15

Together, the combination of evidence on the composition of food groups, randomised trials with risk
factors as outcomes, and large cohort studies provides strong evidence that adherence to the PHD will
have major health benefits. This conclusion is further reinforced by randomised trials of the
Mediterranean diet, a dietary pattern that is one example of the PHD, documenting benefits for major
health outcomes.

Based on this body of evidence, PHD reference values (Table 1) were developed to provide a foundation
for optimal health outcomes, and to use as a starting point to evaluate global environmental impacts of
food consumption that are addressed in subsequent sections. Importantly, for individuals, these values
should not be considered as exact targets, but rather represent approximate ranges and proportions, with
potential substitutions among foods to accommodate different total energy intakes and preferences. An
updated review of available evidence is included in Appendix 1A; a summary is provided here.
Dietary energy requirements vary greatly between individuals depending on age, sex, body size, physical
activity, and metabolic factors. We estimated an average global energy intake of 2400 kcal/day for adults
using a new doubly-labelled water-based methodology, assuming an ideal BMI of 22 kg/m 2 and an active
level of physical activity (Appendix 1C).38 Assuming current average global physical activity levels and BMI
among adults of approximately 25 kg/m2 leads to a similar energy intake.
Grains, including a diversity of options such as wheat, rice, maize, oats, barley, rye, teff, amaranth, fonio,
buckwheat, millets, quinoa, and others are the primary sources of energy worldwide and an important
source of fibre, protein, and many micronutrients. Consumption of whole grains (100% of the bran, germ,
and endosperm in an intact grain), but not refined (depleted) grains, improves blood lipid values and other
biomarkers39 and is inversely associated with risks of weight gain,40,41 type 2 diabetes,42 coronary heart
disease,43,44 colorectal cancer,43 and total mortality.45 The inverse association between intake of whole
grains and weight appears linear, but a concern is that high intake of whole grains could displace other
healthy foods and reduce micronutrient absorption. Thus, we used 210 g/day dry weight as a reference
value for grains (midway within a ~20-50% of energy), considering this to be flexible and emphasising
whole grains versus refined grains.
Tubers, including potatoes, sweet potatoes, taro, yams, and cassava amongst others, are a major energy
source in some populations, but commonly consumed white potatoes do not provide the amounts of fibre
or micronutrients as do whole grains. Compared with whole grains or non-starchy vegetables,
consumption of potatoes is associated with greater weight gain46 and risk of type 2 diabetes,47,48 and the
association is stronger for fried potatoes than non-fried potatoes.47,48 Data on other tubers are limited.
Because these findings support consumption of whole grains over potatoes as a major energy source, we
used 50 g/day as the reference value.
Fruits and vegetables, including seaweeds and other aquatic plants, are a primary source of many
nutrients and phytochemicals. Intakes are inversely associated with risks of weight gain, 40,49 type 2

diabetes,50,51 coronary heart disease,51 some cancers,52,53 and total mortality.54 More recent evidence
supports a reduced risk of cognitive decline and dementia.55–57 Because of differences in the composition
of specific fruits or vegetables and their distinct associations with health outcomes, a variety is desirable,
specifically including green leafy and dark orange foods. Most health benefits accrue with about five
servings per day; we used 500 g/day as the reference value with no specified upper limit.
Nuts, legumes, and seeds provide protein, unsaturated fats, fibre, and many micronutrients. Nuts and
legumes have beneficial effects on blood lipids,58 and consumption of nuts, including peanuts, have been
associated with lower risks of type 2 diabetes,51 coronary heart disease,51,59 and total mortality.60,61 Soy
foods contain substantial amounts of both omega-3 and omega-6 fatty acids and are uniquely high in
phytoestrogens, and consumption has been associated with lower risks of cardiovascular disease 62,63 and
breast cancer.64 As nuts and legumes are healthy sources of protein, the reference value includes 125
g/day dry weight (about 3-5 servings per day of legumes, nuts, and seeds, including at least 1 serving of
nuts); a variety is encouraged.
Red (mammalian) meat is high in protein, haem iron, and other minerals, but is also high in saturated fat
and cholesterol and low in essential polyunsaturated fatty acids. In populations consuming very high
amounts of carbohydrates, small amounts of red meat or other animal-source foods can provide critical
amounts of essential nutrients that have positive health benefits. Consistent with the fatty acid
composition, and compared with plant sources of protein such as nuts, soy, and other legumes,
consumption of red meat increases blood levels of LDL-cholesterol39,65 and is associated with increased
risk of coronary heart disease.51,66 Also, consumption of red meat is linearly associated with elevated risk
of type 2 diabetes among different populations, including Asia, Europe, and North America; both
unprocessed and processed red meat contribute to risk but the latter association is stronger. 37,67 Red meat
intake has also been positively associated with unhealthy weight gain and risks of gestational diabetes,67
colorectal cancer (especially processed red meat),68 frailty,69 and unhealthy ageing.70,71 Consumption of
red meat is associated with higher risk of total mortality in countries that have been consuming high
amounts for many decades.72
Because the association of red meat with type 2 diabetes has been documented in many large
studies,37,51,66,67,73–75 and diabetes is a sentinel disease for many adverse cardiometabolic outcomes, this
dose-response relationship was used to determine the reference value in Table 1. According to data from
three large US cohorts with over 22,000 incident cases of diabetes and many repeated measures of diet
over three decades, the increase in diabetes incidence with increase in red meat consumption was
approximately linear with a minimal risk close to zero consumption of red meat. 76 We used 15 g/day
(about one serving per week) as the reference value with a range including zero to allow a modest intake
while avoiding statistically significant increases in risk (see Appendix 1A for additional details).
Poultry meat has nutritional values similar to red meat but an intermediate polyunsaturated fat
composition between red meat and plant protein sources. Associations with risks of non-communicable
diseases and mortality have also generally been intermediate;37,77,78 an inverse association with stroke has

been an exception.51 Because these relationships suggest a priority of poultry over red meat, we used 30
g/day of poultry (about two servings per week) as the reference value.
Eggs are a concentrated source of protein and essential nutrients and can play an important role in
childhood diets. They are also high in dietary cholesterol and have generally been intermediate with
respect to non-communicable diseases risk79,80 except for positive associations with risks of coronary heart
disease among people with diabetes.81,82 Consistent with other guidelines, we used 15 g/day or about two
eggs/week as the reference value.
Milk and derivatives have broad nutritional value but high content of saturated fat and little
polyunsaturated fat. High calcium content has been a primary justification for substantial lifetime
consumption. However, calcium requirements have been based on short-term studies, and milk intake
has not been clearly related to risk of bone fractures.83 Consistent with its fatty acid composition, dairy
fat substantially increases LDL-cholesterol compared to unsaturated plant oils.84 Epidemiologic evidence
is largely based on cow milk; as predicted, dairy fat is positively associated with cardiovascular disease. 85
Risks of cardiometabolic disease51 and overall mortality86,87 associated with milk intake are intermediate
between red meat and healthy plant sources of protein. Consumption of dairy foods is associated with
low risks of colorectal cancer88 but elevated risks of prostate cancer.89 Some evidence suggests that
yoghurt consumption, and possibly other fermented products, may be associated with less weight gain 90
and reduced risks of type 2 diabetes51 compared with other dairy foods. We used 250 g/day of milk or the
equivalent amount of milk derivatives (one serving per day) as the reference value because of its
intermediate relation with non-communicable diseases risk and substantial contribution to calcium
intake; the range includes zero because many populations globally do not consume milk and still have low
fracture risk.83 In a recent meta-analysis, a small decrease in risk of cardiovascular disease was seen with
about one serving/day of dairy foods, especially low fat milk and cheese, but li le further change in risk
with higher intakes. 91
Fish, shellfish, and other aquatic animal-source foods are major sources of protein and essential
nutrients for many populations, including in low and middle-income countries. In many diets, small fish
and bivalves are often overlooked as potential foods. Collectively, these foods are particularly important
as a source of omega-3 fatty acids (minimally present in terrestrial animal-source foods); low intakes of
omega-3 fatty acids can slow down neurologic development in childhood92 and possibly with cognitive
decline in adults;93 high intakes of omega-3 fatty acids are associated with reduced risk of cardiovascular
disease.94,95 The relation between intake of fish or omega-3 fatty acids and cardiovascular diseases
appears to be non-linear;94 most of the benefit is achieved with 30 g/day of fish (about two 100-g servings
per week), which is used as the reference value.
Added fats Evidence from prospective cohort studies and randomised trials has not suggested a reduction
in cardiovascular disease96 or cancer97 by decreasing total fat intake. However, added fats differ greatly in
their composition and health effects; partial hydrogenation creates deleterious trans isomers that are
associated with increased risks of cardiovascular diseases98 and all-cause mortality99 and this process is
now banned in many countries.100 Non-hydrogenated plant oils that contain mainly unsaturated fatty

acids reduce LDL-cholesterol,101 and intake is strongly related to lower risks of cardiometabolic diseases
and total mortality.102 These inverse associations appear linear within the ranges studied. Palm oil is
relatively high in saturated fat and low in polyunsaturated fatty acids; although studies of health outcomes
are limited, available evidence favours relatively unsaturated plant oils. 103 Compared with plant oils, dairy
fat increases LDL-cholesterol and is associated with increased risk of cardiovascular disease and total
mortality.84,85,91 Because non-hydrogenated plant oils have positive health effects, intake can be flexible
within limits for total energy, for example, if exchanged isocalorically with whole grains. We used an intake
of 17% of energy (primarily as unsaturated plant oils including both omega-6 and omega-3 fatty acids) as
a reference value, which is similar to that in a Mediterranean diet that reduces incidence of cardiovascular
disease.104

Overall Dietary Patterns and Health

528
529
530
531

Evaluations of overall dietary patterns in relation to risk of health outcomes represent the combined
effects of all dietary components, including their interactions (Appendix 1A). Dietary patterns have been
developed to describe traditional diets of specific regions or cultures (e.g., Mediterranean or
Japanese),112,113 using statistical approaches such as principal components or reduced rank regression

Added or free sugars provide no nutritional value and can cause harms in excessive intakes. Intakes,
particularly in the form of sugar-sweetened beverages, have adverse cardiometabolic effects and have
been positively associated with weight gain,105 type 2 diabetes,106 coronary heart disease,107 and total
mortality.106 Consistent with conclusions of the WHO108 and national guidelines, a maximum intake of 5%
of energy from added or free sugars is used as a reference value.
Sodium, commonly consumed as salt or mono-sodium glutamate, is essential, but intakes greater than
1500 mg/day increase blood pressure, and intakes greater than about 2000 mg/day are linearly associated
with an increased risk of cardiovascular disease.109 Thus, up to 2000 mg/day sodium (5 g/day as salt) was
used as the reference value.
Food processing Minimal processing, such as drying grains, pulses and nuts, chilling or freezing fruits and
vegetables, pasteurizing milk and fermenting milk into yoghurt, reduce food microbial contamination,
extend freshness, enhance sensory properties, facilitate preparation, add variety to diets, 110 and reduce
the time and labour costs of food preparation. Micronutrient fortification can improve micronutrient
density, notably for nutritionally vulnerable populations. However, processing can also have adverse
effects, including reduction in food nutritional content, addition of excessive salt and sugar, and use of
unhealthy fats and refined grains. Processes that destroy the structure of whole foods, chemically alter
their components, and assemble them with a variety of sensory-related additives into products that are
liable to displace unprocessed or minimally processed foods and freshly prepared dishes and meals are
considered ultra-processing.110 Dietary patterns high in ultra-processed foods have been associated with
thirty-two adverse health outcomes spanning mortality, cancer, and mental, respiratory, cardiovascular,
gastrointestinal, and metabolic health outcomes.111 The Commission concluded that most foods should
be consumed whole, unprocessed, or minimally processed.

analysis,114 or, as for the PHD, based on available evidence relating dietary components to health
outcomes.
In meta-analyses, dietary patterns similar to the PHD were associated with lower risk of all-cause
mortality.115,116 Within three large cohorts, the Mediterranean dietary score and five other commonly
used indices of diet quality were similarly inversely associated with overall mortality and a composite
outcome of major chronic diseases.117 Within-person improvements in dietary quality scores over time
were associated with lower risks of all-cause mortality.118 These and other dietary patterns have also been
associated with many other outcomes, including lower risks of depression and dementia. 119,120 Over recent
decades, supported by extensive analyses in many populations, a substantial convergence has developed
over the broad definition of a healthy dietary pattern.
Since the 2019 Commission, multiple investigators have created indices representing consistency with the
PHD, which is strongly correlated with other dietary quality indices.18,121 In prospective studies across the
world, significant inverse associations were reported for the PHD and a number of health outcomes
including total cardiovascular diseases, ischaemic heart disease, myocardial infarction, atrial fibrillation,
stroke, heart failure, type 2 diabetes, obesity, colorectal cancer, lung cancer, and other cancers. A few
studies reported null associations, but these findings need to be placed in the context of methodological
limitations (see Appendix 1A).122–124 In an analysis of 206,404 men and women followed for up to 33 years,
over 54,000 deaths were documented; overall mortality was 28% lower comparing the highest to the
lowest decile of adherence to the PHD (Appendix 1A).18 Although findings on the PHD and mortality in
two cohorts of Chinese adults125,126 are consistent with what has been seen in the United States and
Europe (Appendix 1A), data from low- and middle-income countries are limited by the lack of large cohort
studies in most regions. Associations could vary depending on dietary preferences within food groups,
and further research from those areas is needed.
Table 1 presents reference values of the PHD pattern for flexitarian adults. Scenarios for vegetarian or
pescatarian dietary patterns and different total energy intakes are presented in Appendix 1A. The PHD
pattern described in the current Commission is not substantially different from the 2019 Commission. 1
The dietary components in Table 1 have been re-ordered to facilitate interpretation and with slight
rounding of several numbers. Total population level adult energy intake is specified at 2400 kcal/day
(instead of 2500 kcal/day as in the 2019 Commission) based on updated values (Appendix 1B) and
recognising that energy requirements vary by individuals.38

food cultures, and with different total energy intakes are described in Appendix 1B.

Nutrient Adequacy of the PHD

591

The primary evaluation of nutrient adequacy of an overall diet is based on health outcomes, which
represent the cumulative consequences of all aspects of diet quality, including intakes of total calories,
essential nutrients, other dietary components, effects of processing, and their interactions. We evaluated
the nutrient adequacy of the PHD for adults compared to current diets by pairing estimated intake with
nutrient densities and comparing those to the average requirements of each population group (Appendix
1D). 127Current intake was estimated by triangulating waste-adjusted food availability data with data from
dietary surveys and estimated energy requirements (Appendix 1F).
For most nutrients, the PHD is adequate and performs better than current average diets, notably for fatty
acid profile, protein, free/added sugar, fibre, folate, magnesium, potassium, and zinc (Appendix 1D).
Intakes of calcium, vitamin B12, iron, and iodine from the PHD warrant further attention, especially in
populations that have low dietary diversity. Important differences are observed by national income
(Appendix 1D). In low-income countries, required intakes of most nutrients in the PHD are substantially
higher than current intakes, while in higher income countries, intakes of some micronutrients like vitamin
B12 and B6 are lower than current intakes, while remaining adequate. Op misa on within the PHD
reference values for general food groups (e.g., increasing the propor on of green leafy vegetables for
iron, fermented soy foods for B12, and algae for B12 and iodine) can ensure nutri onal adequacy for all
popula on groups.128 On average, calcium intake in the PHD is higher than current intakes, but it was
estimated to be lower than recommended intake in some countries without optimisation (Appendix 1D).
However, calcium requirements are likely overestimated because they are based on short-term studies. 83
Notably, Indonesia has the lowest calcium intake (approximately 250 mg/day), and the lowest fracture
rate.83 Global average vitamin B12 intakes, already currently slightly below adequate intake, were slightly
reduced in the PHD when not optimised. Vitamin B12 intakes are likely to be low if intakes of all animalsource foods are on the lower end of the PHD ranges (e.g., in vegan and vegetarian diets) and no
supplementary foods are consumed; this merits attention because deficiencies can result in permanent
neurologic damage.129 Dietary modifications with traditionally available food sources (e.g., fermented
soybeans and algae in East Asia, fermented sesame in Western Africa, bivalves in coastal areas, and
nutritional yeast more widely) can ensure vitamin B12 adequacy.128 In populations with biochemical
evidence of inadequate intake, fortification or micronutrient supplementation should be considered.
Absorbed iron intake in the PHD reference diet is similar to the current global average intake. Absorption
efficiency was estimated by accounting for regulatory components such as phytates, vitamin C, and haem
and non-haem iron in diets (Appendix 1D). The iron needs of women of reproductive age merit specific
attention (see section below). Specific attention should be placed to ensure adequate intake of iron-rich
food sources, including legumes, soy products, dark green vegetables, and whole grains. Increases in red
meat consumption (as an iron source) above the recommended ranges of the PHD are associated with
other adverse health outcomes as outlined above and in Appendix 1A. Estimated iodine intake currently
and from the PHD appear marginal, but intake depends strongly on soil composition where foods are
produced (proximity to marine environments) and seafood consumption including algae.

Absorption of nutrients like iron, zinc, and vitamin A can be compounded by parasitic and other infectious
diseases common in low and middle-income countries (LIC, MIC). Because of complexities in estimated
nutrient intake, absorption, and losses, monitoring the prevalence of nutrient deficiency is critical.
Improvements in diet quality, together with appropriate fortification (e.g., of salt for iodine deficiency) or
micronutrient supplementation strategies and non-nutritional interventions (e.g., water, sanitation, and
infectious disease control), are possible approaches to address nutrient deficiency.

More than one billion adolescent girls and women suffer from undernutrition, anaemia, and other
micronutrient deficiencies which can result in devastating health impacts.134 The overall health of women
of reproductive age is supported by the PHD as presented in Table 1 and Appendix 1E. The iron needs of
women of reproductive age merit specific attention due to menstrual loss, pregnancy, and lactation.

Dietary needs of Infants and Young Children

667
668
669
670
671

Young children under the age of two years have unique requirements for rapid growth and development.
Exclusive breast feeding, including timely access to colostrum, in the first six months of infancy is strongly
advised to ensure optimal nutrition, reducing risk of infectious disease, prevention of both undernutrition
and obesity, and for other benefits for infants and their mothers.140 From around six months of age,
introduction of safe complementary foods based on diverse, locally available, and nutrient-dense food is

The PHD provides approximately 14% of energy from protein, which exceeds requirements (EAR 0.7 g/kg
body weight, approximately 10% of energy intake), and will provide sufficient essential amino acids. 130
The relation between protein intake and health outcomes will depend on the protein sources. In a metaanalysis, protein intake was inversely associated with lower overall mortality, but this apparent beneﬁt
was en rely due to plant rather than animal sources of protein.131 Somewhat higher protein intakes (e.g.,
15 to 30% of energy) have been suggested as important for older adults.132 This was supported in an
analysis on healthy ageing, where the benefit was almost entirely due to plant rather than animal sources
of protein.133 The PHD provides approximately 53% of calories from carbohydrates and 35% from total fat,
which is compatible with the flexible amounts of these macronutrients.

A healthy diet during pregnancy is critical to prevent adverse maternal outcomes, support healthy foetal
development, and avoid foetal programming that can transmit metabolic dysfunction to future
generations.135 Overall food intake should be adjusted for optimal weight gain, and include a variety of
healthy foods,136,137 consistent with the PHD (Table 1). Although balanced vegetarian diets can support
healthy foetal development,138 use of micronutrient supplements or fortified foods may be indicated if
animal-sourced foods are below the reference intakes. Because of extra nutritional demands of
pregnancy, the WHO recommends136 routine iron and folic acid supplementation to prevent maternal
anaemia, puerperal sepsis, spina bifida, and anencephaly. Other context-specific recommendations
include specific micronutrient supplementation (calcium, vitamin A, and zinc) or routine use of multiple
micronutrient supplementa on during pregnancy, which is now used rou nely in many countries because
of beneﬁts beyond those provided by iron and folate alone.139

essential for optimal growth and development.141 Continued breast feeding is important and deserves
promotion, protection, and support.

Adopting the PHD pattern globally

688
689
690
691
692
693
694

The components of healthy diets, described broadly in Table 1 as reference values with ranges and
potential exchanges among food groups, allow great flexibility because they are compatible with a wide
variety of foods, agricultural systems, cultural traditions, and individual dietary preferences. 128 Possible
ranges for individual components are described, but with overall intakes of animal-sourced foods being
approximately two servings per day. The suggested daily or weekly amounts can be consumed at once or
spread out in small amounts; for example, red meat might be consumed as an 80 to 100 g serving once a
week or as small amounts in mixed dishes or soups, as is customary in many food traditions.

Dietary habits and taste preferences are developed in early childhood, and it is important that children
become familiar with healthy foods that lays the foundation for a healthy life. Fish, meat, eggs, and dairy
foods are rich sources of some key nutrients for children of this age, including iron, vitamin B12, zinc, and
calcium.142,143 The amounts of these animal-source foods in Table 1 would be suitable for most young
children 6-24 months of age.140 Lower amounts of animal-source foods can result in nutritional
deficiencies if not adequately replaced, including Vitamin B12 deficiency, which is associated with poor
neurodevelopment in young children.142 For children with animal-source food intakes below the PHD
reference values, complementing their diet with the use of nutrient supplements or fortified foods can
make nutrient intakes adequate.140,143,144 Minimally processed foods should be prioritised, while ultraprocessed foods and beverages should be minimised as most are nutritionally unbalanced and may
contain harmful additives.

The balance of healthy animal- and plant-source foods presented in the PHD is consistent with many
dietary patterns, including Indigenous diets,145,146 the well-studied Mediterranean diet,113,147 and other
traditional diets from around the world. The traditional diets have in common with the PHD a base of
whole grains or tubers, a wide variety of greens and other vegetables, legumes, and small to modest
amounts of fish or meat with herbs and sauces that add flavour, variety, and nutritional value. 145,148,149
Moreover, many traditional food practices support improved bioavailability of nutrients and build on rich
knowledge of the seasonality of cultivated and wild food species, supporting year-round food and
nutrition security.145,146 Although more research on traditional diets in different regions is desirable, many
of these patterns are consistent with high dietary quality 150 and low rates of chronic disease. Protecting
healthy traditional diets and food systems can be a critical way to avoid transitions to unhealthy
consumption. Examples of healthy meals based on tradi onal foods and ﬂavours and consistent with the
PHD are described in Appendix 1H.
In settings with humanitarian crises, deep food insecurity, or in populations where diets remain
dominated by staple carbohydrates (e.g. largely rice or cassava), food supplements, including fortified
foods, may be required while improving diet quality.151,152 The time-cost and labour, mainly of women, for
food preparation needs to be considered, and if cooking fuel is limited, this can prevent the preparation
24

713
714
715

720
721

724

726

of nutrient-dense foods, such as legumes. Availability of refrigeration and cooking facilities may also
constrain food preparation. In some situations, processed, ready-to-eat foods may be necessary, and care
is needed to ensure these are nutritionally balanced. Adequate amounts of safe water for drinking and
sanitation are always essential. Importantly, achieving PHD-aligned diets represents an important goal for
populations shouldering a high burden of undernutrition.

Summary and Conclusions of Healthy Diets

728
729
730
731

753

We draw on key evidence from long-term observational studies relating individual dietary components
and overall dietary patterns to major chronic disease outcomes, randomised trials with intermediate-risk
factors as outcomes, together with randomised trials of health endpoints.

Figure 2 describes the gaps between the PHD reference values and current diets that vary greatly among
regions. Notably, red meat and tuber/starchy root intakes are excessive in most regions except South Asia.
Tuber/starchy root intake in Sub-Saharan African substantially exceeds the reference value and is higher
compared to other regions. Free or added sugars intake is higher than the reference value in all regions
except Sub-Saharan Africa and East Asia-Pacific. In most regions, intakes of many healthy foods are low,
including vegetables, fruits, legumes, nuts and seeds, and whole grains (replacing refined grains);
increasing consumption of these foods offer important opportunities to improve health.

New evidence, including dose-response rela onships, has added substan al support for the health
beneﬁts of the PHD described in the 2019 EAT-Lancet Commission (Appendix 1A).1 This further supports
emphasising whole grains over reﬁned grains and potatoes, 48 plant sources of protein (legumes, pulses,
and nuts) over red meat (unprocessed or processed),51,76 unsaturated plant oils over saturated fats,85,153,154
including generous amounts of fruits and vegetables,52 moderate amounts of dairy, ﬁsh, eggs, and poultry,
and limi ng free or added sugars and salt.109,155,156
The elements of a healthy diet and the recommended intake ranges for main food groups outlined in
Table 1 allow great flexibility because they are compatible with a wide variety of foods, agricultural
systems, cultural traditions, and individual dietary preferences. They can be combined in various types of
omnivorous or flexitarian diets as well as vegetarian and vegan diets. The findings of health benefits in
many populations for this and similar dietary patterns show that healthy dietary patterns can be achieved
in contemporary populations and in many countries globally.
In making global estimates of impacts of diets on health, another general conclusion is that available data
on the composition of foods, national dietary intakes, and the relation of dietary factors to health are far
from optimal. Notably, large cohort studies with long follow-up are lacking from Africa, Latin America, and
most parts of Asia. Because aspects of diet strongly influence health, enhancements in data availability
and quality are needed to provide better precision in future analyses and more specific regional and local
dietary guidance.

Panel 2: The Planetary Health Diet and Estimated Reduction in Avoidable Deaths among Adults
The poten al reduc on in avoidable deaths among adults by adop ng the planetary health diet (PHD)
has been es mated in two ways for this Commission. One approach used an updated compara ve risk
assessment (CRA) analysis based on rela ve risks for speciﬁc dietary factors and incidence of speciﬁc
diseases (e.g., red meat intake and type 2 diabetes) using published meta-analyses of cohort studies,
most having a single assessment of diet (Appendix 1G). These associa ons were then applied to
country-speciﬁc data on diets and cause-speciﬁc mortality. In this approach, adop on of the PHD was
es mated to prevent about 10 million avoidable deaths per year among adults globally, represen ng
17% of total mortality.P19 About half of the reduc on in avoidable deaths was due to composi onrelated risks, including increased whole grains, fruits and vegetables, legumes and nuts, and less red
and processed meat consump on. The remaining half was due to an cipated reduc ons in
underweight, overweight, and obesity.
To es mate more comprehensively the poten al popula on level impact of adop ng the overall PHD
pa ern on mortality, the PHD reference valuesP20 were used to create the PHD Index (PHDI), a score
for the consistency of individual or na onal diets with the PHD pa ern.P21,P22 The associa on between
individual PHD scores and mortality was es mated from the follow-up of over 200,000 men and
women over more than 30 years; diet was assessed every four years to account for changes in food
consump on over me and to reduce measurement errors (Appendix 1A).P21
Combining the rela ve risks for PHD scores and mortality with the same data on country-speciﬁc diets
and mortality used for the CRA analysis(described above), an es mated 15 million avoidable deaths
per year among adults (27 % of total deaths) could be avoided globally by achieving a PHD score of
120 (140 being perfect consistency).P22 This es mate did not include assump ons about reduc ons in
overweight or obesity; including such beneﬁts would result in greater reduc ons in mortality. Notably,
when only the baseline dietary assessment was used in the follow-up study, about two-thirds of the
mortality reduc on was missed. Because achieving a global PHD score of 120 is ambi ous, we also
es mated the poten al beneﬁt from achieving a global PHD score of 100 (only 15 points above current
diets); the es mated reduc on of avoidable deaths was seven million annually (13% of total deaths).
In both the CRA and the PHD pa ern analyses, eﬀects of sodium intake were not included due to
diﬃcul es in measurement, and both analyses did not include indirect eﬀects of diet mediated by the
environmental impacts of food produc on. Both analyses used data primarily from high-income
countries for es ma ng rela ve risks because studies from other world regions are currently lacking,
represen ng an important research gap that should be addressed. However, much evidence supports
that rela ve risks for diet and health outcomes are quite generalizable across popula ons. Addi onal
beneﬁts due to substan al improvements in diet quality are also likely for children, but these are
diﬃcult to es mate quan ta vely because of major interac ons between diet and infec ous diseases,
which are par cularly important in low- and middle-income countries.

27

In summary, diﬀerent methods for es ma ng the impacts of adherence to the PHD yield diﬀerent
es mates of the popula on level impact on mortality. As expected, the use of an overall dietary
pa ern that incorporates the addi ve and interac ve eﬀects of most major dietary components,
combined with repeated assessments of diet over the follow-up period to calculate rela ve risks,
provides a higher es mate of avoidable deaths. For thoroughness and transparency, we have provided
results from two approaches; with con nued improvements in data sources, further reﬁnements can
be expected in the future.
