Panel 3: Indirect Eﬀects of Diet on Human Health
In addi on to the direct eﬀects of diet on human health, food systems and diets also have many
indirect eﬀects on health mediated by their environmental impacts (see sec on on Sustainable Food
Systems within Planetary Boundaries) or societal factors (see sec on on Jus ce in Food Systems).
Here we provide some examples mediated by environmental impacts which we recommend merit
greater a en on by the scien ﬁc community.
Climate Change has many extensively documented eﬀects on human health, including heat stress
directly aﬀec ng human health, extreme weather events such as windstorms, ﬂooding, and prolonged
droughts aﬀec ng food produc on, water security, and a endant deteriora on in sanita on pu ng
people at risk of malnutri on, and increases in geographic areas and number of people aﬀected by
infec ous diseases such as dengue, malaria, and West Nile fever. P23 Moreover, climate change aﬀects
crop yields,P24 nutri onal content,P25 and food-based pathogens, with major indirect eﬀects on human
health through eﬀects on the availability and aﬀordability of nutri ous food.
Air Pollu on resul ng from food produc on includes small par culate ma er and ground level ozone,
which increase rates of cardiovascular, pulmonary, and neurogenera ve disease, and premature
deaths.P26 Globally agriculture accounts for 650,000 deaths or 20% of mortality related to poor air
quality.P27 This is largely from nitrogen pollu on from fer lizers leading to emissions of nitrogen oxides,
and nitrogen dioxide pollu on associated with around 4 million new cases of paediatric asthma a
year.P28 Biomass burning, notably in the global south, accounts for a smaller propor on, including from
land clearing and cooking fuel (164,000 deaths, 5% of mortality related to poor air quality). An
es mated 3 to 6% of global exposure to small par culate air pollu on could be avoided by adop on
of more plant-rich diets, resul ng in approximately 100,000 to 300,000 avoidable deaths annually.P29
Nitrogen Pollu on of drinking water due to excess fer liser use has been associated with
methemoglobinemia, colorectal cancer, thyroid disease, and birth defects. P30 Other adverse eﬀects of
nitrogen pollu on in waters may be mediated by eutrophica on of water bodies and overgrowth by
toxic algal blooms.P31 The ground water threshold is exceeded in 38% of agricultural lands globally P32
par cularly impac ng the health of vulnerable rural popula ons with strong groundwater
dependencies.

28

Biocides (including pes cides, herbicides, and fungicides) are used extensively in agriculture. P33
Although biocides are regulated in most countries to prevent risks to human health, their misuse (i.e.
overapplica on and lack of appropriate protec ve measures, par cularly for agricultural workers) and
co-exposure resul ng from mixtures in the environment, has mul ple adverse eﬀects on health.P34
Chronic exposure to pes cides, including by consump on of contaminated foods, is associated with
risks of Parkinson’s disease, diabetes, cancer, cardiovascular disease, and possibly with infer lity.P35
An microbial Resistance and Pandemic Risk have increased in part due to increased animal–human
contact (either through direct interac on with wildlife or indirectly through vectors or intermediate,
domes cated species) which is a prerequisite for zoono c spillover.P36 The large-scale global increases
in consump on of animal-source foods coming from a very small number of species, o en produced
in highly concentrated systems, increases risk.P37,P38 Ca le and hogs now make up ~43% of the
mammalian biomass on the planet (humans make up ~38%),P39 and domes c poultry makes up ~70%
of the avian biomass.P40 This large biomass poses a risk for the emergence of novel pathogens aﬀec ng
humans and animals, with an accelera on in the frequency of such epidemic/pandemic disease
events.P41 The scaleup in animal-source food produc on has been enabled by using an microbials as
growth promoters, and an microbials used for animal produc on represent about two-thirds of the
global produc on of an microbials.P42 The risk of an microbial resistance emerging from the use of
an microbials in animals is well-documented,P43,P44 and an es mated 1.4 million people die each year
from untreatable microbial infec ons.P45 We es mated that adop on of the PHD would decrease
an microbial use by ~42% due to reduc ons in animal sourced foods.P46

Section 2: Sustainable Food Systems within Planetary Boundaries
Planetary boundaries is an Earth system’s framework157 that quantifies limits for biophysical processes
that regulate the stability and resilience of life-support systems on Earth. Transgression of these
boundaries pushes the Earth system into an unsafe environmental space for humanity. The latest update
of the planetary boundary framework158 concludes that six of nine planetary boundaries have already
been transgressed. The 2019 EAT-Lancet Commission assessed five of these boundaries — land,
biodiversity, climate, blue water flows, and biogeochemical flows — and found that food systems carry
the greatest responsibility for boundary transgressions. Here we re-evaluate the evidence on food
systems impact on all nine planetary boundaries, including four boundaries not included in the 2019
Commission: aerosol loading, ozone depletion, ocean acidification, and novel entities (Table 2).
We quantify specific food system boundaries that represent the share of the Planetary boundaries
allocated to food systems (Figure 3). In contrast to planetary boundaries, food system boundaries are not
based on increasing risk levels in the Earth system but represent science-based environmental targets for
the global food system, aligned with the planetary boundary framework. As there is no consistent
framework to deﬁne such targets, we deﬁne food system boundaries based on three principles from the
exis ng literature: (i) through propor onal reduc ons necessary to move back to the safe opera ng space
(based on present-day food system contribu ons to transgressions); (ii) through top-down sectoral
op miza on of mi ga on costs using integrated assessment models; and (iii) by considering food system
boundaries equal to planetary boundaries (based on large uncertain es or the on dominant contribu on
of food systems to planetary boundary transgressions). In some cases, food system boundaries are based
on evidence that considers limi ng Earth system impacts while retaining the produc vity of agricultural
systems (see Text A2.1 and Table A2.1).159
Mee ng rising food demands must be achieved from less land, with food produc on that generates rather
than degrades ecological func ons.160,161 Sustainable Intensiﬁca on (SI) entails achieving important
reduc ons in the environmental impacts through increased eﬃciency, reduced losses, and reduced
pollu on.162 Ecological Intensiﬁca on (EI) is a subset of SI that enhances the environmental performance
of food produc on by promo ng ecological processes within agricultural ﬁelds, farms and landscapes,
such as above- and belowground carbon sequestra on, nutrient cycling and storage, pollina on, and
biological pest regula on.163,164 We synthesise evidence on the impacts of key groups of prac ces for
sustainable and ecological intensiﬁca on (SEI) across ﬁeld-scale indicators, to assess the poten al of SEI
in mi ga ng planetary boundary transgressions (Sec on Reducing the Environmental Foodprint of Food
Produc on). We use a novel ecological intensiﬁca on module from the FABLE model165 to assess the
global poten al of these prac ces across planetary boundaries (Panel 6).

Climate change

Our food systems release almost a third of the emissions driving climate change, and are also especially
vulnerable to global warming, with profound eﬀects on people’s food supply and health.170 Agriculture
and food release 16–17.7 GtCO2e yr–1, or about 30% of total global greenhouse gas (GHG) emissions.170–
172
Around a third of these emissions come from agriculture, including both livestock and crop production,
a third from conversion of natural ecosystems to cropping and grazing lands, and a third from nonagricultural aspects of food supply — including transport, cold storage, processing, retail, catering, food
management in the home, the manufacture of fertilisers and other agricultural inputs, and waste
management (Table A2.2).170,172 Most agricultural emissions come from produc on of animal-source food
(Figure 10a). Enteric fermenta on from ruminant animals (i.e. beef and lamb, and dairy) and manure
management linked to red meat and poultry account for large shares (Figure 1). Ruminant farming is the
primary driver of land use change.164 Up to a third of food system-wide emissions derive from oﬀ-farm
food-related processes, including fer lizer produc on and distribu on, post-farm food processing and
transforma on and transporta on. For example, more than 10% (1.9 GtCO2e yr-1) of the global food

system emissions are a ributed to food that is lost or wasted along the supply chain.173 Reaffirming the
boundary set in the 2019 Commission, we propose that GHG emissions from food systems are kept under
5 GtCO2e yr–1 by 2050 to keep global warming below 1.5 or 2°C (Figure 1). Several recent analyses support
a 5 GtCO2e yr–1 boundary, highligh ng that a more ambi ous boundary is possible when mi ga on op ons
include dietary change (Table A2.3). The residual emissions in this food system boundary comprise only
methane (CH4) and nitrous oxide (N2O) emissions from biological processes in agriculture. These emissions
are diﬃcult to abate even with global adoption of the PHD, though the boundary could in theory be further
reduced with wider adoption of increasingly plant-rich diets or major advances in emission-reducing
technologies.

Land system change

867
868
869
870
871
872
873

Human activities have substantially altered the Earth surface through conversion of intact lands to
croplands, grazing lands, and other industrial and urban land uses. Approximately 50% of ice-free lands
can still be considered largely 'intact' (natural ecosystems with low human appropriation or use). 31 Halting
further conversion of these lands, particularly protecting large forest biomes, is necessary to halt
biodiversity loss, reduce human climate forcing, and secure hydrological functioning of ecosystems.
31,158,177
We set the land boundary at 85% intactness of ecoregions included in boreal and tropical forest
biomes and at 50–60% for all other ecoregions as defined in the Planetary Boundaries and Earth

To meet the climate boundary involves tremendous ambition. Full energy decarbonisation and zero land
use change would together eliminate two-thirds of food system emissions, by largely eradicating CO 2
emissions. Energy use would need to be decarbonised throughout the food system, including in food
transport, refrigeration and preparation, on-farm machinery, and manufacture of agricultural inputs. Netzero land use change, aligned with the Land System Change and Biosphere Integrity boundaries, needs a
combination of secure land governance, increases in productivity, and reductions in demand. The
remaining third – the residual CH4 and N2O emissions from agriculture – can be reduced through dietary
change, management of food waste, and farming prac ces (e.g. improved livestock feed and fer liser use
eﬃciency). A further opportunity to keep food systems within the boundary is carbon sequestra on in
agriculture, which, when coupled with emissions reduc ons, could make food systems a net carbon sink
(e.g. Panel 5 & 6, Sec on Improving Environmental Performance of Food Produc on) by removing by
removing 1.4–5.5 –1GtCO2e (es mated economic poten al at a carbon price of US$100 per tonne).170,174,175
Sustainable and ecological intensiﬁca on prac ces that sequester carbon include soil carbon
management and agroforestry with an es mated 0.4–0.9 GtCO2e yr-1 and 0.4–1.1 GtCO2e yr-1 respec vely
with a soil carbon prices of US$100 per tonne.
A systems approach is essential to navigate trade-offs and rebound effects. Since options to reduce
emissions diminish as our remaining carbon budget falls below 200 GtCO2, direct capture of emissions is
now a universal component of scientific pathways to achieve 1.5 or 2°C (Figure 1). The preferred
technology is Bioenergy, Carbon Capture and Storage (BECCS), a land-intensive solution that would
compete with aﬀoresta on and offset reduced demand for land to feed livestock.176

Biosphere integrity

903
904
905
906

Life on our planet depends on the capacity of the biosphere to safeguard critical Earth system functions,
which in turn requires healthy, functioning ecosystems. Two complementary control variables for
biosphere integrity include biosphere functional integrity and ecosystem functional integrity. Biosphere
functional integrity refers to the sum of Net Primary Produc vity (NPP) from all ecosystems on Earth and
thus is used as an Earth system-wide control variable.183 NPP captures the photosynthe c energy and
material ﬂows into the biosphere, with the devia on between poten al NPP of intact ecosystems and the
actual NPP of converted systems. The biosphere integrity boundary sets a limit to human appropria on
of this energy (HANPP) at <10% of Holocene NPP (5.5 GtC yr-1).158 Func onal integrity of ecosystems is
deﬁned as ecosystems’ capacity to sustain nature’s contribu ons to people (e.g. food produc on).184
Intact ecosystems are func onally intact, i.e. with high func onal integrity. This boundary, which is set at
20–25% natural or semi-natural habitat per km2, is thus applied to agricultural and urban land-use
systems. From an Earth system perspec ve, func onal integrity contributes to ensuring gene and species

Currently, 48 Mkm2 (37% of the global land area) are used for agricultural production, of which croplands
and grazing lands cover 33% and 67% respectively.178 Almost 70% of ecoregions fall below the 50%
intactness boundary,31 predominantly driven by historical conversion of intact nature to agriculture
(Figure A2.1). Agricultural land alone breaches the local intactness boundary (>50% agricultural land
cover) in a third of the ecoregions (Figure A2.2). The con nuing loss of intact ecosystems, notably forests,
is predominantly driven by agriculture,179 and is particularly high in tropical forests that are cleared for
pasture, oil crops and soy production.179,180
Remaining within the land system boundary requires zero conversion of intact lands, by keeping global
agricultural area well below 48 Mkm2. This is a modification of how the land boundary was articulated in
the 2019 EAT-Lancet Commission as 13 M km2 which only included croplands. In addition to halting
conversion, reducing agricultural land and restoring intactness is critical across ecoregions, and
particularly important in forest biomes that are currently strongly transgressed, and require 5.5 Mkm 2 and
3 Mkm2 of tropical and temperate forest restoration, respectively, at the expense of agricultural land
(Figure A2.3).
The key strategy to reduce agricultural land area is a shift to healthy diets, particularly away from animalbased products and associated feed production (Section 4).181,182 Decreasing food loss and waste is a lever
with which the demand-side pressure can be further reduced. In aggregate, maintaining or increasing
yields is cri cal to keep agriculture within a reduced land area, recognising regional diﬀerences depending
on current yield levels. Sustainable and ecological intensiﬁca on (SEI) prac ces chieﬂy demonstrate either
improved (desirable eﬀect sizes) or maintained (negligible eﬀect sizes) produc vity (Sec on Reducing
Environmental Foodprints of Food Produc on). EI would require slightly more land than other practices
aiming to improve production but still fall below the land boundary globally (Panel 4).

this value ecosystem services to and from food produc on are lost. Recent reviews have increased this
threshold to 20–25% necessary to maintain minimal func oning, rather than complete loss of
func on.184,185

Freshwater change

952
953
954
955
956

Freshwater availability is critical to support drinking water needs, ecosystem functioning, and climate
mitigation by enabling carbon sequestration in the biosphere. 189 The freshwater cycle is increasingly
altered by human actions, affecting both blue water (rivers, reservoirs and groundwater) and green water
(held in soil and in plants).190 Recent planetary boundary assessments show that we have transgressed
boundaries for both blue and green water.158,191 These boundaries propose limits to the occurrence of

Agricultural systems, notably extensive monocultures, are the single largest drivers of both biosphere and
ecosystem functional integrity loss, through lowering actual NPP from land conversion, extracting NPP by
harvesting biomass, and below threshold retention of habitat within agricultural lands. Agriculture is
responsible for appropriating 72–85% of the total HANPP (9.9–11.7 GtC yr-1)186, while a significant portion
of agricultural lands (33–66%) are below functional integrity thresholds, and 88% of agricultural land are
used for food production (Figure 4a).184,187
Remaining within the food system boundary for biosphere integrity requires a reduction of HANPP back
within the safe planetary boundary level (<5.5 GtC yr-1),159 by increasing productivity on existing cropland
(i.e. closing agricultural yield gaps from e.g. nutrient deficits, see Section Nitrogen), and restoring
ecosystems on former agricultural lands (e.g. reforestation), to increase actual NPP back to levels of
potential NPP (Section Land System Change). Ecological Intensification practices, while not directly
assessed against HANPP here, include practices that ensure that larger portions of NPP are retained in or
returned to fields, for example as crop residue, manure, or perennial vegetation. Maximum field scale
HANPP of sustainable production systems merits attention as a key scalable sustainability metric.
Furthermore, reducing HANPP from harvested crops (currently ±2 GtC yr-1)186 is only possibly through
dietary shifts that reduce HANPP from crop production for animal feed, and by reducing food loss and
waste and feeding losses. Globally, around 775 kcal per capita per day is lost or wasted (represen ng
approximately 20% of the agricultural land use), of which the majority are crops. These losses occur along
the full supply chain (post-harvest, retail, food service, households), see Panel 4173,188 (Section
Transformation).
Increasing the functional integrity on productive lands requires widespread inclusion of embedded habitat
in agricultural lands to reach the planetary boundary safe zone.184,185 This target can be achieved by
protecting and restoring riparian buffers and hedgerows, improved fallows, returning crop waste to ﬁelds,
and other practices fitting agroecosystem context. Agricultural diversification practices support the
ecosystem functional integrity directly and can be managed to further enhance carbon sequestration
(Climate Boundary), increase biological pest control and pollination (Novel Entities Boundary), and
reduce losses of nutrients to the environment (Biogeochemical Flows Boundary).

excep onally dry and wet local events (including blue and green water availability), and deviate from the
blue water boundary proposed in the 2019 Commission, which is based on volumetric limits to
consumptive water use. It constrained the global agricultural consumptive use to <2,500 km3 yr-1 192 to
preserve regional ecosystem flow requirements (hereafter ‘EFR’, meaning the minimum water volume,
timing and quality needed to maintain good ecological status of aquatic systems). Here, we adopt the
novel boundary deﬁni on for green water (i.e. global land area beyond pre-industrial local green water
variability baseline), while keeping – although upda ng – the volumetric limit for blue water.
Food systems are major blue water users, mainly via irrigation.159,193 Water withdrawal estimates from
rivers and groundwater range between 2,700–3,100 km3 yr-1 ,171,193 of which between 1,200–1,800 km3 yr1
are consumptive use.193,194 Irrigation is responsible for around 70–90% of the global consumptive water
use, although estimates vary depending on the accounting methods and models used. 159,194 The majority
of agricultural land and produc on159 is rainfed and hence depends solely on green water193, but soil
moisture availability is changing across the world,191 including on agricultural lands (16.8% of agricultural
land is currently transgressed).196,197 We preliminarily define the food system boundary for green water
based on the latest planetary boundary definition (i.e. limi ng the land area beyond pre-industrial
variability baseline to 11%)159). For blue water, we propose that agricultural water consump on remains
below 2,000 km3 yr-1, which is lower than in the 2019 Commission (2,500 km3 yr-1), that allocated 90% of
the safe opera ng space to agriculture. We propose a more stringent approach considering uncertain es
in agricultural consump ve water use 193, and acknowledge the lower (stricter) end of the planetary
boundary uncertainty range (1,100 km3 yr-1).192 While this suggests that food systems are within the safe
operating space globally (Table 2), many rivers are experiencing severe water stress from irrigation water
withdrawal alone.198 In fact, >5% of the global food production depends on regional blue water
transgressions by tapping into EFRs (Figure 4b).198 In those areas, limiting irrigation water demand without
affecting crop yield could be achieved through shifting production to less water demanding foods, 199
reducing food loss and waste (currently wasting 20-25% of freshwater withdrawals),171,173 and increasing
water productivity through the adoption of agricultural practices that improve soil water holding capacity
(Section Reducing Environmental Footprint of Food Production). SEI practices are significantly effective
in increasing soil organic carbon (SOC) content, which in turn improves the water-holding capacity of
soils.200 Crop and non-crop diversification, especially agroforestry (e.g., Muchane et al., 2020 201), exhibit
strong desirable effects on the water regulation of soils by e.g. creating diverse root-systems and enabling
infiltration.

Nitrogen (N) and phosphorus (P) play a critical role in biological life, including crop and pasture production.
In some regions, crop yields are below their potential due to deficient N and P availability, while in others,
agricultural N and P surplus from excessive inputs cause environmental pollution, leading to biodiversity
loss and human health impacts.166,204 In addition, N2O emissions from fertiliser and manure contribute to
climate change (Section Climate Change) and stratospheric ozone depletion (Section Stratospheric Ozone
Depletion). Here, we adopt the ‘N surplus’ from agricultural land as control variable,167 defined as N input
minus the removal of N via crops and grasses. In contrast to ‘N input’ adopted in the 2019 Commission, N
surplus covers remaining reactive nitrogen that can lead to environmental harm via deposition on natural
ecosystems, groundwater leaching and surface water load.
Unlike N input, es mates of N surplus are not aﬀected by nitrogen use efficiencies (NUE, i.e. N removal
from plants compared to total N input), but ignore N removal from plants that moves up the food supply
chain and can end up in the environment via waste-water streams (i.e. via human excreta). The planetary
boundary for N surplus on agricultural land is 57 Tg N yr-1, allowing for an increase in N use in regions that
are currently deficient (Figure 4c). For P, we adopt ‘P losses to surface water’, which strongly depend on
the available P stocks in soils and erosion rates. Unlike the ‘P applica on’ control variable used in the 2019
Commission, we use P losses since applied (excess) P can be adsorbed and build up soil P stocks without
directly causing harmful environmental effects (unlike N). Based on critical P concentrations to preserve

good ecological status of global surface waters,205,206 we propose a global P loss boundary of 6.1 Tg of P
yr-1 .207
Current agricultural N inputs on cropland and managed grasslands (233 Tg N yr-1) are partly removed by
crops (114 Tg N yr-1), while 119 Tg yr-1 remains as surplus.167 This surplus — together with other N sources
from wastewater, aquaculture, NOx deposition from transport, and natural sources (i.e. allochthonous
matter)— contributes to N deposition on terrestrial ecosystems (23 Tg N yr-1, around 50% from food
systems), N loading in surface water (71 Tg N yr-1, around 70% from food systems), and leaching to ground
water (56 Tg of N yr-1, around 80% from food systems) (Table A2.4).159,207 Furthermore, almost 10 Tg of P
yr-1 is delivered from soils to surface water, with 75% of the P delivery (7.2 Tg P yr-1) derived from food
systems (Table A2.4).166
Bringing agricultural N surplus back within global safe levels (< 57 Tg N yr-1) requires a reduc on of
agricultural N input from 233 to 134 Tg N yr-1,167 (Panel 4) based on current NUE. Assuming propor onal
reduc ons across other sectors can only slightly relax this value (to 140 Tg N yr -1)167 since most N sources
are a ributed to the food systems (including agriculture, aquaculture and N from waste water) (Table
A2.4); while further reducing the contribu on from natural N sources (from allochthonous organic ma er)
is not possible. We therefore propose the food system boundary for N surplus to be equal to the planetary
boundary.
Increasing NUE from 0.48 (current global average) to an achievable 0.67 208 may bring us close to the safe
operating space without compromising yield.167 Nutrient surpluses are highly unevenly distributed, with
redistribu on being as important as a reduc on in fer lizer rates to limit planetary boundary eﬀects
(Figure 4c).
Agricultural diversification and fertiliser management show significant potential to reduce nutrient losses
to the environment, improve NUE, with some increased and chiefly neutral yield effects (Section Reducing
the Environmental Foodprints of Food Production). While reduced disturbance showed mixed results for
reducing nutrient losses in general, no-tillage significantly reduces phosphorus losses. Soil P stocks below
target levels should be increased globally, and P applica on should maintain this soil P status by replacing
P removed during cul va on and harvest and reducing physical loss via erosion control.
Returning within boundary conditions remains a challenge without greater attention to food system losses
originating from food consumption. Recovery of N and P sources from sewage water must similarly be
implemented to prevent post-consumption losses (i.e. around 26 Tg N yr-1 from human excreta of which
12 Tg N yr-1 is lost to surface waters).166 The impact of circular systems remains poorly assessed (but see
Panel 4) and merits attention. Recycling of N and P on fields, not only by using manure but also compost
and sludge, decreases losses to the biosphere, although these practices are increasingly challenged by
novel entity contaminants (e.g. Forever Chemicals or PFAS) in organic waste streams which can make
agricultural fields toxic.209

40

Panel 4: Circular food systems
Transi oning to more circular systems is thought to poten ally reduce (mineral) N and P use. To assess
these poten al contribu ons, we used CiFoS,P47 a biophysical op miza on model to simulate four 2050
scenarios that were benchmarked to a 2020 simula on (see Van Zanten et al., 2025P48 and Appendix 2
for more details):
1. BAU-2050-Opt: scenario op mizes a future 2050 scenario by minimizing mul ple
environmental impacts (use of land, N and P and GHG emissions) towards the food system
share of planetary boundaries, by realloca ng produc on globally. The economic and social
implica ons of this realloca on were not assessed.
2. BAU-2050-Opt+Cir: in addi on to the BAU-2050-Opt, allows a transi on to a more circular food
system where le overs (e.g., by-products, human excreta, feeding losses and food loss and
waste) are recycled.
3. EL-Opt: simulates a scenario which takes into account dietary shi s compa ble with the
Planetary Health Diet, allows food loss and waste (FLW) reduc ons of 50% and produc on
increases (in line with the mul -model ensemble described in Sec on 4).
4. EL-Opt+Cir: scenario adds circularity components to EL-Opt.
In the BAU-Opt scenario the produc on of le overs was lower (4.1 Gt) compared to BAU-Opt+Cir (6.8
Gt) and the diﬀerence between EL-Opt (1.7Gt) and EL-Opt+Cir (3.1Gt). U lized le overs increased from
24% in BAU-Opt to 84% for BAU-Opt+Cir and from 22% in EL-Opt to 90% for EL-Opt+Cir (Panel Figure
A). Results in the circular scenarios (BAU-Opt+Cir and EL-Opt+Cir) show a preference for recycling over
reducing the produc on of le overs. In 2020, the total use of N is 259 TgN and total use of P is 40 TgP,
including both mineral fer lizers and organic sources (Panel Figure B). Compared to 2020, in an
op mized but not circular food system (BAU-Opt) the use of N decreased by 21% to 205 TgN, and the
use of P decreased by 49% to 21 TgP. Adding circularity measures (BAU-Opt+Cir) reduced N by 29% to
184 TgN and P by 55% to 18 TgP and EL-Opt reduced N by 45% to 142 TgN and P by 67% to 13TgP.
Combining circularity with an EAT-Lancet-aligned food system (EL-Opt+Cir) reduced N by 50% to
128TgN and P by 73% to 11TgP. N and P use in both EL scenarios are in line with staying within their
respec ve planetary boundary (boundary value of 134 TgN total N input to stay within the N surplus
boundary of 57 TgN per year (Table 2) and between 8 - 16 Tg of mineral P fer lizer per year based on
Springmann et al., 2018 P49 to limit the amount of P reaching surface waters). Although the results of
EL-Opt and EL-Opt+Cir only slightly diﬀer, there are clear diﬀerences in the fer lizer sources used, e.g.
more organic fer lizer sources are used in EL-Opt+Cir (Panel Figure B). Nevertheless, despite
substan al reduc ons in mineral fer lizer use due to increases in circular sources of N and P (ElOpt+Cir), mineral fer lizers remain a cri cal input into agricultural produc on.

The planetary boundary for novel entities proposes limits to the release of human-made substances in
the environment — ranging from synthetic chemicals, such as plastics and endocrine disruptors, to
radioactive materials and genetically modified organisms. The risk posed to Earth system stability and
resilience from novel entities is uncertain, but potentially large and persistent following cumulative and
mixing effects (i.e. chemical interaction of multiple substances in the environment), thereby threatening
all life on Earth. There are >350,000 synthetical chemicals produced and released in the environment, and
many are insufficiently tested for their potential environmental risks.210 Uncertainties regarding the
associated risks of novel entities continue to frustrate the definition of a novel entities boundary, which

is therefore preliminarily and precautionarily placed at zero release of synthetic chemical compounds
without adequate safety testing that can demonstrate that no harmful environmental effects occur upon

Aerosol loading

1118
1119
1120

Air pollution is a major human health issue (Panel 3), negatively affects crop production by changing the
photosynthetically available light,222 and can have critical Earth system impacts on precipitation and
circulation patterns of the atmosphere and ocean. The planetary boundary for aerosol loading is

While novel entities were not assessed in the 2019 Commission, food systems contribute to the release
of various novel entities, such as pesticides,168,203 genetically modified crops, antimicrobials, (micro)plastics, 211 and ‘forever chemicals’, such as PFAS, throughout the food chain.212 Here, we adopt ‘pesticide
application’ and ‘antimicrobial use’ as indicators for novel entities, because their widespread use in
agriculture and potential risks to the environment and human health. 159,213,214
Pesticides are widely used in crop protection and weed control and pose major health risks to non-target
species. Global pesticide application amounts to 3.3–3.7 Tg of pesticide active substances (PAS) yr-1 213, of
which 85–90% is used in agriculture.215 Safety levels for individual pesticide concentrations are crossed
throughout terrestrial and aquatic systems (Figure 4d). Although most pesticides degrade over time,
remaining residues build up in organisms and pose risks through mixing-effects in the environment. 216
Furthermore, legacy contamination can continue for extended periods of time, decades in some cases, in
soil, surface water and groundwater.168 These factors challenge the definition of safe levels of pesticide
use. To minimise exceedance of high risks (i.e., a greater than 90% probability for a random species to be
affected by pesticides), we propose a global reduction of 70% compared to current levels equa ng to a
maximum applica on of 1 Tg of PAS yr-1 (Figure A2.4). A further reduction of more than 95% (max.
application of 0.2 Tg PAS yr-1) is required to remain below “low” environmental risks (i.e., a less than 5%
probability for a random species to be affected by pesticides).159,203
Ecological intensiﬁca on prac ces can manage pest outbreaks without compromising crop yield, while
enhancing biosphere ecosystem func onal integrity (Figure 5).217 However, some agricultural prac ces
such as reduced soil disturbance can result in higher weed loads (Figure 5), highligh ng the importance
of employing integrated complementary prac ces (Panel 5).
An microbial use for food-animal produc on is es mated at 73–130 kilotons yr-1 169,218,219 and has
increased an microbial resistance prevalence.66 Se ng a boundary for an microbial use is challenging
due to the uncertain es in the environmental risks from increasing an microbial resistance.214 Based on
WHO recommenda ons and available evidence on lowering average applica on rates while keeping
eﬀec ve produc on systems,219 we propose the complete hal ng of prophylac c an microbial use, and
halving of exis ng average applica on rates from 2020 values of 50 to 25 mg kg-1 animal.159 Global shi s
to ﬂexitarian diets can reduce global an microbial use by around 42% compared to baseline values for
2050, whereas combining with moderate biosecurity measures could reduce global use by 49%.221

expressed as the mean annual interhemispheric difference in the aerosol optical depth (AOD), 158 capturing
the reduction in sunlight from air pollutants, such as nitrogen components (NH3, NOx and N2O), SO2, CO,
organic compounds, and particulate matter (PM). Increased AOD affects regional rainfall patterns, and
changes in the interhemispheric difference are associated with abrupt transitions in monsoon systems.
Although the planetary boundary for atmospheric aerosol loading is not transgressed (Table 2), food
systems are a prominent source of air pollutants (Figure A2.5), such as ammonia from fertiliser use and
livestock production (37–47 Tg N yr-1, 86% of total NH3 emissions);167,223 airborne dust from human-driven
land system change (e.g. desertification and land management practices); and primary PM2.5 emissions,
i.e. aerosols’ diameter smaller than 2.5 micrometre (>28% of total primary PM2.5; emissions), mostly from
burning for clearing vegetation.223 Dominant sources of emissions vary regionally and between
hemispheres.224 In parts of the northern hemisphere, N-based emissions from livestock and fertiliser are
the dominant source of aerosol-loading, accounting for >80% of NH3 emissions forming secondary PM2.5
particles, while in the southern hemisphere, biomass burning is the predominant source of trace-gas
emissions and PM2.5 concentrations.224 Emissions from biomass burning (globally estimated to contribute
42 Tg yr-1 PM2.5),225 that can be attributed agriculture are uncertain, although some estimates suggest
that 28% of the global direct PM2.5 emissions are food related (Table A2.1). Regionally, shares can be
higher, particularly in countries in the southern hemisphere (i.e. biomass burning contributes 90% to
PM2.5 in Angola).224

Ocean Acidification

1151
1152
1153
1154
1155

Ocean acidification is linked to loss of marine species dependent on calcium carbonate, and changes to
marine carbon storage. Acidification also aggravates impact of other biogeochemical changes (e.g. ocean
warming and associated thermal heat stress) and therefore is considered a threat multiplier to marine
life.228 Ocean acidification, measured through the change in the aragonite saturation state (Ω arag),158 is
driven by atmospheric CO2 concentration (i.e. 25% of historical anthropogenic CO2 emissions are absorbed
by the ocean229) and is therefore strongly related to the climate boundary. Although the ocean
acidiﬁca on planetary boundary is not transgressed (Table 2),158 it is moving rapidly into the zone of
increasing risk following relatively constant CO2 emissions (total of 40.6 GtCO2 yr-1 ￼ of which food systems
are responsible for nearly 25%.159,172 Remaining with the food system boundary for climate change and
biosphere integrity can eﬀec vely bring back CO2 emissions close to zero and allows us to remain within
ocean acidiﬁca on boundary.

Remaining within aerosol boundaries requires emission reductions in both the northern and southern
hemisphere. The boundary for nitrogen surplus (Sec on Biogeochemical Flows) suggests a reduction of
NH3 emissions (>45% reduc on of current emissions, from 37 to 20 Tg N yr-1) is required, mostly in the
northern hemisphere where N surplus (and associated NH3 emissions from manure and fertilizer167) is high
(Figure 4c). Dietary shifts can reduce agricultural NH3 emissions with more than 50% in North America and
Europe.226 In the southern hemisphere, biomass burning from land conversion and on agricultural lands
should be eliminated in line with the Land System change and Climate change boundaries. However, it is
uncertain how much reduced NH3 emissions and biomass burning can bring back total PM2.5
concentrations across hemispheres.159

Stratospheric ozone depletion

1164
1165
1166
1167
1168

The planetary boundary for stratospheric ozone depletion limits the breakdown of ozone that protects
life on Earth from harmful solar radiation. Coordinated global intervention phasing out production of
ozone-depleting chlorofluorocarbons and halons (i.e. through the Montreal Protocol adoption in 1987)
has restored the ozone layer.158 However, the most important ozone-depleting substance remaining is
N2O, which is not regulated under the Montreal Protocol. Agriculture is the largest emitter of
anthropogenic N2O (54–69% of total N2O emissions),230 but ozone depletion rates remain insignificant.
Even without reductions in N2O emissions, stratospheric ozone levels are expected to recover by the end
of this century.167 We therefore restate the nitrogen surplus boundary that respects local boundaries for
biodiversity and water quality (Section Nitrogen and Phosphorus losses), and thereby reduces N2O
emissions from reduced fertilizer and manure applications with 55% compared to current levels (from 3.9
to 1.8 Tg N yr-1).159,167

Reducing the environmental footprint of food production

Sustainable and ecological intensiﬁca on (SEI) practices offer considerable potential to mitigate planetary
boundary transgressions without nega vely aﬀec ng yields (Figure 5). Importantly, many practices offer
simultaneous benefits across several boundaries. Soil organic carbon (SOC) accumula on can be
enhanced with agricultural diversification practices that promote plant root-formation, microbial activity,
and the return of organic matter to soils, thereby reducing HANPP, while uncertain es remain regarding
the permanence of soil carbon pools which challenge estimates of this potential. 231,232 Agricultural
diversification increases agroecosystem biodiversity, which can be specifically managed to assist in weed
and pest abatement, or to reduce the prevalence of crop diseases and nutrient losses while maintaining
or improving yields. Improved fer liser management enhances crop NUE, reduces nutrient losses, and
contributes to SOC, although with higher variability for GHG emissions and yields.
The eﬀects of the prac ces (Figure 5) are not directly addi ve, since they were typically obtained from
studies comparing a single prac ce against a controlled alterna ve. Prac ce eﬀects vary depending on
context-speciﬁc, biophysical factors such as clima c and soil condi ons, as well as the exact
implementa on of the prac ce. Working farms typically implement mul ple prac ces simultaneously,
and the evidence to date suggests synergies leading to enhanced environmental, as well as social,
beneﬁts.233 While the meta-analyses provide valuable informa on on the technical poten al at the ﬁeldscale, es mates of the global poten al of selected SEI prac ce groups have also been explored in
modelling studies such as, but not limited to those men oned in Panels 5-7. These modelling studies,
using diﬀerent methodologies, factor in addi onal constrains and moderators, such as our current
understanding of applicable areas. When applied to all suitable areas globally and assuming maximum
complementarity among measures, sets of SEI prac ces were found to provide a poten al increase in
nitrogen use eﬃciency, from its average of 48%, to 78%,208 and a global carbon sequestra on poten al
between 0.44 and 0.68 Gt C yr−1. When limita ons of applica on as well as current applica on levels were
factored in, the carbon sequestra on poten al was es mated to be 0.28 to 0.43 Gt C yr–1,175 while the
GHG mi ga on poten al of 4.4 Pg CO2e on average, was es mated to occur by 2050.234 Despite these

Integra ng livestock in food produc on systems, when respec ng local environmental capaci es, such as
nutrient loads and grazing intensi es, can represent an SEI strategy that supports ecological processes in
agricultural ecosystems (Panel 8).242 Livestock can play an integral role in the food web, such as nutrient
and energy cycling, as well as promo ng biodiversity. Animals and crop produc on prac ces can be
interlinked via feed produc on, for both terrestrial livestock and farmed ﬁsh, with synergies to be gained
through e.g. cover-crop grazing,243 forage rota ons, or integra ng animals for weed or pest
management.244 While grazing can increase SOC of grasslands, the evidence is variable, 237 but shows that
grazing intensity has a signiﬁcant eﬀect, with less intensive systems enhancing SOC. 245 Some of the
prac ces assessed here (Figure 5) include livestock-integration, namely silvopastoral systems, using
manure as organic amendments, and rice-animal co-cultures.
A comprehensive assessment of foods from aquatic systems is provided by the Blue Foods Assessment. 246
For wild fisheries, improved yields and environmental performance can be achieved by fishing within
ecological limits, informed by scientific advice247,248 and using production methods that limit indirect
impacts to ecosystems (e.g. habitat destruction and bycatch,249 including co-management of marine areas
with local communities.250,251 In aquaculture, most environmental impacts are driven by feeds252 that emit
GHG and use cropland,253 while aquatic farms impact local ecosystems through acidification,
eutrophication, and disease risks for wild populations.254,255 These impacts can be limited through SEI
practices that improve nutrient cycling and feed efficiencies. For mariculture, integrated multitrophic
systems such as those growing bivalves and seaweeds near fish farms can increase yields, capture carbon,
and reduce nutrient pollution.256 Targeted feed improvements through continued reductions in wild fish
use,255 improved by-product utilisation,257 and higher conversion efficiencies of plant ingredients, can
further help improve environmental performance of 'fed’ aquatic foods. 258
Panel 5: Conservation Agriculture, a combination of sustainable and ecological intensification
practices with impact at scale
Conservation agriculture (CA) is a well-establishedP50,P51 widely applicable and appliedP52 example of
the complementarity of SEI practices, whereby reduced soil disturbance, continuous soil cover, and
crop diversification form the basis of the system. In this integrated approach, reduced soil disturbance
protects the soil from erosion and conserves moisture, continuous cover suppresses weeds, and crop
diversification reduce pests and disease. Global models applying CA, which here consists solely of no
llage, crop residues on ﬁeld, and non-leguminous cover crops, on all agricultural lands resulted,
es mate there will be an overall decrease in nitrogen leaching (-22%), and an increase in root-zone soil
moisture (+15%) and SOC (+2%) by 2050, when compared to a land-use scenario without CA (averaging
over two Integrated Assessment Models and ﬁve Global Change Models; Appendix 2B). Importantly,
47

other SEI practices can be combined with CA. When rainwater harvesting was combined with CA, global
modelling results showed a minor added reduction (–2%) for nitrogen leaching and a small increase
in root-zone soil moisture (+2%), but effectively no change for SOC. While field studies have shown
that the exact outcomes of CA are context-specific, evidence shows that CA improves crop
productivity, especially in dry climates and rainfed production systems, thus offering a climate-change
adaptation strategy.P53,P54 In resource-poor and vulnerable smallholder farming systems, socioeconomic challenges in the appropriate adoption of CA need to be carefully considered. P54

The FABLE Calculator is a demand-driven food and land use system model designed to explore the
food, biodiversity, climate, water, and socioeconomics impacts of future development pathways. P55 It
includes 88 agricultural products to model how demand for these products drives land use change and
associated impacts on food and nutri on security, climate mi ga on, biodiversity, and socio-economic
outcomes.
The FABLE Calculator diﬀeren ates between types of agricultural prac ces used on cropland and can
therefore be used to explore the impact of scaling ecological intensiﬁca on prac ces. Here, ecological
intensiﬁca on (EI) represents crop and non-crop diversiﬁca on, including intercropping, cover crops,
crop rota ons, agroforestry, cul var mixtures, and embedding natural vegeta on into cropped
landscapes. We modelled the eﬀect of con nuing current trends (BAU pathway), compared to
expanding EI prac ces across all cropland by 2050 in combina on with the EAT-Lancet (EL) popula on
growth, economic growth, climate change, diet, produc vity, food loss and waste assump ons as
described in the modelling sec on and Table A.4.3 (EL+EI pathway). The EL pathway represents a
decomposi on scenario on the EL+EI pathway, where agricultural prac ces match BAU assump ons,
allowing us to isolate the eﬀects of EI. See Appendix 2 for further methodological details.

FABLE model results show that under BAU, the food system boundaries for land, biodiversity,
emissions, water and nutrient use are exceeded by 2050. Under an EL pathway, the food system stays
within land, biodiversity, and emission boundaries. Increases in produc vity, dietary shi s, and
reduced food loss and waste, reduce the land area needed for agricultural produc on which in turn
drives the expansion of natural land available for biodiversity. Annual net GHG emissions would fall
from 7.35 Gt CO2e in 2050 under BAU to 2.75 Gt CO2e under EL (~60% reduc on compared to 2020).
This reduc on in GHG emissions is greater than those suggested by the economic model ensemble
(median 20%), or DIA-GIO (32%) (Figure 10b), which may be explained by diﬀerences in how emissions
are computed across models. GHG emission reduc ons in the FABLE EL pathway are driven by a
decrease in emissions from agricultural produc on for both crops and livestock, and increased carbon
sequestra on from avoided deforesta on and restora on of abandoned agricultural land to forest and
other natural land (with higher rates of carbon sequestra on in living biomass and soil). Nitrogen use
is reduced by 30% compared to BAU, while there are rela vely small reduc ons in water consump on
and phosphorus use, yet nutrient and water use con nue to exceed food system boundaries.
Combining EL with ecological intensiﬁca on (EL+EI pathway) further reduces agricultural land area,
water and nitrogen use, and substan ally reduces emissions to -0.51 Gt CO2e. Many of the EI prac ces
that the FABLE Calculator are not used in many of the ensemble models. Emission reduc ons are driven
by increased sequestra on on croplands (in soil) and, to a lesser extent, addi onal conversion of
agricultural land to other uses due to enhanced produc vity for some commodi es. The FABLE model
results suggest that signiﬁcant eﬀort in integra ng EI prac ces could contribute to achieving a net zeroemission food system. Es mates of the eﬀect of the prac ces on soil organic carbon sequestra on are
taken from global meta-analyses, and do not account for regional or local soil carbon satura on
limits,P56 which mean the results should be interpreted with cau on.
The most eﬀec ve applica on of EI prac ces needs to be tailored to local contexts and should be coselected with local farmers and landscape stakeholders, integra ng local and scien ﬁc knowledge. The
complexity of implementa on at scale across mul ple domains, and local carbon sequestra on
satura on points may limit both the speed of implementa on, and dura on of carbon sequestra on
poten al of ecological intensiﬁca on.

Section 3: Justice in Food Systems

What is a Just Food System

1254
1255
1256
1257
1258

1261

1267
1268

1291

Justice involves the ‘fair treatment’ of people, both individuals and groups. Although various ar cula ons
of jus ce exist across disciplines and sectors (e.g., Philosophy, Economics, Sociology, and Law), here we
draw on three interrelated dimensions of jus ce that feature prominently in the literature on social and
environmental jus ce: namely, Distribu ve, Representa onal (or Procedural), and Recogni onal (Figure
6).259,260 Distributive justice involves the fair distribution of important resources, opportunities, or
capabilities (healthy food, wages, clean, healthy, and sustainable environment) as well as the fair
allocation of benefits and burdens.33 Representational justice concerns decision-making and power,
including formal policymaking processes as well as broader societal decision-making processes.259,261 It
requires fair processes and a fair distribu on of power, the protec on of key freedoms, and poli cal voice
and representa on, including meaningful participation by those most affected by injus ce. Recognitional
justice manifests in the structures and norms of society and recognises a diversity of intersecting identities
and experiences, shaped by cultural, legal, historical and spatial contexts, and involves people from all
social groups being able to participate as equals.259,261,262
Our analysis concerns how these three dimensions of jus ce play out both within and across socie es. For
a society to be just, all people must experience distribu ve, representa onal and recogni onal jus ce,
and for global and interna onal jus ce this must also exist among na ons and people throughout the
world.261,263 Our analysis also encompasses both jus ce among people living at the same me (intragenera onal jus ce) and jus ce among people in diﬀerent genera ons (inter-genera onal jus ce).33 For
example, intergenera onal jus ce requires that there be distribu ve jus ce across genera ons, including
a fair distribu on of beneﬁts and burdens between current and future genera ons of people. Keeping
food systems within planetary boundaries can help to ensure that in the future, people have a safe
environment, and ensuring healthy diets are accessible to all, par cularly for women of childbearing age
and young children, can help ensure the health of future genera ons. Jus ce of all these forms is central
to our analysis and recommenda ons. Another form of jus ce, interspecies jus ce, concerns whether
humans treat other species fairly.33 However, beyond recognising the need to transi on to within the
planetary boundaries, including keeping the Earth within 1.5 degrees C of global warming, and the need
to minimise biodiversity and environmental losses, we do not focus on interspecies jus ce in our jus ce
analysis.
Establishing what counts as ‘fair treatment’ for an analysis of jus ce, requires consensus,264 and in the
absence of a global process relevant to food systems, we chose to align substan ally our account of ‘fair
treatment’ with interna onal human rights trea es and other instruments (Figure 6).265,266 These human
rights instruments represent a consensus view of what cons tutes minimum acceptable standards for
speciﬁc resources (e.g., food) and opportuni es (e.g., work).265 We appeal to the content of recognized
human rights to iden fy jus ce-based en tlements, and to specify, and quan fy minimum acceptable
levels (Figure 7, Appendix 3, Table 3). Our account of jus ce within food systems can thus be seen as the
minimum condi ons for what would cons tute jus ce. Recognising that all human rights are universal,
50

1292
1293
1294
1295
1296
1297
1298
1299
1300
1301
1302
1303
1304
1305
1306
1307
1308
1309
1310
1311
1312
1313

indivisible, interdependent, and interrelated, we focus on three rights closely related to food systems: the
right to food, the right to a healthy environment, and the right to decent work. We also draw on
interna onal human rights’ commitments to non-discrimina on, par cipa on, and civil and poli cal
rights. Central to our account is agency, understood as people’s capacity to make choices that shape their
own circumstances and to exercise voice through par cipa on in broader societal decision-making
processes. Agency is recognized as an important contributor to food security, secure livelihoods and to
addressing inequi es within food systems (Clapp et al., 2022) (Appendix 3).
Justice thus requires that everyone everywhere, regardless of social identity, has their rights to food, a
healthy environment, and decent work realized, and has poli cal voice and fair representa on. Achieving
jus ce therefore requires recognizing the social systems and other condi ons that can promote jus ce,
or, conversely, can func on as structural barriers to it. These include poli cal ins tu ons and economic
systems, and the interplay between them, resul ng in diﬀerent kinds of voice, power, and agency for
government, businesses, formal and informal food producers and workers, civil society organiza ons, and
the public; food, health, educa onal, and other social systems, which provide important resources and
opportuni es; and other social structures and norms. People are unevenly impacted by these social
systems, with some groups systema cally having fewer resources and opportuni es, and less voice,
power, and agency, thus experiencing structural inequality that leaves them vulnerable and marginalised
(Panel 7) Achieving jus ce may require fundamental transforma on of these poli cal systems. However,
here our focus is that jus ce requires paying par cular a en on to these people and addressing their
speciﬁc needs.262

Figure 6. Conceptual framework of the jus ce sec on. The three jus ce dimensions and three human rights are
interrelated and interdependent. Achieving the three jus ce dimensions of distribu ve, recogni onal, and

Panel 7: Recogni onal Injus ces: The need for a en on to gender and intersec onality
Diﬀerent groups of people – women, men, and children – of diﬀerent wealth and social groups, age and
stage in the life cycle, of diﬀerent ethnici es, and from diﬀerent geographic loca ons have diﬀerent
livelihood opportuni es and o en have diﬀeren al nutri onal needs. They also have diﬀering levels of
choice in terms of how far they can access and aﬀord healthy diets and decent work. Not paying
a en on to these group diﬀerences can lead to signiﬁcant harm, not only for the individuals concerned,
but for humanity more broadly by losing contextual knowledge and prac ces (e.g., Indigenous farming
prac ces).
Ethnic minori es and Indigenous peoples are o en at the greatest risk of losing their livelihoods and
access to healthy and culturally important food, processes that start with a loss of access to land or
natural resources (e.g., forests) through unjust conserva on or extrac on policies (e.g., exclusion from
management),P57 or the lack of protec on policies. Many of these marginalised groups, including
women, and par cularly women from minority groups, also face signiﬁcant structural barriers and
discrimina on, rooted in colonialism, racism, and patriarchy leading to lower access to technology,

52

training and underrepresenta on in poli cal and managerial posi ons,P58,P59 making it diﬃcult for them
to build decent and resilient livelihoods.
People living in countries with conﬂict are especially marginalised in numerous ways. Hunger and
starva on have o en been used as poli cal weapons of war, and con nue to be used as such today,
through inten onal destruc on of food systems, especially agricultural lands (e.g., Sudan, Ukraine and
Pales ne), withholding/restric ng food aid (e.g., Yemen and Pales ne), and unilateral coercive
measures (e.g., Sudan, Venezuela and Syria). The impact of these conﬂicts spread across the whole food
system, aﬀec ng the availability and price of food and other resources (e.g., fer lizer), regionally and
globally, though par cularly amongst the rural and those in poverty.P60 Thus, conﬂicts and war lead to
further marginalisa on and directly or indirectly cause hunger, food insecurity, and famines
undermining the right to food for all,P61 with worse eﬀects on people in poverty, minority groups,
women, and children.
These characteris cs are not exhaus ve as marginalisa on can manifest diﬀerently across and within
regions/countries. Furthermore, the opportuni es people have access to o en diﬀer based on the
intersec on of key characteris cs, such as wealth or poverty with gender, geographic loca on, age, and
ethnicity. For example, in middle-income countries (MIC) women tend to be more food-insecure than men
(Appendix 3, Figure A3.1). Yet, women appear to have greater dietary quality than men, especially in
higher income countries (Appendix 3, Figure A3.1). Meanwhile poverty is increasingly urbanising at a
global scale, with the urban poor lacking access to healthy food, pa erns that emerge in part due to a
changing climate and the migra on of the rural poor to urban areas.P62
An intersec onal lens (i.e. paying a en on to mul ple injus ces and aspects of iden ty that intersect
to exacerbate the experience of marginalisa on) is therefore key to iden fying the speciﬁc
vulnerabili es aﬀec ng marginalised people.P63 This requires suﬃcient levels of data disaggrega on
across contexts, as well as recogni on of alterna ve knowledge systems, especially Indigenous
knowledge.P64 Yet, data remains skewed towards par cular social groups and geographic regions. For
example, evidence from Canada and the US suggests that migrant workers face poor and dangerous
working condi ons and food insecurity, but literature on migrant workers in other regions, especially
LMICs, is limited.P65 Similarly, some geographies, such as the Middle East, are persistently
underrepresented in English published literature.

Social Foundations

1322

1328

We opera onalise our jus ce analysis through the lens of social founda ons which require basic human
rights, such as food, water, health, and energy, to be met for everyone. 267,268 Previous work has focused
on deﬁning the minimum resources required to meet people's human rights, to avoid resource
depriva ons and harms.33,268 However, the availability of resources alone is insuﬃcient to avoid these
harms.269 Therefore, we instead focus on the condi ons that enable people’s human rights to be met. We
specify condi ons (i.e., social founda ons) that would help enable each of the three rights (food, decent
work, healthy environment) to be realized (Table 3). Through a combina on of expert consulta on and a
53

1329
1330
1331
1332
1333
1334
1335
1336
1337
1338
1339
1340

review of the literature, we ﬁrst iden fy the individual and cross-cu ng food system components relevant
to each human right. Next, and drawing on the outcomes from a recently completed mul -stage, mul stakeholder evalua on of food systems indicators,29 we iden fy a suite of candidate indicators for each
component and screen for coverage, scale, relevance, and overlap. Where indicators for key food system
components were not available (e.g., individual and collec ve agency) we reviewed the literature to
propose addi onal indicators for development and monitoring.270 The ﬁnal selec on included a limited
set of indicators that conveyed discrete dimensions of the condi ons that enable people’s rights to be
met, rather than outcomes of these rights being (un)met, which act as our proxy variables. (Appendix 3).
For each proxy variable, we specify the level at which the social founda on would be considered met,
es mate the number of people falling below each social founda on globally, and examine the extent of
harms associated with each of the three human rights not being realized (Table 3, Figure A3).

The Right to Food
The right to food involves the right to a secure and stable supply of suﬃcient quan es of aﬀordable,
healthy, culturally appropriate and sustainably produced food that considers peoples’ prac ces and
customs, to ensure all forms of malnutri on can be avoided, and a digniﬁed life supported.271,272 The right
to food has been interpreted not only as a right to have access to suﬃcient food to enable a healthy diet
but also as placing responsibili es on states to promote good nutri on and health, for example through
regula ng food adver sing, labelling requirements and other measures that shape food environments to
encourage healthier diets.271,273 We thus iden fy two social founda ons linked to the right to food, namely
healthy aﬀordable diets and food environments that do not undermine people’s capaci es to make
healthy choices, which we refer to as “healthy food environments.”
Healthy diets are considered, by the FAO, to be aﬀordable when they cost less than 52% of average
household income.274 We therefore align our minimum condi on/social founda on for aﬀordable healthy
diets with the FAO’s deﬁni on of an aﬀordable healthy diet (Table 3). In 2022, 2.8 billion people globally
were below the social founda on of an aﬀordable healthy diet. This causes diet-related harms that extend
beyond their immediate impacts to damage economies and increase risk of conﬂict and disease. Indeed,
in the same year 2.3 billion people were food insecure and 1.8 billion had no access to clean waterstrongly linked condi ons to poor food safety and nutrient absorp on (Table 3, Figure 7).
The extent to which regions fall below these foundations, and the severity of the associated harms, differ
across geography and social group (i.e., ethnicity, gender, and wealth). While the rapid globalisa on of
the food system through trade and subsidies has made more food available globally and brought food
prices down, it has also suppressed wages and the prices producers receive, leaving healthy diets least
aﬀordable in Low-Income Countries (LIC) and Lower Middle-Income Countries (LMIC), where food
insecurity, low-birth weights, and anaemia are also most prevalent (Figure 7, Figure A3.1). It is also these
contexts where most food system workers live, leaving them o en least able to aﬀord a healthy diet.
Although jus ce requires that healthy food becomes more aﬀordable and available, this will not
necessarily result in healthier diets.4,275 As economies develop and family incomes grow, healthy diets
become more aﬀordable but ultra-processed foods, animal-sourced red meats, and sugar sweetened
beverages also become more available and exposure to unhealthy food adver sing increases.275–277 As a
result, diet quality globally is lowest in Upper Middle-Income Countries (UMIC) and High-Income Countries
(HIC), par cularly among men, while rates of overweight and obesity are highest in these same regions,
par cularly among women (Figure A3.1). Furthermore, while healthy diets are more aﬀordable in HIC,
they remain unaﬀordable or inaccessible to many people, resul ng in lower diet quality and higher obesity
rates for those on low incomes.278,279 In contrast, in UMIC such as places in La n America, higher income
groups, despite being food secure, have a poorer diet quality than low-income groups, likely due to the
hyper-availability of unhealthy foods.280 Rapidly changing food environments and unequal access to
housing, water, health and market services mean LMIC and UMIC, par cularly in urban and peri-urban
57

areas, are now epicentres of mul ple forms of malnutri on and associated outcomes (Figure A3.1).4,281
These regions are also where most of the global popula on currently lives, and where most future
popula on growth will occur, underscoring the cri cal global importance of healthy diets being available
and aﬀordable, in a food environment that is conducive to healthy choices. 25
Food environments are the physical, economic, political, socio-cultural and digital contexts in which
consumers engage with the food system to make decisions about acquiring, preparing and consuming
food.282 Food environments and food choices are increasingly shaped by adver sing and commercial
marke ng, thus expenditure on marke ng of unhealthy food would be an apt proxy variable for healthy
food environments. However, global data on this exposure is lacking. As an alterna ve proxy indicator for
an unhealthy food environment, we use intake of sugar from sugar sweetened beverages (SSB), which is
associated with cardiometabolic disease and social inequities.283 We align our minimum condition for a
healthy food environment with the WHO’s recommenda on of less than 10% of total energy intake from
free or added sugars (ideally reduced to less than 5%, as used in the PHD).108 In 2018, 5.6 billion people
globally, were below the minimum condi on/social founda on of a healthy food environment, with their
intake of free or added sugar exceeding this level. These pa erns are associated with over 75,000 early
deaths, 2.5 billion adults, and 360 million children and adolescents who are overweight or living with
obesity (Table 7, A3).
Food environments determine food choices in a range of ways depending on context, and considering the
aﬀordability, availability, accessibility or convenience, and desirability of foods in the food environment,
especially for healthy food versus unhealthy food.284 Thus, while higher incomes can promote greater diet
diversity, the type of food environment can weaken or strengthen this rela onship.284 In the face of rising
corporate concentra on and marke ng of unhealthy food, territorial and local markets, with a focus on
small-scale and informal actors, are seen to have the poten al not just to revitalise and stabilise local
produc on but also enhance healthy consump on choices for poor people.281 This becomes cri cally
important where rapid urbanisa on is changing food environments in UMIC and LMIC, crea ng food voids
(environments ‘lacking in food’), food deserts (areas where sufficient healthy food is inaccessible)282 and
food swamps (areas where unhealthy, usually ultra-processed, foods285 are abundantly available,
accessible, and affordable),282 earlier seen only in HIC (HLPE Report 19). In each of these cases, people are
at higher risk of food insecurity, micronutrient deficiencies, overweight/obesity, and NCDs, 281,282,286,287 but
for different reasons. Low diet quality could be due to lack of availability, accessibility, and/or affordability
of healthy foods, or because unhealthy foods are abundantly available, affordable, and convenient.282
Changes in food environments have also accompanied changes in food cultures. The shift to unhealthy
diets is characterised by a loss of traditional foods and food deskilling – a loss of cooking skills and
knowledge.275,288 Changes in food cultures can be explained by several factors. First, time poverty has
increased especially for families due to greater women’s participation (who largely remain in charge of
food provisioning despite improvements in gender equality) in the labour force and generally low food
involvement of fathers.289,290 Second, the loss of local biodiversity, notably cultivated and foraged species,
and resource access have impacted healthy food provision in some regions, especially amongst Indigenous
communities.291 Children are especially susceptible to unhealthy food advertising, leading to unhealthy
58

1447
1448
1449
1450

food habits, which can persist to adulthood. Shifts to unhealthy diets can involve moving food culture
away from traditional foods, including family meals. These changes have implications for diets and health,
but equally for the acquisition of food knowledge and skills, especially for children. 293

The Right to a Healthy Environment
The right to a healthy environment rests on the recogni on that realizing the right to food and many other
rights requires having a clean, healthy and sustainable environment. This right is understood as
encompassing several substan ve rights, including to a safe climate and non-toxic environment, which
we adopt as two social founda ons of this right.294 Climate conditions habitable for humans have been
broadly characterised as land with a mean annual temperature of ≤ 29 C°, 297,298 and we use this as our
minimum condition for a safe climate. By 2070, 419 million people globally will fall below the social
founda on of a safe climate, and already in 202233 high temperatures accounted for the loss of 14 million
DALYs6 (Figure 7, Table 3, Figure A3.2). Climate impacts are and will continue to be experienced
inequitably, intersecting with poverty and geography to disproportionately impact certain peoples and
regions. Despite being the least responsible, climate change will exacerbate experiences of food insecurity
for many LICs across Africa and for people living in poverty.299,300 Meanwhile, women, particularly in rural
areas, LMICs and Indigenous communities, and agricultural producers and workers are more likely to have
their livelihoods undermined by climate change, and experience physical injury and ill-health. 296,301–303
More generally, within food systems, women, children, Indigenous and rural communi es, and those in
poverty, because of their marginaliza on, are most impacted by unsafe and unhealthy
environments,295,296 underscoring the importance of human rights for all.
A wide range of food system pollutants exist that create toxic environments and have nega ve human
health eﬀects (Panel 3).33 We focus on only nitrogen leaching to groundwater, 75% of which comes from
agriculture,33 due to data availability. Nitrogen pollution is connected to adverse reproductive effects, and
cancer in humans.33,167 WHO guidelines specify a safe level of nitrogen for drinking water as 50 mg
NO3−/litre,304 thus defining our minimum condition for healthy environments. In 2022, 5 billion people
globally fell below this social founda on and were exposed to unsafe levels of nitrogen leaching, which
was associated with the loss of 42 billion DALYs (Figure 7, Table 3, Figure A3.2) and 4 million new cases
of paediatric cancer a year.6,33,167,304 As with the right to food, these harms are not distributed equally with
rural dwellers most likely to be affected by agricultural pollution305,306 and with the poorest regions of the
world particularly across Africa, South Asia, and China experiencing the greatest number of DALYs due to
unsafe water, with as few as 10% of rural populations having access to clean water (Figure A3.2).

The Right to Decent Work
The right to decent work15,307 entails the right of everyone to live in dignity,13,307,308 to work in just and
favourable condi ons, to be free to choose their work with a salary that allows them to live and support
a family, and to receive equal pay. We iden fy two social founda ons linked to the right to decent work,
namely a living wage and meaningful representa on. A living wage conveys the minimum level of
renumera on necessary to support an acceptable standard of living for the worker and their family. 307,308
Although not always put into eﬀect, a legal minimum wage has been set in 90% of the 187 ILO countries.
59

It equates to an average of 67% of the median wage in developing and 55% in developed na ons,13 which
we use as the social founda on of a living wage. In 2022, an es mated 34% of the food system workforce,
including agricultural (agriculture, forestry, ﬁshing) and non-agricultural, formal and informal workers,
was below the social founda on of a living wage, women earned 50% of what men did, and 159 million
children were involved in child labour, predominantly in agriculture (Table 3, Figure 7, Figure A3.3).309,310
However, trade-oﬀs exist and policies must balance the compe ng need of ensuring healthy food is
aﬀordable while providing adequate and liveable renumera ons (Sec on 5).
Women’s work in agriculture remains undervalued and often seen as an extension of domestic chores. 311
Women are often paid by the piece instead of hourly rates like men, and their wages are sometimes given
to male relatives, limiting their economic independence.312 If gendered wage gaps in agricultural labour
remain unaddressed, condi ons will likely worsen because of the ongoing “feminisation of agriculture” in
all regions other than HICs.313 This is due to men leaving for non-farm work in sectors such as construction
to obtain more stable and lucrative livelihoods, inﬂa ng the presence of women in a low-paid sector.314
Furthermore, sexual harassment due to power imbalances between female farmworkers and employers
remains pervasive (for example, studies have reported incidences of 24-93% in the US), with few
opportunities for reporting due to stigma and risk of retaliation.312 However, food system injustices are
not limited to women as male migrant farm workers, for example in the US, also face abuse and
exploitation.312 Jus ce and the right to decent work require living wages for all food systems workers,
regardless of gender and other forms of marginalisa on.
Collec ve bargaining is one way to enable the meaningful representa on of both workers and employers
and ensure opportuni es to regulate the condi ons of work are present.316 The right to collec ve
bargaining is a fundamental ILO conven on (No 98)317 and an enabling right that paves the way for
inclusive and eﬀec ve governance of work, ensuring decent wages and working condi ons, and social
protec on.316 Collec ve bargaining coverage varies considerably around the world, but when present
facilitates equality, contributes to safe and healthy work places, and builds resilience. We set our social
founda on for meaningful par cipa on at a collec ve bargaining coverage rate of 72% of employees,
which although below the EU’s threshold of 80%, 318 is equivalent to that found by the ILO in mul employer se ngs, that tend to be more successful. Between 2008-2020, 2.6 billion people globally were
below the social founda on of meaningful representa on, with no access to collec ve bargaining (Figure
7, Table 3).319
Collective bargaining is highest in HICs, reaching close to 100% in some countries (e.g., Italy, France), but
low in LMICs, where it ranges from 0%-40% and can be close to 1% (e.g., in Malaysia and Paraguay). 316 The
majority (69%) of the approximately 1.02 billion people employed in food systems (Figure A3.3),320 who
support an es mated 3.8 billion people,310 are found in LMICs (i.e., Asia or Africa). Most of these jobs are
informal, seasonal, unregulated, and unmonitored, with workers living in poverty, reﬂec ng the low levels
of representa on. Average agricultural wages are near or below na onal minimum wages (Figure A3.3),13
leaving food system workers likely one of the groups least able to aﬀord a healthy diet. Yet, in these
contexts, agricultural livelihoods account for over 50% of employment (Figure A3.3) underscoring the
considerable and cascading importance of meaningful representa on for these workers.
