Despite generally high levels of collective bargaining in HIC, recent shifts in food systems towards more
concentrated systems and specialized units of production (e.g., intensive production of a single crop or
species with high technological inputs such as salmon or chicken) has undermined worker participation
resulting in poor labour standards and animal welfare.315 This is partly due to informal contracts being the
norm among small and medium enterprises in LMICs and some HICs.321,322 There is evidence that informal
enterprises are highly organized and streamlined, yet the labour conditions can be exploitative, including
exploitation of child labour.323,324 Forced labour is prevalent on land and at sea, not only in LMICs but also
in some HIC, including the US food supply chain.325 Therefore, a just food system would include food
systems workers (especially smallholders) earning a living wage, with access to collective bargaining and
the ability to advocate for their rights and working conditions.

Individual and Collec ve Agency
Agency, people’s capacity to make choices that shape their own circumstances and to par cipate in
broader societal decision-making processes, has been recognized as an important contributor to food
security and nutri on, secure livelihoods, decent work, healthy environments, and to addressing wider
inequi es within food systems. Meanwhile, ample evidence exists on how people’s freedoms and agency
are curtailed within current food systems.296,326,327 We thus recognise individual and collec ve agency as
not just important in themselves, but speciﬁc forms of agency are also, arguably, guaranteed by human
rights. Human rights emphasise the importance of rightsholders par cipa ng in decisions that aﬀect
them, and human rights trea es and other instruments include many rights guaranteeing civil and poli cal
freedoms, and speciﬁc forms of choice or control for individuals and groups. Human rights is commi ed
to elimina ng discrimina on. Taken together, these components of human rights embody a broad
commitment to individual and collec ve agency. Recognising that it is not possible to fully quan fy
agency, and that it remains challenging to iden fy proxy variables that capture agency in this capacious
sense, we propose three social founda ons and related proxy variables that capture three facets of
agency: non-discrimina on, freedom from corporate control, and freedom of voice and expression.270,328–
330

Non-Discrimina on
People’s agency – their capacity to make choices and par cipate in decision-making -- can be constrained
by structural inequali es shaped by social posi on and iden ty, par cularly class and gender. Ensuring
that all people have agency thus requires addressing discrimina on in all its forms, including inequali es
in access to key resources and services, unjust power diﬀeren als between diﬀerent groups and
inten onal discrimina on. As a proxy variable for non-discrimina on, we use the Social Ins tu ons and
Gender Index which measures discrimina on against women, though this indicator is par al given the
presence of discrimina on against many dis nct groups.
Discrimina on against women is prevalent globally, although highest in South Asia, the Middle East &
North Africa, and across many parts of sub-Saharan Africa (Figure A3.4). In no country in the world are
women afforded the same opportunities as men that allow them equal representation in government or
in the workforce more broadly, undermining their access to decent work and healthy food. 331 Agency is

therefore about inequality and choice; what ma ers is the “inequali es in people’s capacity to make

Freedom from corporate control
Highly concentrated market structures, where just a few ﬁrms dominate key steps along food supply
chains,332 can signal power imbalances where dominant ﬁrms, due to weakened compe on, can exercise
market power that enables them to generate excess proﬁts14,333,334 The exercise of market power by large
ﬁrms can in turn undermine the agency within food systems, especially for the most marginalized people.
Most economists consider that the likelihood of ﬁrms exercising market power increases where the top
four ﬁrms control over 40% of the market.334 Although other metrics are also o en used, due to data
limita on we focus on CR4, for which data is more available, and specify 40% as our minimum social
founda on for freedom from corporate control, CR4 above 40% would indicate people’s agency is at risk
of being curtailed and thus requires further inves ga on (Table 3).
Strong evidence has shown a very high degree of corporate concentra on all along food supply chains,
with just a few large ﬁrms domina ng interna onal markets for agricultural inputs (seeds and fer lisers),
agricultural commodity trade (soybean, poultry), certain processed and packaged foods (biscuits,
confec onary, so drinks) and na onal markets for food retail.14 Evidence suggests that access to market
power is extensive in food systems, allowing dominant ﬁrms to set prices and generate excess proﬁts. 14,17
Concentrated ﬁrms’ capacity to raise prices for seeds, fer lizer, grain, or packaged foods, has an impact
on people’s access to food, inﬂuencing which foods they can and cannot consume. Dominant ﬁrms that
source from smaller scale food producers have a degree of ‘buyer power’, meaning they can dictate the
prices they are willing to pay to producers and other suppliers, such as workers, thus directly impac ng
suppliers’ incomes and ability to access food.17
Large ﬁrms and concentrated commercial ac vity also shape broader condi ons within food systems
through business decisions. Corporate strategies to increase the level of processing of packaged
convenience foods, for example, have widespread impacts on both food produc on and consump on
environments, and can encourage people to eat less healthy foods because of limited product choices and
manipula ve adver sing and packaging.17,335 Business decisions of dominant ﬁrms have a
dispropor onate eﬀect on agricultural produc on technologies, labour condi ons for food systems
workers, including in some cases forced labour,336 and environmental prac ces,337 which directly impact
producers’ and consumers’ op ons. Large and dominant ﬁrms in concentrated markets can inﬂuence
policy and governance processes in ways that inﬂuence democra c par cipa on in food systems
governance, which can directly undermine agency.14 This inﬂuence is through direct lobbying as well as
indirect means including sponsorship of research and revolving door appointments between industry and
government, inﬂuencing measures such as dietary guidelines and regula ons within food systems,338,339
o en crowding out ci zen voices in the policy process.
Measuring the impact of corporate power on human agency in food systems is not straigh orward, in
large part because well-established metrics for agency do not exist, 330 and ﬁrms’ market share data and
other indicators like ﬁrms’ marginal proﬁts and markups (the amount ﬁrms charge over and above their

costs) are not always publicly available. Corporate concentra on (as measured by CR4) and proﬁt data

However, food systems market data, at both global and na onal scales is o en unavailable or behind
prohibi ve paywalls presen ng challenges for assessment of these dynamics. We therefore underscore
the need for companies to make proﬁt and markup data available, enabling the tracking of an indicator of
market power.
Although this approach would beneﬁt from more transparent data repor ng from ﬁrms, these indicators
highlight markets where dominant ﬁrms are more likely to inﬂuence food systems in ways that can
constrain people’s agency. Stronger policies are needed at the na onal and interna onal levels to prevent
corporate concentra on from subver ng agency through market and other forms of power available to
large and dominant ﬁrms. More robust compe on policies, for example, can help in preven ng excessive
market power and ensure prices remain fair for both buyers and sellers.17,340
Freedom of expression and par cipa on in decision-making
We focus on freedom of expression as an important facet of individual and collec ve agency, because it
allows ci zens and governments to be be er informed, enables civil society to hold corpora ons to
account, and helps to sustain a range of other human rights 16 However, in many parts of the world, most
notably in parts of the Middle East, North Africa, Eastern Europe, and Asia, freedom of expression is
signiﬁcantly curtailed, which in turn undermines agency (Figure A3.4). Although indicators can never fully
capture complex concepts, as policies and laws are mediated by social context, the V-Dem freedom of
expression index captures the extent to which a government respects freedom of press and media,
freedom of people to discuss poli cal ma ers at home or in public, as well as the freedom of academic
and cultural expression. Countries with laws upholding the freedom of expression, may curtail freedom in
prac ce and vice versa, we nevertheless draw on this as a preliminary indicator in the absence of more
nuanced data.
When people have agency to par cipate in food systems governance and decision-making in meaningful
ways, they can shape their food systems to make them more just. Mul ple possibili es for civil society
and ci zen engagement exist in democracies, including on ma ers related to food systems, such as
elec ons, public debates, and consulta ons on keys laws and policies.4 However, where these
opportuni es to engage are lacking due to discrimina on, exclusion or in many cases, lack of freedom of
expression (table 3), people are more likely to be marginalised and exposed to harm, and may take ac on
through protests or other forms of movements.15
With the growing concentration of power - financial, technological, economic, and political – across many
food systems, citizen agency is increasingly claimed or manifested through diverse forms of food activism.
These forms of food ac vism span rights-based movements for justice, reactive street protests, alternative
food systems governance structures, initiatives safeguarding the quality and cultural significance of food,
and other modes of individual and collective advocacy.341 Movements for food jus ce and protests o en
involve marginalised groups – farmers, women, Indigenous and rural peoples – and are mo vated by
concerns about food and nutri on security, prices and aﬀordability, and livelihoods, and seek the

more ambi ous state policies to address climate change or food systems transforma ons.342 Nevertheless
ci zens’ freedom of expression and other aspects of agency remain central to addressing structural
inequali es and discrimina on within the food system.341,343

A Safe and Just Space

1676
1677
1678
1679
1680
1681
1682
1683
1684
1685
1686
1687

A just transforma on should look to keep everyone within a safe and just space, below the boundary that
provides planetary stability, and above the founda on that supports jus ce (shown in green in Figure 8).
However, the social harms associated with falling below the social founda ons deﬁned here are not evenly
distributed, leaving far too many people with unmet human rights (to the le of the dashed ver cal line
in Figure 8). Meanwhile, people are not equally responsible for the extent to which the food system
boundaries are already transgressed; far too many people’s dietary habits, if adopted globally, would
cause the global food system to exceed the planetary boundaries (e.g. dietary pa erns of countries that
lie above the dashed horizontal line in Figure 8). Moving the global population to a safe and just space
requires everyone to take reasonable responsibility for keeping the food systems within planetary
boundaries, by minimising the pressures they exert on the planet and suppor ng eﬀorts to ensure the
beneﬁts from food systems are distributed fairly and everyone's human rights are met (Figure 8).

1688
1689
1690
1691
1692
1693
1694
1695

Decisions regarding what constitutes ‘reasonable responsibility’ for keeping the food systems within
planetary boundaries involve considerations of fair allocation. These decisions should be reached through
deliberative processes that take current capabilities and past responsibilities into account, including
where lasting legacies (e.g. colonial histories) continue to produce unjust outcomes for many marginalised
communities. These processes can draw on existing principles and mechanisms that support equitable
burden-sharing in international processes and negotiations (e.g. climate change and trade), such as
common but differentiated responsibilities and differentiated capabilities.344

The nature of state response to ci zen agency varies, ranging from neglect or repression to concessions
and even policy change, and widespread ci zen consulta on in processes of decision-making cannot be
taken for granted.341 States appear responsive to movements for food justice, o en to avoid poli cal
conﬂict or public cri que, though at mes also driven by the global push for sustainability.342 Diﬀerent
forms of ci zen agency and their associated state responses give voice to diverse groups of stakeholders
aﬀected by food system changes and are key to representa onal jus ce.341
In sum, agency is a social founda on for the rights to food, decent work and a healthy environment, and
is independently important from a jus ce perspec ve. Freedom of expression and other civil and poli cal
freedoms support agency within food systems, while corporate concentra on and discrimina on
undermine it. When people lack agency, they may assert it with protests and other forms of ac vism. For
all these reasons, it is important to try and characterize, quan fy and track agency.

Here, we apply the simplest principle as a star ng point, which assumes an equal share of the planetary
boundaries but makes no accommodation for differentiated capabilities or needs (e.g. more efficient
technologies).344 For each country, and for five planetary boundaries where combined data is available
(GHG emissions, land use, water use, nitrogen, phosphorus), we calculate if and how the food systems
share of the planetary boundary values would be exceeded if the dietary consumption patterns of that
one nation were to be adopted globally. We contrasted this with es mates of a country’s distance from
three social founda ons, which had the most comprehensive data (diet aﬀordability, agricultural wages,
and nitrogen leaching) (Appendix 3). We used Kobe plots to visualize associations between the social
foundations and planetary boundaries.
On average 3.7 billion people, predominantly in low-income and lower-middle countries in Sub Saharan
Africa and South Asia do not beneﬁt from the social foundations (countries whose foundation <100). In
these contexts, key barriers limit ci zens abili es to meet their human rights. At the same me, 6.9 billion
people, from all income groupings, lie above the planetary boundaries (countries with average boundary
>100%) (Figure 8). These are countries whose dietary pa erns, if adopted globally, would transgress all
planetary boundaries. Therefore, policies that look to secure social founda ons, must simultaneously
ensure that changes to dietary pa erns will not put dispropor onate pressure on planetary boundaries.
Meanwhile only 115 million people are currently in the safe and just space. Eﬀorts to support jus ce have
not succeeded in protec ng the planet, and conversely condi ons that support a safe planet, do not
necessarily guarantee jus ce. Policies are therefore needed to simultaneously protect the planet and
address jus ce, because currently, almost every country in the world is outside (or on the edge) of the
Safe and Just corridor, with places least responsible for destabilising the planet falling the furthest below
the social founda ons. Similarly, those furthest above the planetary boundaries tend also to be the
wealthiest, with the diets of the top third of the global popula on, from High-Income and Upper Middleincome na ons, amoun ng to over 70% of the food system share of the planetary pressure.

Sec on 4: Assessing poten al environmental and socioeconomic
consequences of a food systems transforma on
Exploring what food systems that are more aligned with health, environmental, and justice objectives
could look like, requires assessing the potential consequences of how food systems would be
restructured by dietary change, increased productivity, and reduced food loss and waste (FLW). We
use two complementary modelling approaches for that purpose.
First, we assembled a multi-model ensemble of ten global economic models used in high-level
assessments of climate change, land use and food security345–350 and involved in the Agricultural Model
Intercomparison and Improvement Project (AgMIP).350–352 The ensemble of economic models includes
a mix of par al equilibrium models (agricultural sector only), with more detailed biophysical and
sectoral representa on, and general equilibrium models (whole of economy), which can simulate
interac ons across non-agricultural sectors (e.g., energy). All simulate important system dynamics that
would drive changes across a transforming food system, including, for example, the ability of producers
to subs tute inputs based on their availability and cost.
Second, we used DIA-GIO (the Global Input-Output module of the Dietary Impact Assessment model),
an updated version of the static input-output food model developed for the 2019 EAT-Lancet
Commission (See modelling description in Appendix 4 for summary of updates).194 This was done to
improve comparability with the modelling methodology used by the 2019 Commission, and
recognizing there is uncertainty to the capacity of economic systems to adapt to rapid large-scale
changes. Delays to a food systems transforma on, for example, would constrain the scope of food
systems to adapt, limi ng the possibility to eﬃciently reallocate resources across alternate uses such
as energy or ﬁbre produc on. Therefore, we used DIA-GIO as an accoun ng tool to scope the
biophysical op on space based on the current and projected supply, demand, and trade rela onships,
but without the eﬀects of economic feedback. This allowed us to consider poten al implica ons of a
food system with constrained capacity for, or dedicated policies (e.g., on land-use changes) that would
regulate, resource realloca on.
There is substantial uncertainty in how food systems would transform, and using a range of models
and modelling approaches allows us to engage with this uncertainty, which we will do by reporting
ensemble median, and where appropriate the full ensemble range (i.e., min to max) as well as
comparing to DIA-GIO. See supplementary materials (Appendix 4) for more detailed documentation
on models, and modelling approaches applied in this analysis.

Defining alternative future food systems

In addition to updating and extending the modelling suite, this analysis has updated the scenario
framework used to assess global and regional changes across food systems. We constructed three core
scenarios described below (Table A4.3).
The business-as-usual scenario (BAU) projects how food systems could look by 2050 assuming a
continuation of current trends and policies. This scenario assumes population and economic growth
based on the “Middle-of-the-Road" scenario (SSP2) of the Shared Socioeconomic Pathways, a scenario

framework designed for the IPCC to model the consequences of climate change in the 21st century.353–
356
This scenario projects global population and GDP to grow by 23% to 9.6 billion people and 127% to
282 trillion US$ between 2020 and 2050 respectively (Figure A4.2, Table A4.9).357–359 To simulate the
consequences of climate change in the BAU, we applied a representa ve concentra on pathway (RCP
7.0) which assumes limited mi ga on eﬀorts and temperature increases of around 2°C by 2050. The
biophysical consequences of this future climate were represented using published estimated changes
in crop yields,360 livestock yields,361 and agricultural labour productivity.362 Following model
parameterization in the 2019 Commission,1,194 DIA-GIO BAU projections to 2050 were informed by
projections from IMPACT363, one of the participating global economic models.
The EL scenario (EAT-Lancet style food systems transformation) starting in 2020 deviates from BAU
along three key dimensions. In EL, we assume that by 2050: (1) Healthy diets (Table 1) are adopted by
all; (2) a 7-10 percent increase on top of global BAU agricultural productivity based on projections
quantified as a part of previous model intercomparisons350,364 for SSP1,350,364 a more sustainable
shared-socioeconomic pathway (Table A4.10); and (3) a halving of BAU food loss and waste (FLW),
consistent with SDG 12.3. Finally, an additional scenario, ELM (EAT-Lancet Mitigation) combines EL
with ambitious mitigation. 347,350 Ambitious mitigation was based on previous modelling efforts to
assess determinants of land-use across SSP projections350 and land-use policies and decarbonization
pathways to achieve 1.5°C warming by the end of the century.347,350 Ambi ous mi ga on includes a
range of policies (emissions pricing and land-use regula ons), which deﬁne market condi ons that
determine the mix of technologies and prac ces (e.g., aﬀoresta on, renewables, and bioenergy)
adopted to maintain carbon budgets. Each economic model has somewhat diﬀerent capacity to model
decarboniza on pathways, see Table A4.17 for a summary of each model's implementa on of
mi ga on. This scenario was run recognising that a transi on towards more sustainable food systems
cannot be achieved without a wider societal transition towards greater sustainability. This scenario
also allowed us to assess potential synergies and trade-offs between a food systems transforma on
and ambi ous economy-wide mi ga on eﬀorts, par cularly through poten al compe on for landuse. While mi ga on eﬀorts would be expected to reduce climate impacts by end-of-century, we keep
BAU climate impacts (RCP 7.0) in ELM, recognising the uncertainty of these eﬀorts transla ng to
reduc ons in climate impacts by 2050.
We emphasise that these scenarios aim to explore end points of a food systems transformation, and
do not explore possible pathways that could lead to these outcomes by 2050. Assessing the implication
of these endpoints is an important first step in understanding the potential benefits and costs of the
changes simulated in these scenarios. Future work is needed to explore the pathways of change under
varying assumptions of global development.

Across the model ensemble, the BAU scenario, compared to 2020, shows a 37% increase in global
agricultural production by 2050 (Figure 9a). Livestock production increases by around 33% and crops
by 37%. All regions see increased overall agricultural production from 2020, with the largest increases
in Sub-Saharan Africa (around 100%) and the smallest increases in China (11% for ensemble, 18% for
DIA-GIO) and Europe (18% for ensemble, 16% for DIA-GIO) (Figure 9b).

Model results suggest that by 2050, the combined effects in an EL scenario could result in total
agricultural production (AGR) near 2020 levels, but with important changes to the composition of
production (Figure 9c). Compared to 2020, livestock production declines by 22% (27% for DIA-GIO).
Driven primarily by reductions in demand for crop production for animal feed which would decline
substantially from BAU 2050 levels. While the models agree on the direction of change (i.e., declining
crop production compared to BAU) there is less agreement on the magnitude of this reduction, with
some models suggesting 2050 crop production would remain above 2020 levels, whereas others
suggesting it could fall slightly below 2020 levels.
A sectoral analysis demonstrates that the largest declines are in ruminant (RUM) production (cattle,
goats, sheep) with a 55% (75% for DIA-GIO) reduction compared to BAU 2050 levels, or a 33% (63% for
DIA-GIO) decline on 2020 levels across the ensemble (Figure 9c, Figure A4.3). This is accompanied by
a decline in global ruminant animal numbers of 26% (result reported by IMPACT and GLOBIOM). Nonruminant production saw a smaller decline of 24% (-46% in DIA-GIO), with larger declines for pork (48% based on three ensemble models reporting, -83% in DIA-GIO) than poultry (-14% for both DIA-GIO
and the mean of two reporting ensemble models) production (Figure A4.4). The economic models
generally show smaller declines in production compared to DIA-GIO, as other sectors (e.g., for pet
food, bone meal) increase their demand for animal sourced foods (See Figure A4.10 for changes in
food, feed, and other demand). EL demand for animal feed declines (-3%) from around 2.5 billion
tonnes in 2020 to 2.4 billion tonnes by 2050. This compares to a 39% increase in feed production to
around 3.5 billion tonnes in BAU.
There is less ensemble model agreement on changes in cereals, but the median results show modest
production declines in rice (-13%), wheat (-4%), and coarse grains (all other grains) (-3%). Sugar crop
production also shows absolute declines (-7% for ensemble, -30% for DIA-GIO) on 2020 levels in EL.
The modelling approaches differ in the projections of vegetables, fruits, nuts and seeds, legumes, roots
and tubers, primarily due to different commodity aggregations methods (VFN in Figure 9). When we
disaggregate the food group, we see much greater agreement between the ensemble and DIA-GIO,
with vegetable production increasing by 42% (48% for DIA-GIO), fruit by 61% (36% for DIA-GIO), nuts
by 172% (155% for DIA-GIO), legumes by 187% (56% for DIA-GIO), with roots and tubers declining by
9% (-42% for DIA-GIO). Differences in legumes can be attributed to ensemble models reporting
soybeans (accounting for the bulk of legume production) as oilseeds (OSD) rather than legumes,
highlighting differences in how different disciplines (i.e., nutrition vs production) analyse food
production and consumption. When we combine OSD and legumes across the ensemble the median
production by 2050 for EL is 1.07 billion tonnes compared to 1.08 billion for DIA-GIO.
Considering the impacts of individual drivers, if we only include improved productivity the median
projec on for produc on across the ensemble is similar to BAU (38% vs 37% for BAU), but the range
of model projec ons is somewhat higher (31%-53% min-max vs 25%-41% in BAU) (Figure A4.5). The
use of global economic models highlights that, in isolation (and without dedicated policies), efficiency
gains may not translate to lower overall production and may even increase it. Reducing FLW or dietary
change in isolation leads to a smaller increase in production of 29% (17% for DIA-GIO) and 15% (18%
for DIA-GIO) respectively compared to 2020 (Figure A4.5). Reduced FLW achieves this by shrinking the
gap between what is consumed and what is purchased, signalling a need for less production to meet

ELM shows larger declines in production compared to EL across livestock sectors, with RUM, NRM, and
DRY declining by 37, 35, and 15 percent compared to 33, 24, and 7 percent respectively. These larger
declines are the consequence of increased production costs from the pricing of emissions. On the other

from a growing bioeconomy (e.g., biofuels, biomass, bioplastics) to facilitate a transition away from
fossil fuels (Figure 9e, Figure A4.10).

Assessing potential impact on Climate, Land, Water, Nitrogen, and Phosphorous
Boundaries
The modelling results show that in BAU, the environmental impact of agricultural production is
projected to increase compared to 2020, where agricultural production is already above the food share
of multiple planetary boundaries, with animal products responsible for the majority of emissions, and
grains for the majority of nitrogen, phosphorous, and water use (Figure 10a). Agricultural emissions
are expected to rise by 33% (20 to 50%; 51% for DIA-GIO) in the model ensemble, primarily driven by
a 22% (18 to 25%) increase in ruminant animal numbers. The model ensemble projects agricultural
crop yields to increase by 24% (17 to 46%), slowing but not preventing agricultural land expansion,
which increases by 4% (1 to 7%; 16% for DIA-GIO) or ~200 Mha, with cropland expanding by 10% (4 to
25%). Resource use for producing crops increases in BAU according to ensemble results, with blue
water use increasing by 13% (-10 to 43%; 1% for DIA-GIO), suggesting uncertainty on future increases
on water-use efficiency (i.e., adoption of irrigation technologies, planting of more water efficient
crops) and rates of expansion of irrigated area to 2050. Nitrogen and phosphorous application
increasing by 41% (22 to 55%; 31% for DIA-GIO) and 41% (36 to 46%; 26% for DIA-GIO) respectively.
BAU results suggest food systems could exceed the resource-use boundaries by more than 100% for
nitrogen-use and by 55-75% for land, water, and phosphorous use (Figure 10b).
All three components of EL (dietary change, increased productivity, reduced FLW) contribute to more
efficient and less resource-intensive food systems by 2050. To achieve the agricultural emissions target
of 5 GtCO2e yr-1 from CH4 and N2O from biological processes, non-CO2 emissions at the farm-gate would
need to decrease by more than 30% from current estimates of 7.1 GtCO 2e yr-1.172 Under EL, agricultural
non-CO2 emissions decline, primarily due to fewer ruminants, with the ensemble median and DIA-GIO
showing a 20% and 32% decline compared to 2020 respectively. However, there is low agreement
across models on whether these reductions are sufficient to meet the food system boundary (–48 to
13% from 2020 across models). All regions show emissions reductions in EL compared to BAU by 2050,
with the largest decreases occurring in Sub-Saharan Africa, Brazil, and the rest of Latin America (Figure
A4.6). When EL is coupled with emissions pricing and land-use policies in ELM, the model ensemble
shows agricultural emissions declining further to 34% (-84 to -18%) of 2020 levels, and below the food
system boundary.
Ensemble results suggest total global agricultural land use (cropland + grazing land) could fall by 7% (26% to 1%) below 2020 levels (decrease of ~340 Mha) in EL, a substantial departure from BAU
projections described above. Most of this decline comes from reductions in grazing land (-11%, -59%
to 1%). Latin America accounts for 30% of the decline in grazing land from 2020 (with Brazil alone

accounting for 16%), while Sub-Saharan Africa accounts for 22%. Such a reduction in future agricultural
land-use could provide room for conservation or restoration of natural ecosystems, particularly in
these regions. The ensemble results suggest an increase in cropland (10%, -3% to 42%) from 2020,
whereas DIA-GIO suggests that cropland area could decrease to 22% below 2020 levels without other
uses of land (Figure A4.9). Coupling ambi ous mitigation with a shift to healthy diets under ELM shows
even greater reductions in future agricultural area, with agricultural land declining globally by 14% (28 to -4%) compared to 2020. This larger reduc on is due to both drivers reducing the demand for and
produc on of animal products, and in par cular ruminant produc on, the main users of grazing land.
Current estimates indicate that global blue water use remains below the food system boundary.
However, regional water scarcity remains a critical concern, and addi onal eﬀorts may be needed to
increase water use eﬃciency and manage water-use, particularly in areas already experiencing water
stress (e.g., Indo-Gange c plains, Mediterranean, etc.). EL lowers water use compared to BAU 2050
levels, but there is low agreement across the ensemble in terms of whether this reduction is sufficient
to fall below 2020 levels (4%, -25 to 39%; -8% for DIA-GIO). Ensemble results suggest similar blue water
use in ELM compared to EL (3%, -40 to 45%), but with results showing a wider range of outcomes.
While EL reduces both N- and P- use compared to BAU, usage levels are s ll projected to increase
compared to 2020 (15%, -17 to 32%; and 15%, 5 to 25%, respec vely), and remain above the planetary
boundaries. DIA-GIO results suggest that under EL, nitrogen and phosphorus inputs could decline
substan ally compared to current levels (-35 and -53%, respec vely) and near or below planetary
boundaries, when demand from non-food sectors does not increase.
There are important complementari es across the three drivers of EL, which in combina on achieved
be er environmental outcomes than any individual driver in isola on.366 Dietary change alone could
substan ally contribute to improvements along land and emissions boundaries, but there is greater
uncertainty on the outcome across water, N- and P-use by 2050, as many commodi es growing in
demand also require these inputs. DIA-GIO showed greater environmental gains from increased
produc vity and reduced FLW, than did the economic models, where rebound eﬀects in demand limit
the environmental gains from these drivers. For example, increased produc vity in ruminant
produc on lowers the cost of ruminant products, which in turn leads to lower consumer prices and
s mulates increased consumer demand. This increase in demand leads to a larger ruminant herd
leading to similar emissions under the increased produc vity scenario compared to BAU (Fig 10b). A
shi to healthy diets would avoid this outcome as it would reduce demand for ruminant products. A
shi to healthy diets, on the other hand, would increase demand for more perishable fruits and
vegetables and could increase the volume of FLW, highligh ng the increased importance of
interven ons to reduce FLW in an EL future.
The range of environmental results (cropland extent, and blue water, N, and P use) highlights
substan al uncertainty in how agri-food systems could respond to a food system transforma on. A
transi on to a healthy diet would include reduc ons in demand for some agricultural commodi es
(e.g., animal-sourced foods, and sugar), accompanied by reduc ons in intermediate demand from
these sectors for inputs (e.g., animal feed). However, this transi on would also increase the demand
for other agricultural commodi es (e.g., fruits, vegetables, legumes, and nuts).

Whether this transi on would lead to a net reduc on in resource use by agriculture depends on a
variety of factors. First is whether resources (cropland, water, fer lizers) dedicated to producing crops
no longer demanded can easily be converted to producing agricultural commodi es that are increasing
in demand. For example, not all cropland dedicated to feed crops would be suitable (agro-clima cally
or economically) to produce fruits, vegetables, legumes, and nuts. Second, are the crops increasing in
demand less resource intensive than those that are declining in demand. While animal-sourced foods
are generally resource intensive, many of the crops increasing in demand can also be rela vely
resource intensive, par cularly with respect to water and fer lizer use. Third, many crops that are
important animal feeds (e.g., cereals and oilseeds) are also inputs to a variety of industrial uses (e.g.,
cosme cs, bioplas cs, biofuels, etc.). Less demand for animal feeds would make these agricultural
commodi es cheaper, which could spur addi onal demand from outside the food system, limi ng
environmental gains. Models with larger reduc ons in resource use (i.e. DIA-GIO), assume limited
rebounds in demand and less compe on for realloca ng resources to producing a healthy diet,
whereas many of the economic models suggest there could be a wider range of poten al uses for
resources that could lead to smaller environmental gains without addi onal economic incen ves (e.g.,
true cost accoun ng) or policies (land-use regula on). Poten al increased compe on for resource
use is more evident in ELM, where there is increased demand for agricultural products from the
bioeconomy (i.e., biofuels, biomass, bioplastics) as a part of transitioning away from fossil fuels, and
land-use policies that restricts land expansion leading to increased agricultural intensification of
existing cropland.
This suggests that the modelled changes could contribute substantially to reducing environmental
pressure across many dimensions compared to remaining on a BAU trajectory. However, there is
uncertainty with respect to reducing environmental impact compared to today or fully remaining
within a safe operating space. Much of this uncertainty depends on how broad the scope is for
alterna ve uses for natural resources and agricultural commodi es, emphasising the need to couple
supply- and demand-side changes to avoid undesired consequences and rebound eﬀects. The model
results present a cri cal founda on for a food system transforma on, even as additional policies and
innovations367,368 that promote improved food system eﬃciency and resource use (e.g., precision ag,
fertilizer management, improved produc on alloca on), further reductions in FLW, applications of
sustainable intensification approaches (Panels 5 and 6 and Appendix 2), or circularity369–371 (Panel 4)
may be required to ensure a complete transi on to a safe opera ng space.

Assessing agricultural labour and livelihood implications
Changes in labour demand can have implica ons for produc on costs and income, as well as ul mately
produc on and total demand.372 Across all scenarios (BAU, EL, ELM), assumed economic development
and increasing urbaniza on leads to an agriculture sector that contributes a smaller share of total
economic output and employment by 2050.373,374 While the structural change in demand suggested by
a dietary change in EL (Figure 9) would have important consequences on which sectors are increasing

or decreasing their demand for labour, globally, we see modest addi onal reduc ons in labour demand
under EL or ELM compared to BAU (Figure 11a, and Appendix 4 Table A4.22). These results are
consistent with recent analyses using current sectoral and regional labour requirements, which found
a shift to a healthy diet would lead to a less labour-intensive global food system.375 All of which
suggests that a shift to a healthy diet would not only be less resource-intensive, as highlighted in the
previous sec on; but also modestly less labour-intensive to produce. The global reduc on in labour
intensity is driven by various factors, including a reduc on in the produc on of animal feeds, and a
reduc on in output from more labour-intensive sectors like industrial pork, poultry, and dairy
produc on oﬀse ng increased labour demand in expanding crop sectors like vegetable, fruits and
nuts.
Livestock sectors would face par cular economic challenges, with the livestock share of total
agricultural value of produc on declining from ~40% in 2020 to around 15% by 2050 in EL and ELM
(Figure 11b). This is due to a 41% decline in global produc on, coupled with a 31% decline in producer
prices for livestock products,376 which contributes to a 36% reduc on in livestock labour demand by
2050 compared to BAU. The increased demand for some plant-based foods, especially vegetables,
fruits, and nuts, would drive increased demand for labour in these expanding sectors, which globally
across all of agriculture oﬀset most of the declines in livestock labour demand. These ensemble results
are broadly consistent with DIA-GIO results, which suggest the shi s in sectoral demand for labour
would result in a modest reduc on in overall agricultural labour demand (<5%) globally.
The agricultural sector contributes a larger share of GDP and employment in lower-income regions (up
to 40%) compared to higher-income regions (<5%).377 Despite a decline in the contribu on of
agriculture to the economy compared to current levels, by 2050 agriculture would remain a relatively
large source of GDP and employment in many regions (e.g., India, and Sub-Saharan Africa). The labour
demands by sector also vary substantially across region and food system. As such, the challenges of
restructuring agriculture and food sectors would vary regionally.378 The restructuring of food systems
could be relatively more challenging in regions with large agricultural labour forces (e.g., India, SubSaharan Africa, and China), or large livestock sectors (e.g., Latin America). Regions with larger
vegetable, fruit and nut sectors (e.g., China), may benefit from changes in demand without the need
for substantial reallocation of resources across agricultural sectors. Figure 11 shows the size of
agricultural labour force by region (panel a) and the relative importance of the livestock sector and the
VFN sectors (panel b) to highlight regions where restructuring of labour demand could present greater
challenges.

An EAT-Lancet aligned food systems transforma on would ‘mark a paradigm shi in our rela onship
with farmed animals’,P66 with a considerably transformed and smaller livestock system globally

we es mate that the producer animal numbers would decline globally to 1.1 billion (-26%)
ruminants for meat (ca le, sheep, goats), 785 million (-4%) dairy animals, 66 billion (-19%) nonruminants (of which >95% poultry, <5% pigs). Conversely, ﬁsh produc on would increase to 220
million tonnes (+46% on 2020 levels). From the perspec ve of value of produc on, we see an
absolute contrac on from 2020 levels, with a 43% decline across the global terrestrial livestock
sector (-US$650 billion) by 2050. Propor onally this is more marked for ruminant meat (-71%), than
non-ruminants (-46%) or dairy (-20%). It also represents a 50% decline in terrestrial livestock sector
employment by 2050, no ng that in a BAU scenario we see a decline of around 25%.

Change in livestock animal numbers indicate reduc ons in CH4 and N2O emissions (-22% versus
2020), and grazing land (-11% versus 2020, ~340Mha). Further improvements and innova ons in
prac ces can and should be developed and applied to further reduce emissions and both increase
and capture environmental beneﬁts of livestock produc on. For example, some of the methane
emissions linked to ruminant management can poten ally be mi gated in the futureP68 (Figure 5).

77

The meat and dairy sectors are the most impacted by shi s to healthy diets, which must cri cally
take into account livelihoods, culture, and power dynamics. While we recognise the conten ous and
complex nature of animal-sourced foods produc on and consump on, a smaller livestock sector
creates opportuni es to give greater considera on to animal health and wellbeing, environmental
outcomes, and labour quality. We ﬂag six important opportuni es and considera ons to navigate
this transi on which we recommend considering in combina on, rather than in isola on:
Gains through Eﬃciency and Technology: There are signiﬁcant eﬃciency gains in produc on,
notably in the global south, that can markedly reduce an cipated environmental and health impacts
of produc on. Emissions intensity can be improved so that per unit produc on, less GHG would be
emi ed.P69 Cau on is urged around densely occupied conﬁned systems notably around other
environmental eﬀects such as concentrated nutrient pollu on, and disease risk, over-use of
an microbials, animal health and welfare and labour condi ons. The assumed conserva on gains
of improved eﬃciency are only a ained with complementary policies and ac ons that limit, for
example, con nued expansion into intact lands.
Zero Conversion of Intact Forests, and Grasslands: The addi onal conversion of forests for pasture
or feed systems is antagonis c to achieve both human and environmental goals. O en forgo en are
intact grasslands, which are even more threatened than forest globally. Only 30% of intact
grasslands are remaining, making them the most converted, but also least protected ecosystems
globally.P70
Gains through Nature Posi ve Rangeland Management: While intensive (over)grazing contributes
to land degrada on,P71,P72 integra ng grazing livestock to maintain grasslands is also a possibility for
conserving and restoring grassland ecosystemsP73 including carbon sequestra on. Bai et al. es mate
increases in SOC of 2.3 to 7.3 GtCO2e year−1 for grassland restora on in general, with Improved
grazing management providing 0.15 to 0.7 GTCO2e year−1.P74 Improving grazing management ranges
from complete removal of grazing livestock to diﬀerent ways of reducing grazing intensity,
highligh ng the need to balance the management with the agroecosystem condi ons.P73
Importantly, the produc on of supplementary feeds needs to be internalised into the overall eﬀects
of the livestock produc on systems, due to e.g. deforesta on.P75 In forest biomes, silvo-pastoral
systems present some opportunity to mi gate eﬀects of livestock while contribu ng to produc vity
gains.P76
Gains Through Integra on: Modern agriculture has o en separated animal and plant produc ons
systems. Methods reconnec ng these systems, whether integrated in landscapes or connected
through circular produc on such as insect-based feed oﬀer promise.P77-79 Feeding livestock with
byproducts in circular systems has poten al, but the number of animals that could be reared is
limited by the availability of such feed, resul ng in substan ally lower produc on levels than what
is currently produced.P79
Gains in Animal Welfare: Perspec ves emphasising the welfare and wellbeing of animals in
agriculture have risen due to a growing concern related to intensive, conﬁned, industrialised farming
systems, or ‘factory farming’. For instance high stocking densi es,P81,P82 long transporta on mes,P83
restric ng natural behaviourP84 pose animal welfare concerns, and also extensive systems come with
78

their own challenges.P85 Animal welfare concerns have also been shown to inﬂuence consumer
choice, along with the produce quality, even outranking environmental concerns.P86 Importantly,
discussion on animal welfare and wellbeing should reﬂect current, mul disciplinary knowledge,
bringing together advancing research on animal cogni on and behaviour, veterinary science and
ethics.P87
Non-ruminant Animal Sourced Foods: In some regions beef consump on is ﬂat or declining (e.g.
USA, Europe), largely replaced by poultry and pork produc on. Blue foods, or aqua c foods are
common to many cultures and tradi ons. In general, animal sourced foods will have higher
environmental footprints than plant sourced foods because of lost energy conversion between
trophic levels. This loss is lowest for aqua c and avian animal sourced foods and can be further
mi gated by more circular systems. However, most livestock produc on systems share similar
disease, and pollu on risks at scale, notably in dense highly conﬁned systems and all have concerns
related to animal health and welfare.

Assessing production costs and food expenditure implications
Using various modelling approaches, we observe a smaller and less resource intensive food system
with EL compared to BAU. It is one that would require fewer natural resources (i.e., land and water),
chemical (N and P), and labour inputs. Across the ensemble, these changes could amount to a
moderate reduction in average agricultural producer prices of 8% for EL by 2050 compared to BAU
(Figure 12a). Assuming current input requirements and prices, DIA-GIO projected a similar reduction
in costs (10%). This suggests that transformed food systems by 2050 could produce healthy diets for
all while reducing input costs, natural resource use, as well as environmental pressures compared to
BAU. However, whether this potential is realized will ultimately depend on the transi on pathway (i.e.,
the mix of policies, technologies, and cultural changes) followed to foster an EL compa ble food system
transforma on.
Across food systems, lower production costs could increase average food affordability if passed along
to consumers. That said, it is important to note that lower producer prices can contribute to lower
agricultural wages and income with negative consequences on food affordability, particularly in rural
areas. Ensemble results suggest that average agricultural prices would not change substantially from
2020 levels for both BAU and EL. This contrasts with ELM, where increases in producer prices
contribute to a 10% increase in consumer prices compared to 2020. Food expenditure (reported by
AIM, ENVISAGE, and MAGNET), which is a factor of consumer prices and food demand, is projected to
grow in all three scenarios, but at a slower rate than per capita income, with the share of global income
spent on food falling from 7% to 5%, 4%, and 4% by 2050 for BAU, EL, and ELM respectively (Figure
12b). Globally, changes in food expenditure between BAU and EL are driven by two offsetting shifts in
the consumption bundle. First, a decline in the demand for, and the prices of, relatively more expensive
animal sourced foods. For example, demand for, and consumer prices of, ruminant meat fall by 62%
and 16% respectively compared to 2020, even as ruminant prices remain substantially higher than
prices of all other commodities (Figure 12a). Second, demand for and prices of VFN increase
substantially, which at the global level mostly offset decreased spending on animal products.

While globally, by 2050, these two shifts mostly offset each other, some regions currently consuming
relatively low levels of animal sourced foods (e.g., India), or would require relatively large increases in
VFN demand (e.g., Eastern Europe, Caucasus, and Central Asia) to achieve a healthy diet could see
increased food expenditure in 2050 compared to BAU. By contrast, regions that are currently
overconsuming animal sourced foods (e.g. USA) could see declining food expenditures. This result is
consistent with recent analysis using DIA-GIO that found that for individual consumers today, healthy
diets could already be more affordable than current unhealthy diets in high- and upper middle-income
countries, even as they remain more expensive in low- and lower middle-income countries8.
Even as the share of income spent on food is declining for all regions and scenarios compared to 2020,
the affordability of diverse healthy diets remains a concern. Lower income regions that import a
substantial share of their VFN (e.g., Other Asia), could have additional concerns for increased
vulnerability to international price shocks on domestic food affordability and availability. And while
progress is made, Sub-Saharan Africa continues to spend a larger share of income (21%) on food in EL
than any other region. This suggests lower income regions (bottom of list of regions in Figure 12b)
could remain relatively more vulnerable to food poverty, and that targeted interventions to account
for higher VFN prices and social protections to ensure consumers can afford a healthy diet may be
needed.
These results highlight that a food systems transformation, if achieved, could contribute towards social
and environmental goals. However, the mechanisms for getting there (e.g., taxes, repurposing of
subsidies, incorporation of true cost of food approaches) will be critical to informing and incentivizing
behaviour change towards more sustainable production practices and healthier consumption patterns.
The cost of these actions will ultimately determine whether a food system transformation increases or
decreases the cost of individual foods and the overall diet.

Mitigation efforts (Figure A4.7) could increase the cost of agricultural production by limiting land
supply and pricing emissions. Across the ensemble, mitigation efforts in isolation would increase
consumer prices by 17% (0 to 40%) compared to BAU 2050 prices. This is broadly consistent with past
studies suggesting that mitigation efforts could increase food prices. 349 This increased risk can be
explained by multiple factors that raise the price of producing and consuming food. First, land use and
mitigation policies increase the cost of key agricultural inputs (e.g., land, fertilizers) raising the cost of
production. Second, emissions pricing raises the cost of agricultural products, particularly of emission
intensive commodities like ruminant meat. Vulnerability to changes in food prices varies, with lower
income regions already at higher risk of food insecurity, like Sub-Saharan Africa, the most vulnerable
to price increases. Panel 9 explores how the EL components contribute to changes in producer and
consumer prices, and their potential policy implications.

Low- and middle-income regions contribute the largest reductions in emissions and agricultural landuse in EL and ELM (e.g., China, Brazil, Sub-Saharan Africa), and predominantly are regions where
dietary change risks increasing food prices. These are also the regions that historically have
experienced large changes in land-use or had the least access to novel technologies and affordable
reliable energy. This highlights the importance of increased productivity to reduce agricultural yield
gaps, as well as reducing FLW, both of which could play a critical role in alleviating price pressures
caused by a shift to healthy diets and/or ambitious mitigation. There are potential complementarities
between a shift to a healthy diet and ambitious mitigation, both alleviates pressure on land, and
globally, reduces GHG-intensive foods in the consumption bundle. Further demand-side interventions
could also play a role in managing food affordability concerns. Modelling suggests that targeted food
subsidies bundled with mi ga on policies379,380 or full cost eﬀorts381 could help manage these price
increases, as discussed in the following section.
Panel 9: Understanding the drivers of price changes to inform policy priorities
To assess which scenario drivers (dietary change, increased produc vity, reduced food loss and waste,
and ambi ous mi ga on) were most responsible for changes in agricultural prices we conducted a
decomposi on analysis using four models from the ensemble (AIM, CAPRI, ENVISAGE, and MAGNET)
that reported regional consumer and producer (e.g., farm gate) prices (See Appendix 4 Fig A4.7 for full
ensemble results of producer prices).
Ambi ous mi ga on could increase the cost of produc on in all regions by limi ng agricultural land
expansion and pricing emissions. Increased producer prices translate to higher consumer prices, posing
a risk to food security. Dietary change on the other hand has more varied impact on prices. In Brazil
and the USA, regions with high animal-sourced food consump on, adop on of healthy diets could
reduce average consumer prices by 7% and 4% respec vely (Panel ﬁgure g and h, yellow bar).
However, in India, where there is low red meat consump on, increased demand for vegetables, fruits,
and nuts could contribute to a 10% increase in average consumer prices (Panel ﬁgure e, yellow bar)
Increased produc vity and reduced FLW contribute to more eﬃcient food systems, reducing the cost
of produc on and leading to lower consumer prices in all regions. The poten al to mi gate price
increases is par cularly cri cal in lower income regions with greater food security challenges. For
example, in India and Sub-Saharan Africa reduced FLW and increased produc vity par ally oﬀset the
price increases due to ambi ous mi ga on and dietary change (Panel ﬁgure blue and red bars). FLW
reduc ons contribute more to reduc ons in consumer prices than producer prices, with the biggest
gains in regions with rela vely high consumer waste (e.g., USA Panel ﬁgure h). Broadly, these results
suggest the importance of supply-side interven ons that increase the eﬃciency of food systems,
par cularly in low-income se ngs where price rises pose immediate food security risks.
Previous modellingP88–91 has suggested supply-side measures (investments in resource use eﬃciency,
agricultural R&D, improved extension services), would be insuﬃcient to alleviate food insecurity. As
such, policies and interven ons that improve infrastructure and func oning of markets, as well as
demand-side interven ons, such as price subsidies and poverty allevia ons measures, would be
needed to manage higher diet costs in lower income regions.

Section 5: Solutions and actions to improve health, environmental
sustainability, and justice
A “great food transformation”1 is required if the world is to align with the EAT-Lancet's vision by 2050.
This transformation must operate across multiple leverage points, including profound shifts in the
underlying objec ves of food systems outcomes.382,383 The focus must move beyond simply maximizing
proﬁt and volume in agriculture to achieving food security in ways that priori ze diet quality and
advance health, sustainability, and social justice. Equally essen al is interven ons that change
underlying rules and incen ves of the food system, such as taxes, subsidies, and regulations,382,383 as
these form the condi ons under which deeper shifts in values, norms, and governance can emerge.
This section focuses on these underlying rules and incentives through the lens of eight domain-specific
solutions aimed at advancing health, environment, and/or justice goals simultaneously. For every
solution, we identify two to three actions that the Commission has evaluated, based on synthesis of
exis ng literature, as critical for transforming food systems. These ac ons are grounded in available
scientific evidence demonstra ng their poten al to deliver meaningful impacts across health,
environment, and jus ce outcomes, while also crea ng the enabling environment for more profound
systems change. Figure 13 summarizes these solutions and their associated actions.
Several analyses have shown that individual policies and interventions, on their own, are insufficient
to drive multi-goal transformative change. Instead, bundled or integrated approaches are needed—
for example, to keep global warming below 1.5 degrees384 or to reduce the hidden costs of current
food systems (estimated to be 15 trillion US$/year).9 This is because the drivers and outcomes of food
systems are highly interconnected, interacting in both direct and indirect ways.385 Investing in
coordinated sets or packages of solutions is therefore essen al to steer food systems transformation
in positive directions.
We highlight some of these dynamics in actions 3 and 15-23. The domain-specific actions presented in
this sec on are not isolated; rather, they are deeply interconnected and can produce synergistic eﬀects
across goals, while also naviga ng poten al trade-offs. For example, introducing regulations to limit
land use expansion may promote health and environmental sustainability but could risk undermining
justice if it restricts access for marginalized or indigenous people’s access to that land. However, if such
regulations are implemented alongside efforts that recognise and protect the resource use of these
groups, both sustainability and justice can be enhanced. By implementing actions as part of carefully
designed 'bundles,’ it becomes possible to advance multiple goals simultaneously (see Section 6:
Transformation).

Solu on 1: Shi to healthy diets
To shi diets towards The Planetary Health Diet, it should ideally be more available, aﬀordable,
convenient, aspira onal, and delicious than unhealthy alterna ves. Two sets of feasible interven ons
emerged from a scoping review of systema c reviews.386 Both sets of interven ons focus on food
environments—the physical, economic, poli cal, and sociocultural se ngs in which people engage
with the food system to acquire food387 —most o en considered more cost-eﬀec ve and equitable
than those targe ng individuals or households.387–389 The ﬁrst set includes price-based interven ons,
which are likely to reduce the purchase and consump on of unhealthy foods.390–392 The second set
involves non-price-based interven ons that restrict adver sing and availability of unhealthy foods and
mandate warning labels on them.
Taxes on foods and beverages with high content of added sugar, salt, or saturated fats (or a
combina on thereof) are par cularly impac ul when combined with subsidies on nutri ous foods that
decrease their rela ve price.392,393 Modelling studies suggest op mal price levels for mi ga ng health
and environmental impacts of unhealthy foods and meat that would require calibra on by
context.379,380 By contrast, restric ng adver sing and marke ng of unhealthy foods could shi
popula ons towards healthier foods.394,395 Labelling foods to denote higher energy content and those
high in fat, sugar, and salt decreases purchasing and consump on396,397 using either mandatory frontof-pack warning labels as in several La n American countries or traﬃc light systems as is common in
Europe.398 Reducing the propor on of unhealthy foods that are available in the market in parallel with
increasing the propor on of healthier foods399,400 can reduce purchasing and consump on, as can
reducing por on, package, and serving sizes of energy dense and unhealthier foods. 401,402
Aﬀordability of food is determined by supply and demand side dynamics as well as by purchasing
power.8,287 When making the PHD aﬀordable for all, increasing purchasing power for lower income
consumers is more important than ‘reducing the price’ of foods.403 Social protec on measures (see
also Solu on 8) including, providing nutri ous school meals (Panel 11), public procurement and
distribu on systems, employment guarantee schemes, and government investments in healthpromo ng insurance systems, all have the poten al to contribute to increased aﬀordability for lowincome households. Living wages, universal basic income, income growth, and targeted income
transfers, especially for economically disadvantaged people,404 and more equitable distribu on of the
beneﬁts of na onal economic growth through ﬁscal policies, would also support increased purchasing
power. Previous modelling studies suggest that the rela ve cost of a healthy diet can be substan ally
reduced, even in LMICs, through combina ons of pro-poor income growth, reduced food loss and
waste, and a cost accoun ng that includes the cost of diet related climate and health.8

Solution 2: Protect and promote healthy, traditional diets
In some regions, traditional diets are closely aligned with the principles of the Planetary Health
Diet.147,405,406 While it is important to acknowledge that not all traditional diets are inherently healthy
or sustainable in their current form, they frequently embody a deep connec on to the local
environment, biological diversity, agricultural practices, cultural beliefs, and long-standing adaptations
to available resources.149,407 However, revitalizing traditional diets pose signiﬁcant challenges. Eﬀorts

Various approaches exist that recognise and enhance the cultural, environmental, and nutri onal value
of Indigenous and tradi onal diets. These approaches aim to make such diets more desirable,
accessible, and affordable.148,409 Cultural valorisa on, including the celebration of traditional foods, can
elevate their social status and render them more aspirational.148 For example, the Food and Agriculture
Organiza on’s designa on of the “Interna onal Year of Millets” sought to raise awareness of the

tradi onal, healthy foods into national food-based dietary guidelines and nutrition education

par cularly under-consumed fruits, nuts, legumes and vegetables.415 These species and their wide
range of varie es within species have o en been displaced by dominant staples like wheat and rice.
Yet, they oﬀer poten ally climate resilience and nutri on beneﬁts, and merit renewed a en on.416

Solution 3: Implement sustainable and ecological intensification
In regions characterized by significant yield gaps, policies aimed at increasing productivity through
sustainable and ecological intensification (SEI) practices can increase resource use efficiency by
lowering the environmental impact per unit of production.417 Conversely, in areas where agricultural
productivity is already high, policy efforts should prioritize reducing environmental degradation and
pollution associated with intensive farming systems and practices.
Securing long-term access to land and water resources is essential to enabling farmers to adopt and
implement SEI practices, many of which entail substan al up-front costs and/or require longer time
horizons to yield benefits—particularly when used to rehabilitate or recover exhausted or degraded
lands.418 Access to land is especially cri cal for women, who frequently face additional and entrenched
legal and sociocultural barriers to ownership and control.419 Despite their pivotal role in suppor ng
marginal groups and remote communi es, public advisory (extension) services have suﬀered from
chronic underfunding.420 Revitalizing and redesigning these services, with coordinated support from
public, private and civil society actors, is essen al to assist farmers in transitioning and adopting new
methods towards sustainable prac ces.420 Strategic investments in farmer networks—facilitating
knowledge exchange, equipment sharing, and improved market access—can also lower adoption
barriers. Brazil's success in promo ng diversiﬁed and organic farming through the integra on of farmer
networks with suppor ve public policies offers a compelling model.421

Addressing the structural imbalances between producers and dominant agribusiness corporations is
critical. Strengthening anti-trust legislation and implementing policies to reduce excessive market
concentration can reduce systemic barriers to SEI adoption and practices.421 Robust competition
policies can foster more equitable pricing for producers, support diversified market structures, and
stimulate innovation,14 all while contributing to the resilience of food systems.422,423 In parallel, tighter
regulation of food commodity speculation could stabilise producer incomes and create more
favourable conditions for long-term investments.424
Both public and private sector investments are needed to support the transition to SEI practices,
par cularly in oﬀse ng ini al costs to advance the research and development of innovations and
appropriate technologies.425 Investment in labour-saving technologies can reduce the burden of
otherwise labour-intensive practices, while crop breeding for high-yielding or climate resilient varie es
suited to low chemical input condi ons can improve the productivity and profitability for SEI. Financial
mechanisms such as payments for ecosystem services,426 cost-share payments,421 and similar
instruments could provide crucial support to farmers shifting towards sustainable prac ces.
Historically, most agricultural subsidies have been directed toward a narrow set of staple crops—
wheat, rice, maize, soy (for feed), and dairy.417 To realign agricultural produc on to dietary
recommenda ons that emphasize increased consump on of legumes, nuts, fruits, and vegetables,
subsidies must reform.427 Targeted investment in under-supported foods, alongside research to
improve their yield, resilience, and regional adaptability, is essen al to building more sustainable,
health promo ng agriculture systems.

Solution 4: Halt agricultural conversion of intact ecosystems
The multi-model ensemble results indicate a projected 7% decline in agricultural land by 2050 rela ve
to 2020 driven by dietary shifts, increased agricultural productivity, and reductions in food loss and
waste. However, to limit the expansion of agriculture and aquatic systems into intact systems, a
combination of regulatory policies, community management, and multi-sectoral commitments and
coordina on are necessary. This may include the strict protection of natural ecosystems, and the
suspension or limi ng of logging concessions and commercial fishing licenses in protected areas. It is
essen al that these regulations are implemented in ways that minimise leakage, i.e. ensuring that the
protection of land and water in one region does not inadvertently result in increased exploita on
elsewhere.428 A en on should be given to popula ons inhabi ng or using resources in protected
areas. This includes ensuring meaningful stakeholder engagement and where appropriate,
empowering leadership by Indigenous communi es.429 Recognizing and securing land and sea tenure
rights for these communi es is essen al. Retaining biodiversity intactness can also be compatible with
certain forms of sustainable land/water use, such as wild harvesting, selec ve logging with native
species, or extensive rangeland management that maintains na ve biodiversity. In such cases,
sustainable uses are those that are well below the 10% Human Appropriation of Net Primary
Productivity (Table 2), and where biodiversity intactness index values stay well above 95, thereby
minimizing impact on species composition.157
Both regional policies and voluntary ini a ves in the EU and the UK have introduced zero deforestation
commitments across supply chains.432 While a few region- and commodity-specific commitments have
contributed to small reductions in deforestation,433 the evidence of effectiveness of these efforts is

Solution 6: Secure decent work

Solution 5: Reduce food loss and waste
The ensemble scenario results indicate that halving food loss and waste (FLW), in alignment with SDG
target 12.3, yields moderate benefits across all five modelled Planetary Boundaries. Some level of loss
or waste is inevitable for food safety and reliability of supply. Food losses and waste can be reduced,
so that there is less demand on food produc on, or else recycled into the circular economy (see Panel
4). The ‘Food Waste Pyramid’436 outlines a hierarchy of recycling management options based on
financial and environmental returns: depending on context, the highest priority is usually to feed waste
to livestock (though livestock numbers are reduced in one of the EAT-Lancet scenarios), followed by
industrial uses—including anaerobic diges on for biogas and other products—then compos ng,
incinera on, and ﬁnally, landﬁll disposal.
In lower-income contexts, food loss predominantly occurs at the produc on and post-harvest stages,
whereas in higher-income contexts, consumer-level food waste is the main contributor.437 Reducing
food loss depends on improving storage, transporta on logistics, and access to labour and equipment
to ensure mely harvests and transforma on of crops into food products sought a er by the
consumer. In some cases, shortening supply chains may reduce food loss by reducing the need for
extended storage and distribu on. Circular economy opportunities—such as the produc on of lowcost high-quality animal feed, renewable energy usage, and improved food safety—can provide
demonstrable benefits (see Panel 4).438
To address food waste in households, food service, and retail, education ini a ves, awareness
campaigns, and skill-building remain the primary institutional tools. Technical interven ons focus on
leveraging data and analytics to improve the eﬃciency of food supply chains. To raise effectiveness of
these soft tools, they should be complemented by regulatory measures, taxes and subsidies.439 More
systemic resolution of food waste must confront underlying drivers, ins tu onal lock-ins, and rebound
effects.440 For example, aesthe c standards and preferences (e.g., size, colour, blemishes) imposed by
markets contribute to supply chain losses and consumer waste. Similarly, current food safety laws and
regula ons, combined with exis ng investments in infrastructure, o en favour incineration or landfill
over alterna ve uses such as feeding waste to animals.

There are several measures that can be implemented to ensure that work within food systems is safe,
dignified, and equitable without discrimination based on gender, race, or other forms of difference.
Securing decent work requires, among other ac ons, raising wages for food system workers to living
wage levels4 and promo ng gender equitability across all roles and sectors.441 Current workers may be
affected by the changes in production and consumption patterns that are essen al for food systems
transformation. For example, a reduction in livestock production could impact employment across
local, regional, and national scales, depending on how and where such transi ons occur.442 Therefore,
improving working conditions and preventing exploitation should include more robust implementation

and enforcement of legislation against child labour and forced labour,443 protection from exposure to

for workers to strengthen the enforcement and uptake of such protec ons.

Solution 7: Ensure meaningful voice and representation
Securing meaningful voice and representation is cri cal to ensuring that decision-making processes in
food systems are more democratic and inclusive. Collective bargaining mechanisms, supported and
implemented through unions and civil society organiza ons, can advocate effectively for the rights and
interests of food systems workers and small-scale producers and actors. For policies concerning both
production and consumption, the proactive engagement of diverse coalitions and perspec ves —
including unions, civil society actors, and marginalised populations—can foster more democratic and
participatory decision-making governance.330 Although these processes may be slowed by
disagreements and trade-offs, expert facilitation and the establishment of clear procedural guidelines
can help ensure momentum and constructive progress.330 Importantly, such inclusive platforms can
contribute to improved food systems governance by enabling interaction among actors across sectors
and scales,446 while empowering local institutions and promoting decentralised decision-making
authority.447
Power imbalances similarly affect consumers, who often have limited influence over the terms of food
access, affordability, and quality.423 Ensuring consumer representation through regulatory
mechanisms, participatory food policy councils, and consumer advocacy groups can help rebalance
these asymmetries and strengthen individual and collective agency within food system governance.448
Addi onal measures to democra ze food systems include the alloca on of reserved seats in policy
forums for marginalised groups,449 the ins tu onalisa on of citizens’ assemblies and consultations.450
Eﬀorts to counter corporate inﬂuence and concentra on should involve stricter regula ons on political
donations, mechanisms to mitigate conflicts of interest, and greater transparency in lobbying and the
funding of scientific research.14 Policies to address corporate concentra on should include
strengthened na onal and interna onal compe on frameworks,14 alongside regulatory mechanisms
to iden fy, monitor, and mi gate undue corporate inﬂuence on policy and governance processes.
Deliberate efforts to build inclusive coalitions that include marginalised groups while also increasing
transparency and accountability and expanding access to information can create new spaces for
dialogue.

Solution 8: Recognise and protect marginalised groups
Embedding justice into decision-making processes ensures that the responsibility for advocacy does
not fall dispropor onately on marginalised groups. The implementation of a comprehensive set of
social protection policies can improve access to, and affordability of, healthy and sustainable diets.
Social protection measures—including school meals (Panel 11), unconditional cash transfers,
maternity entitlements, old age pension, and disability benefits—are broad-based interven ons that
directly or indirectly improve food security and nutri on.4,451

Addi onal strategies include targe ng ﬁnancial support for women farmers, crop and livestock
insurance to buﬀer against shocks, subsidies for farm inputs and technologies, and food price
stabilisation mechanisms such as internationally coordinated food storage policies.452 Ensuring that
solutions are co-created with marginalised communi es can help create context-specific solutions that
are socially acceptable.453 Co-creation should be facilitated through improved representation, as
previously discussed, and by engaging directly with aﬀected groups—especially early in the
transformation process.454 This also requires sustained support, including funding for inclusive
initiatives and training programs that build capacities within marginalised popula ons. Finally, NGOs
play a vital role in safeguarding basic human rights for people in conflict-aﬀected areas, and this role
should be supported by multilateral organisations and na onal governments. Interventions may
include establishing safe zones as a short-term measure, rebuilding food supply chains with
interna onal assistance in the medium term, and crea ng dedicated funding mechanisms to prevent
and alleviate famines in conflict areas over the longterm.327
