While none of the solutions explicitly focus on emerging technologies, technological innovation is
relevant across food system transforma on. Emerging technologies—such as artificial intelligence,
cellular agriculture, nanotechnology, and robotics—have the poten al to signiﬁcantly reshape and
disrupt agriculture and food systems.367,455 These innova ons could enhance agricultural productivity
without expanding land use, thereby contribu ng to reduced deforestation and habitat loss. They may
also facilitate precision farming, reduce waste, and optimize the use of key inputs like water and
fertilizers.367
The eﬀec veness of these technologies will depend on balanced and strategic investments that align
their development, deployment, and use with principles of equity, responsible innovation, and
coherent policy frameworks,456,457 as well as the broader transition to renewable energy sources.
Redirecting public and private funding toward near-ready technologies that support the solutions
described in this report will be critical. Comprehensive evaluation of emerging technologies—
considering their impacts across different geographical and cultural contexts—is essen al to ensure
they contribute to just food systems transformations.458 This includes recognizing that technologies
can have both positive and negative effects, many of which remain uncertain—par cularly in the case
of rapidly advancing tools like ar ﬁcial intelligence. To avoid and or mi gate unintended
environmental and social trade-oﬀs, investments should be carefully targeted to ensure broad
accessibility and fair distribu on of the technologies.455,457 Among the most discussed technologies
relevant to dietary shifts are novel alternatives to replacing conven onal meat. Panel 10 explores their
potential implications for health, environment, and justice.
Panel 10: The poten al role of meat alterna ves in mee ng EAT-Lancet targets
The Commission recommends a plant-rich diet with large shares of protein coming from minimally
processed plant foods, such as legumes (Sec on Healthy Diets). We also ﬁnd that globally, and in most
regions, consump on of red meat and other meats is above healthy and sustainable levels. As
consumer studies have revealed several barriers to replacing animal protein sources with tradi onal
plant protein sources, including convenience, taste, and cultural values, P92 a range of meat alterna ves
are emerging across several regions, par cularly in HICs. They include plant-based alterna ves (PBAs),
91

fermenta on-derived products (using microorganisms to produce protein-rich foods), and cul vated
meat from animal cells designed to replicate the sensory proper es of animal-source foods.P92 Previous
systema c reviewsP94,P95 and modelling studiesP96,P97 suggest that replacing conven onal meat with
these alterna ves could have both posi ve and nega ve implica ons on health, environment, and
jus ce. The poten al impacts are context-dependent and vary based on the speciﬁc ingredients,
processing levels, and what they replace within the diet.
Environmental Implica ons
It is well established that whole high-protein plant foods and tradi onal products prepared from these
foods (tofu, tempeh) have lower environmental impacts than animal foods. Meta-analyses and
modelling studies indicate that PBAs can also have substan ally lower environmental impacts
compared to meat, par cularly regarding greenhouse gas (GHG) emissions and land use, but greater
impacts than minimally processed plant foods.P94,P95,P97 Cul vated meat and fermenta on-derived
products are an cipated to have higher GHG emissions than PBAs and minimally processed plant foods,
and lower to similar GHG emissions, land and water use compared to conven onal meat, with its
impact reduc on poten al being largely dependent on the transi on to renewable energy
sources.P93,P98
Health Implica ons
Replacing red meat with legumes and other plant protein sources can provide several health beneﬁts.
The nutri onal performance of emerging meat alterna ves varies widely based on each product´s
speciﬁc formula on, levels of processing, nutri onal composi on and density, and what they are
replacing in the diet. PBAs that contain substan al amounts of legumes, vegetables, and nuts are found
to have favourable nutri onal proﬁles,P95 contribu ng to higher intake of ﬁbre and folate, and lower
saturated fat intake.P94,P97 However, some PBAs are highly processed, high in added sugar, salt, and
saturated fat. Further research is required on the bioavailability of vitamins and minerals as well as the
protein quality of PBAs and fermenta on-based alterna ves for a be er understanding of the
nutri onal eﬀects of integra ng them into diets. Cul vated meat is expected to have a similar
nutri onal proﬁle as conven onal meat and might therefore be similarly associated with colorectal
cancer and other diet-related diseases.P97 The recommended consump on levels of processed meat
alterna ves remain similar to those of conven onal meat in Table 1.
Jus ce Implica ons
There is less understanding of the role of emerging meat alterna ves on socio-economic consequences
including food and nutri on security. Price is a key driver of purchase and consump on. While
unprocessed legumes are least expensive, PBAs are currently o en more expensive than the products
they aim to replace.P97 Cul vated meats are currently not widely available, but despite high
uncertain es, the exis ng peer-reviewed assessments not related to industry interests suggest it will
not be a cost-compe ve product even with substan al investment and improved produc on
methods.P99–101 The alterna ve meat industry could have addi onal implica ons for the labour market,
e.g., by genera ng and shi ing employment from rural areas to ci es.P93,P102 As many large
transna onal food corpora ons enter the emerging market for meat alterna ves, there are concerns
regarding further market concentra on/consolida on in the form of protein companies.P102,P103
Emerging alterna ves may mi gate animal welfare concerns, although cul vated meat s ll involves
some animal use through ssue biopsy removal.P104

All solu ons iden ﬁed earlier (Figure 13) contribute to a speciﬁc goal (health, sustainability, or jus ce),
with poten al posi ve or nega ve addi onal contribu ons to other goals. For example, ﬁscal policies
aimed at promo ng healthy diets (Ac on 1) can yield co-beneﬁts for both public health and
environmental sustainability. However, such policies may generate trade-oﬀs with jus ce unless
accompanied by public investments in social protec on that increase purchasing power for lowincome households (Ac on 3, 15 and 22-23). While these interven ons may independently contribute
to shi s in produc on management and consump on pa erns toward more sustainable and equitable
outcomes, their transforma ve poten al is fully realized only when implemented as contextually
appropriate bundles of mutually reinforcing ac ons.
Bundling is the implementa on of mul ple ac ons simultaneously or in an inten onally staggered
manner. We refer to bundles as ‘a coherent set of interven ons designed explicitly to achieve a single
or mul ple objec ves of interest’.9 An example of an ac on bundle is the UK ini a ve ‘A blue print to
halve obesity’ in ﬁve years, which comprises more than 30 inten onally integrated policies spanning
diﬀerent domains.462 Bundling has been proposed as a necessary condi on for food systems
transforma on.27,461
Iden fying and bundling ac ons is an inherently social process that requires coopera on and
coordina on beyond the implementa on of singular policy or technical measures.461 It extends beyond
policy coherence by addressing both short- and long-term objec ves, demanding a en on to the
governance of transi on.463 Resistance to change, o en mobilized by actors adversely aﬀected by
speciﬁc measures, can constrain implementa on.461 Systema c bundling oﬀers a means to mi gate
trade-oﬀs between poli cal feasibility and policy eﬀec veness.27,464 It can also increase the
acceptability of measures by, for example, combining ac ons that increase costs for consumers (e.g.,
taxes on meat), with those that oﬀer discounts on low-emission food alterna ves.464 Integra ng social
protec on mechanisms or retraining opportuni es with priori zed interven ons can help mi gate
resistance by addressing short-term vulnerabili es associated with structural transi ons.461 For
instance, combining ac ons to shi dietary pa erns (Solu on 1) and improve sustainable and
ecological intensiﬁca on of healthy food groups (Solu on 3) with the provision of training in skills to
support redeployment (Ac on 16) can support the broader goal of securing decent work (Solu on 6).
Well-sequenced and gradually enacted policies can generate posi ve pping points,465 whereby early
successful interven ons build public trust and facilitate broader acceptance of subsequent reforms. In
contexts where interven ons are met with scep cism or bias, combining interven ons with robust
evalua ons can foster support over me, allowing ci zens to engage with the interven ons directly
and observe its eﬀects.466 Careful policy sequencing is therefore cri cal: poorly med or misaligned
reforms can erode conﬁdence in government abili es, undermining legi macy for future ini a ves.

For example, changes in agricultural management require me to build the necessary skills and

Target-se ng has emerged as a strategy to align diverse actors around shared ambi ons and
responsibili es, improve policy integra on, and help overcome barriers to transforma on.463 Global

Governments can enhance their ability to eﬀec vely bundle, sequence, and priori se interven ons
through ins tu onal mechanisms that foster cross-sectoral collabora on—such as inter-ministerial
coordina on—and by partnering more closely with research organisa ons. A compelling example of
this type of partnership is Brazil’s Fome Zero (Zero Hunger) Program470, which integrated eﬀorts across
Ministries of Health, Educa on, and Social Development, in collabora on with research organisa ons.
The program strategically bundled policies including cash transfers for poor households, support for
small-scale farmers, and nutri on educa on to address food insecurity and promote sustainable
agriculture.470,471

Priori zing diﬀerent ac ons in diﬀerent regions
Bundles of ac ons for food system transforma on will vary signiﬁcantly across contexts. For example,
consump on side ac ons to achieve the PHD (Solu on 1) need to be a high priority in HICs and upper
MICs, as per capita consump on contributes dispropor onately to transgressing planetary boundaries.
The per capita externality costs to human health and ecosystems are nearly four mes higher in North
America and Oceania than in Sub-Saharan Africa,472 despite the rela ve burden of these costs (as a
share of GDP) being greater in LICs. HICs8 also bear a greater responsibility for reducing energy-related
emissions and suppor ng food systems improvements in LMICs,473 eﬀorts that are essen al to
achieving the mi ga on pathway modelled in this Commission (ELM). Accordingly, the societal
beneﬁts of dietary shi s towards the PHD would be especially high in HICs and upper MICs.8
In many LICs and MICs, priori es should instead include maintaining exis ng healthy dietary pa erns
(especially among youth), addressing hunger and undernutri on, and preven ng expansion of
unhealthy diets (Solu on 2).
MICs, in par cular, have the greatest overall poten al to reduce food systems-related climate emissions
on the produc on side by shi ing toward more sustainable agricultural prac ces (e.g. Solu on 3) and
adop ng cleaner, more circular processing systems (incl. Solu on 5).473
Others474 work that includes stakeholder consulta ons on regional food systems priori es have
highlighted the following priori za on focal areas: increasing food availability in East and Southern
Africa; promo ng income growth in West and Central Africa; improving the nutri onal quality of diets
in the Paciﬁc; and protec ng against deforesta on in La n America.

The iden ﬁca on of PHD reference values and ranges, food system boundaries, and social founda ons
represents an ini al step toward establishing science-based targets for food systems. While global
targets can foster a shared vision and ambi on, their transla on into concrete ac ons across scales
and sectors is essen al for achieving meaningful impact.475 Such target se ng should be grounded in
independent scien ﬁc assessments, be a ainable and ac onable, and supported by a transparent
analy cal ra onale. Their achievement must be measurable.476 Science-based targets are most
eﬀec ve when developed and implemented through inclusive dialogues with diverse stakeholders.477
There is a cri cal need for methodological advancements to enable disaggrega on of targets (and their

Recently, there has been a growing uptake of voluntary science-based target commitments by

‘Voluntary Guidelines on Food Systems and Nutri on’, established by the Commi ee on World Food

However, research on voluntary science-based target se ng remains nascent. Emerging evidence
suggests that without eﬀec ve implementa on, monitoring and evalua on, such commitments may
risk hindering transforma ve change—e.g., by legi mising powerful corporate actors and insula ng
them from democra c oversight.479 To accelerate progress and ensure measurable improvements,
transi oning science-based targets from voluntary frameworks to regulatory mechanisms is
essen al.478

Ensure monitoring and evalua on of targets and ac ons

community have emphasized the need for monitoring frameworks that move beyond tracking isolated

tradi onally been treated in silos.482,483 Such approaches enhance compliance and accountability.482
One example is the Food System Countdown to 2030 Ini a ve29,460 which promotes greater
accountability and transparency through comprehensive global monitoring of food systems across ﬁve
domains: diets, nutri on, and health; environment, natural resources, and produc on; livelihoods,
poverty, and equity; governance; and resilience.29,484 Robust monitoring and evalua on (M&E) systems
are also important for suppor ng learnings about what works, par cularly given the limited contextspeciﬁc evidence on the eﬀec veness of food system interven ons, par cularly when bundled.27 M&E

The current evidence gaps, combined with the urgent need for ac on, underscore the need to improve

Stronger alignment between global and sub-global assessments—such as those from the Lancet

roadmaps that connect food system transforma on with climate, biodiversity, health, and economic
objec ves.

Coali ons can bring a variety actors with divergent interests and perspec ves to the table to nego ate
more inclusive, viable, and realis c pathways.487 Food policies will have diﬀerent winners and losers,
and tensions will need to be managed.9 For instance, fear of job losses in certain sectors can paralyse
reforms towards sustainability. In such cases, coali ons could nego ate ways to compensate those who
stand to lose or invest in new opportuni es that would beneﬁt them.9
Diﬀerent food system actors have varying capaci es to enact change. Interven ons that could enable
system shi s have some mes stalled, o en due to delays and bo lenecks in regulatory processes.4
Communi es, Interna onal NGOs, and other stakeholders can deploy diﬀerent capabili es to support
transforma on in diﬀerent ways. By forming stronger alliances and coali ons, these actors can
implement more coordinated and mely interven ons for transforma ve change.492 Realizing food
systems transforma on will require novel forms of partnership among inter-governmental and global
ins tu ons, na onal governments, sub-na onal public authori es (e.g., ci es, states, and
municipali es), and inter-ministerial commi ees can align policies across sectors. Engagement from
the private sector, civil society, and social movements is equally cri cal.473 Diﬀerent actors bring dis nct
capaci es and mandates, each playing a unique role in shaping systemic change.458 Transforma on
cannot occur without forging coali ons that span boundaries, reconcile divergent interests, and build
broad-based legi macy for new policy pathways.490,493 Ini a ves such as the UN Zero Hunger coali on,

Coali ons aiming to advance food systems transforma on involve actors and stakeholders with
diﬀerent interests and tac cs to preserve such interests, some mes designed to maintain the status
quo.494,495 These compe ng interests shape the incen ves and constraints surrounding transforma ve
eﬀorts.461 For coali ons to func on eﬀec vely, three key barriers must be addressed: (1) lack of poli cal
leadership; (2) corporate interests; and (3) weak and fragmented demand for ac on.

Lack of poli cal leadership and appropriate governance framework
Poli cal leadership has been notably absent in driving transforma ve food policy agendas and
commi ng resources to long-term structural change to food systems.423 Short electoral cycles o en
discourage support for policies whose beneﬁts, such as improvements in popula on health, are
realized beyond a single poli cal term. This hesitancy is compounded by substan al upfront
investments required for transforma on, and the perceived risks these pose to, for example,
employment and food prices.9,496 Moreover, government subsidies o en favour large corporate actors
over broader public welfare and public goods.497
Eﬀec ve transforma on requires coordinated, intersectoral policy ac ons that extend beyond food
systems themselves. Yet current governance arrangements o en fall short to systema cally address
and integrate agriculture and the environment, health and nutri on, infrastructure, energy, growth,
and equity.484

Corporate interests used against public interests
The second major barrier is the exercise of corporate power in ways that undermine public interests.
The high degree of corporate concentra on across food systems remains an intractable governance
issue.333,498 This is partly due to the out-sized inﬂuence of large transna onal food and beverage
companies who have considerable power and resources at their disposal to block ini a ves.17
Concentrated market structures, with a few dominant ﬁrms, are common in food systems from
produc on (agricultural inputs), trade (commodity trade), and consump on (food processing and
retail),14,499 that impact people’s access to food e.g. through manipula ve pricing, as well as adver sing
and packaging that shapes consumers’ preferences.500 Corporate actors employ tac cs such as direct
lobbying of governmental oﬃcials to undermine poli cal priori es, including dietary guidelines and
regulatory interven ons.501 Another avenue of inﬂuence by corporate interests is in the sponsorship of
scien ﬁc studies that aligns with commercial interests and the dissemina on of misinforma on aimed
at discredi ng independent scien ﬁc evidence—such as in cases involving meat industry-sponsored
scien sts.502,503 Furthermore, public-private partnerships are increasingly co-opted by corporate actors
to shape discourses, par cularly in the area of sustainability, in ways that reinforce their legi macy
and market power.504,505

A perceived lack of eﬀec ve public demand and acceptance for transforma ve change is frequently
cited by policymakers and private sector actors as jus ﬁca on for depriori sing ambi ous reforms.
Ci zens may resist policies that ul mately serve the public due to the diﬃculty of weighing short-term
costs, par cularly in terms of employment in aﬀected sectors, against long-term beneﬁts.466,486
While grassroot actors play a cri cal role in building public engagement and poli cal momentum for
more equitable and sustainable food systems,506 their eﬀorts are o en fragmented and constrained by
entrenched power dynamics and the structural inequi es of food systems.499,507 In some repressive
poli cal contexts, civil society and grassroot organisa ons are deliberately excluded or marginalized
by authoritarian regimes, further silencing calls for change. In other contexts, grassroot mobilisa on
can itself become a source of polarisa on with some backing policies that might not promote public
good over the long term.
Fragmenta on in public demand can also stem from a lack of consensus on priori es and insuﬃcient
space for dialogue—par cularly facilitated, inclusive processes that can clarify areas of disagreement
and forge common ground.477 These challenges are especially pronounced with global commitment
mechanisms—such as the Paris Agreement or the Kunming-Montreal Global Biodiversity Framework—
into local or na onal ac ons.21 Misinforma on further compounds public disengagement, especially
when systemic challenges like public health are framed as ma ers of individual responsibility. This
framing distorts public understanding of the structural determinants and diminishes support for
necessary reforms.508 The absence of transparent monitoring systems exacerbates the problem by
undermining public trust in transforma onal policies and fostering uncertainty or misconcep ons
about their poten al for delivering broad-based beneﬁts.509

It has been suggested that ﬁnancial resources, averaging US$500 billion each year between now and

to more than 5 trillion a year.9 Importantly, not all ac ons require large amounts of ﬁnancial resources
to be successful. Some can be implemented at rela vely low cost or can even be cost neutral. However,
they require willingness to repriori ze current resource spending. Here, we ﬁrst look at the poten al
to unlock resources within the food system, primarily through bundling of diﬀerent ac vi es that
already happens in the food system. We then explore how to unlock resources by bundling with
broader policy agendas.

Repurposing subsidies, and other food systems investments
Subsidies to the agriculture sector represent a signiﬁcant resource es mated at roughly US$851 billion
in the 2020-2022 period (for the 54 countries that report to the Organiza on for Economic Coopera on
and Development). The Food Systems Economic Commission has es mated that the cost of food
systems transforma on is between US$200-500 billion per year.9 Without changing the degree of
ﬁnancing provided to food produc on, se ng clear condi ons for the use of these currently earmarked
public funds in favour of public goods such as public health, and environmental security would be a
major ﬁrst step towards transforma on.417 At least a third of agricultural subsidies have no public
beneﬁt and could be repurposed.510 In the EU, 82% of the European Union’s agricultural subsidies
favour animal-based agriculture.510 Farm sector subsidies could be used to incen vize or invest in

Shi s in agricultural and other subsidies, or value-added tax (VAT) can reduce health costs and increase
health beneﬁts, while lowering climate impacts.380 Health directed taxa on of meat products could
yield a 9% decrease in premature deaths associated with red and processed meat consump on, with
an es mated 14% decrease in a ributable health costs globally, notably in high and middle-income
countries.380
Measures that seek to distribute income more equitably (via e.g. social protec on measures or
progressive tax policies), combined with a serious public and private sector commitment to reducing
food loss and waste along all food value chains, can together improve the rela ve aﬀordability of
healthy dietary pa erns. When these and other measures were combined in a previous modelling
eﬀort, the cost of Planetary Health Diet aligned diets fell by 25-29% in low-income to lower middleincome countries.8 However, the substan al restructuring of any sector, including the ‘repurposing’ of
any kind of subsidy should be handled with care, since the poten al unintended eﬀects of shi ing
resources from one targeted purpose to another can be as signiﬁcant as the intended eﬀects.512

Alignment of investments with the climate-, nature/biodiversity-, and economic agendas
Another way to unlock resources for food systems transforma on is to align approaches and ﬁnancial
priori es with other cri cally and mutually interdependent policy and investment agendas. A share of
climate or biodiversity ﬁnance funding could be turned towards food systems transforma on. For
example, at the Conference of Par es (COP) climate summit in Egypt in 2022 (COP27), the COP
Presidency and World Health Organiza on launched the Ini a ve on Climate Ac on and Nutri on (ICAN), to be er integrate the global delivery of climate change adapta on and mi ga on goals and the
policy ac on necessary to meet nutri on and sustainable food systems goals.513 This alignment requires
future summit agendas, focused on climate, fossil fuels, renewable energy, employment growth, food
systems, and more, to work towards integra on of targets and investment modali es. However, many
ini a ves currently lack coherence and alignment with agriculture and other policies at diﬀerent levels.
This is the case, for instance, of the many ini a ves on tree plan ng which, if showing considerable
beneﬁts for food security, nutri on and the environment, remain largely uncoordinated (Vansant
2024.514,515 By ramping up these eﬀorts, governments can work more strategically to integrate and
coordinate these mul ple agendas to repurpose ﬁscal incen ves away from economic “bads” (e.g.
carbon emissions, biodiversity destruc on, consump on of excessive sugar, salt or trans-fats, etc.)
towards public goods.516
In 2022, subsidies to the energy sector (currently driven by fossil fuels) were es mated to be around
US$7 trillion.517 Ending subsidy support to pollu ng energy produc on would allow for a repurposing
of resources to alterna ve public sector investments.518 The la er would include regenera ve energy
to support diet quality through, for example, solar-powered drying and chilling of perishable foods, and
other innova ons in cold chain technologies that signiﬁcantly reduce nutrient-rich food loss and waste.
However, project-level climate ﬁnancing for food systems stands at only 4.3%, or US$28 billion, of global

The Commission calls for an urgent, comprehensive approach to food systems transforma on, centred
on the development of context-speciﬁc roadmaps that lay out viable evidence-based solu on sets.
Such roadmaps should focus on bundling ac ons, se ng science-based targets, building inclusive
coali ons, establishing and building on already exis ng monitoring and accountability mechanisms,
and mobilising ﬁnancial resources at scale. At the core of the Commission's framework are three
founda onal elements: (1) recommended food group intake values for the Planetary Health Diet to
advance human health; (2) food system planetary boundaries to ensure achieve environmental
sustainability; and (3) social founda ons to guide the crea on of just food systems. These values,
boundaries, and founda ons can provide the guide for target-se ng and collec ve ac on, adaptable
across geographies and sectors.

Diﬀerent ways to unlock ﬁnance for food systems transforma on could work if banks, investors, and
large businesses adopt and report against the Taskforce on Climate Related Financial Disclosures
(TCFD), help develop the Taskforce on Nature-related Financial Disclosure (TNFD), and set ScienceBased Targets (SBTs), targe ng Paris-aligned/net zero por olios.519 Through these agendas, ﬁnancial
ins tu ons can commit to a variety of por olios to shi towards more sustainable food systems, such
as deforesta on-free por olios (e.g., deforesta on-free pension funds). Public spending should also
create mechanisms for private investment in food systems transforma on. Central banks can for
example introduce stress tes ng for ﬁnancial ins tu ons based on their viability in a future economy
that fully accounts for nega ve externali es.
The cost of food systems transforma on for lower-income groups and countries are beyond their
current ﬁnancing capacity. Li ing their ﬁnancing constraints is cri cal to unlock the global beneﬁts of
transforma on.473 Countries with limited ﬁscal capacity need interna onal support to invest in food
systems sustainability.517
Wealthy countries and other donors should increase Overseas Development Assistance (ODA) from
current low levels (rela ve to their actual and projected na onal wealth), as well as climate ﬁnance for
food systems adapta on through mul lateral development ﬁnance ins tu ons. Wealthy governments
can introduce targeted debt relief to facilitate the ﬂow of capital to food systems transforma ons,
including debt for food swaps. This could be supported by innova ve ﬁnancial instruments, such as
guarantees for a new type of perpetual/long-dated social and environmental bond, with capped
adjustable rates.

The Commission has iden ﬁed eight solu ons and 23 ac ons to enable food systems transforma on.
These can be organized into coherent ‘bundles’ of interven ons that simultaneously advance health,
environmental and jus ce goals. Bundling enhances poli cal feasibility and policy eﬀec veness,
par cularly when designed to priori se the needs of marginalised and disadvantaged popula ons. The
most suitable and eﬀec ve bundles will vary by context and should be tailored to the speciﬁc challenges
and opportuni es of each region and sector.

Cross-sectoral coali ons—including actors from public ins tu ons, private sector, and civil society—
are essen al to achieve synergis c governance. These coali ons must align with exis ng and emerging
global frameworks, such as the Paris Agreement, the Conven on on Biological Diversity, and the post2030 SDG agenda. Ensuring accountability within such coali ons is cri cal. Mechanisms must be
established to insulate policymaking from undue corporate inﬂuence, while civil society and social
movements play a vital role in promo ng transparency and oversight.
The Commission also underscores the need for ac ons beyond the food system. Its modelling indicates
that the PHD can only be achieved within GHG and land-use planetary boundaries if mi ga on eﬀorts
in other sectors are also scaled up. Similarly, shi ing diets among low-income and vulnerable groups
requires coupling dietary interven ons with broader social protec on measures. Aﬀordability must be
achieved not only by lowering food prices but also by addressing structural poverty and inequi es.
Financing for food systems transforma on must draw from broader funding streams—such as those
targe ng climate mi ga on and biodiversity conserva on and protec on—rather than relying solely
on current food systems ﬁnancing.
Finally, the Commission posi ons jus ce as both a central goal and a driving force for food systems
transforma on. Food systems cannot be just without ensuring the PHD is aﬀordable and accessible to
all, and without signiﬁcantly reducing transgressions of planetary boundaries. At the same me, jus ce
is necessary to overcome the deeply entrenched structural barriers that currently impede
transforma ve change. In this sense, jus ce is not only an outcome of food systems transforma on,
but a prerequisite for enabling it.
Panel 11: School meals
School meals are increasingly recognised as a cost-eﬃcient investment for governments to advance
mul ple policy objec ves, including health and nutri on, educa on, social protec on, and
agriculture.P105 Well-designed and strategically implemented school meal programmes throughout the
next 7,000 days – the period of development between the ﬁrst 1,000 days of life and adulthood – can
make a las ng impact, par cularly on the most vulnerable children and adolescents, by improving
a endance and academic performance, and reducing dropout rates.P106
Moreover, school meals programmes make up one of the largest outputs of the global food system,
feeding 420 million children daily. Sustainable school meals coupled with consistent and ac onoriented food educa on can empower future genera ons by fostering healthier and more sustainable
food habits at a cri cal age when life-long dietary preferences and social a tudes are formed and
carried into adulthood.
In 2021, in recogni on of the unique poten al of school meals for improving child health, wellbeing,
and educa on following the COVID-19 pandemic, na onal governments formed the global School
Meals Coali on (SMC), declaring their commitment to scaling up and strengthening their na onal
school meals programmes to ensure every child has access to a nutri ous meal at school by 2030. To
support the (now) 105 SMC member states to achieve this ambi ous mission, the independent
Research Consor um for School Health and Nutri on, with the input of 160 global experts from more

103

than 80 organisa ons, iden ﬁed evidence-based policy approaches to support the delivery of diverse,
balanced, and sustainably produced school meals.
Since these programmes are overwhelmingly domes cally ﬁnanced and managed, the policy levers lie
in the hands of na onal governments, allowing for signiﬁcant change to be made at pace. Incorpora ng
sustainable school meal policies can lower environmental impact, improve climate resilience and
leverage food system transforma on in both high- and low-income se ngs. Key policy strategies to
support posi ve change in this area include providing nutrient-rich menus, that are predominantly
plant-based; sourcing produc on from local farms using sustainable and ecological intensiﬁca on
prac ces that support agrobiodiversity and climate resilience, as well as structured demand and stable
markets to smallholder farmers and processors; shi ing to eﬃcient cooking solu ons to reduce
nega ve health and environmental impacts; reducing food loss and waste; and ensuring ac onoriented food and climate educa on to enable future genera ons to make sustainable choices. With
increased awareness, children also serve as powerful agents of change among their families and
broader communi es.P107
Countries and regional bodies are already demonstra ng that these ac ons are possible. Europe, for
example, now requires a minimum share of organic foods in school menus, P108 and approximately 40%
of na onal school meals programmes currently have agriculture policy objec ves such as
agrobiodiversity, food sovereignty, and climate-smart foods.P109 The na onal school meals programme
in Brazil legislates that at least 30% of the food used in the prepara on of school meals is locally
sourced from family farms.P110 Like Brazil, many countries in Africa and Asia have implemented a food
systems approach to school meals, o en involving public-private partnerships.
While ﬁnancing is o en the barrier to scale-up, food-related costs of providing healthy and sustainable
school meals to every child could amount to up to 1% of GDP in low-income countries where school
meal coverage is low.P111 When weighing the long-term costs of inac on—such as the treatment of
diet-related health issues and the impact of greenhouse gas emissions—the case for inves ng in school
meals becomes undeniably compelling. The study iden ﬁed savings of US$ 120-200 billion in trea ng
diet-related illnesses (including US$ 7-13 billion in low-income countries), and a reduc on in climate
change costs of US$ 18-70 billion (including US$ 1-5 billion in low-income countries). The savings were
greatest, in each case, for meal composi ons in line with recommenda ons for healthy and sustainable
dietary pa erns.
In summary, sustainable school meals policies can posi vely inﬂuence dietary preferences of the next
genera on, and promote the adop on of sustainable produc on prac ces, broaden the local food
basket and s mulate crop diversity, along with other social and economic development outcomes. The
inclusion of school meals on the climate ﬁnance agenda, as well as in Na onally Determined
Contribu ons for both health and the environment are cri cal steps to more resilient, sustainable, and
equitable food systems.

Panel 12: 40 Ci es Ini a ve to achieve the Planetary Health Diet for All by 2030
Launched in October 2019 by C40, a global network of almost 100 mayors of leading ci es commi ed
to addressing climate change, 16 ci es signed up to an ambi ous ini a ve aiming to achieve the
Planetary Health Diet for all by 2030 in ways that are “reﬂec ve of the culture, geography, and
demography of their residents”. Seven of the ci es are in Europe, ﬁve in North America, one in South
America, two in East Asia and one in South-East Asia.
The ci es signing up pledged to implement four important measures by 2030:
 Aligning their food procurement to the Planetary Health Diet.
 Suppor ng an overall increase of healthy plant-based food consump on by shi ing away from
unsustainable, unhealthy diets.
 Reducing food loss and waste by 50% from a 2015 baseline.
 Working with residents, businesses, public ins tu ons and other organiza ons to develop a joint
strategy for implemen ng these measures and achieving these goals inclusively and equitably and
incorpora ng this strategy into Climate Ac on Plans, including incorpora on into na onally
determined contribu ons to climate goals.
Interim evalua on
In September 2023, 15 of the 16 par cipa ng ci es submi ed interim reports on their progress.
Suﬃcient data were provided by seven ci es to quan fy progress towards the ﬁrst two measures.
Between them, these seven ci es serve over 400 million meals a year for diﬀerent groups and
establishments including schools and hospitals. Measured against diﬀerent baseline years from
between 2015 and 2022, ci es reported an increase in their purchasing of plant-sourced protein of
44% (from a mean of 6,356 to 8,667 tonnes) and a decline in their meat purchasing of 19% (from a
mean of 16,648 to 12,765 tonnes). In recogni on of its signiﬁcant poten al in shi ing millions of meals
in major ci es to be healthier and more sustainable, this ini a ve won the pres gious US$2-million
Food Planet Prize awarded by The Curt Bergfors Founda on, in June 2024.
Many examples of progress towards all four measures were described by ci es in the interim
evalua on in 2023 including engaging with ci zens, restaurants, store owners and town planners to
change food environments so healthier and sustainable op ons are readily available and oﬀering
training for businesses on how to reduce food waste.
But while excellent examples abound, the extent of progress for each of the four measures across all
16 ci es remains unknown at the me of wri ng because monitoring systems using similar robust
metrics across all ci es have yet to be put in place.
Barriers to progress reported by ci es
Two barriers to progress were commonly reported by par cipa ng ci es:
Misaligned na onal procurement standards: Procurement standards don’t include
considera on of the environmental impact of foods purchased. To achieve the PHD for all,
ci es are having to make the case for, and then implement this change throughout the en re
public food supply chain on their own.

105

Insuﬃcient resources for evalua on: Budgetary restric ons, coupled with increasing food
prices are curtailing ci es’ capacity to gather data on their progress towards each of the four
aims of this ini a ve.
Maximising the impact of the C40 Good Food Ci es Ini a ve
Providing addi onal resources to C40 Good Food Ci es at this juncture is vital to maximise its impact
and value as a global exemplar of transi oning food systems, by achieving the following:
 Data collec on: se ng up and running monitoring mechanisms to assess progress and adjust
if oﬀ-track for achieving its 2030 goal.
 Coali ons: se ng up mul -level pla orms to engage diﬀerent authori es and ci zens to
create a shared vision.
 Advocacy: support for campaigns and other forms of advocacy to inspire na onal governments
and interna onal partners to increase ambi on and ac on at the scale needed to achieve PHD
for all.
Lessons from C40 all ini a ves needed to achieve the Planetary Health Diet for all
Mayors from 16 ci es around the world are leading the way in transforming the food in their ci es.
Similar ambi ous ini a ves are now needed in every city, town and village on our planet to achieve
the Planetary Health Diet for all.
But these pioneering ci es highlight that realizing these ambi ons will require each ini a ve to be
matched - at its incep on - by suﬃcient resources for two core ac vi es:
 implemen ng a programme of interven ons built on exis ng evidence;
 Robust evalua on, built in from incep on as part of the implementa on plan, so that progress is
monitored in real me. This will allow interven ons showing promise to be op mized and those
that are ineﬀec ve to be stopped, and others started.
